The chapter also notes that senior officials in the State Department, military, and intelligence establishment initially warned that an overtly pro-Zionist policy could damage American strategic and oil interests in the Arab world. Truman nevertheless supported the Zionist objective. The episode shows that foreign policy emerged from the interaction of domestic politics, presidential judgment, bureaucratic advice, and evolving strategic calculations rather than from a single unified American position.
Study instruction:
- "Map the competing influences on Truman: domestic constituencies, advisers, strategic interests, oil, and the emerging Cold War."
Key point:
- US policy was contested internally, even as presidential support proved consequential.
- Domestic political incentives can shape foreign policy alongside strategic assessments.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Memorandum to President Truman, 2 October 1945
10. Q: How did the formation of the Arab League in 1945 affect Palestinian representation?
A: Show answer
The Arab League offered a regional forum for Arab cooperation, but the chapter describes Palestinian disappointment that its founding arrangements did not give Palestinians independent control over their representation. The inaugural communiqué removed references to Palestine, and Arab governments retained influence over the choice of the Palestinian representative. Musa al-‘Alami’s participation in the founding conference was initially blocked by Egypt’s prime minister and then permitted after intervention by a British intelligence officer. Khalidi uses this episode to show that nominally independent Arab governments remained subject to British influence and pursued their own regional priorities. The League’s existence therefore did not automatically produce a unified or autonomous Palestinian diplomatic voice.
Study instruction:
- "Trace who controlled the invitation, the representative, and the agenda at the League’s founding conference."
Key point:
- Regional solidarity did not guarantee independent Palestinian representation.
- Formal institutions can reproduce power imbalances when representation is controlled by member governments.
Source links:- Yale Avalon Project: Alexandria Protocol, 7 October 1944
11. Q: What was the Anglo-American Committee of Inquiry, and what issue was it asked to address?
A: Show answer
The Anglo-American Committee of Inquiry was established by the British and US governments in 1946 to examine the situation of Jewish survivors in Europe and the political, economic, and social conditions in Palestine. A central issue was whether 100,000 displaced Jewish survivors should be admitted to Palestine. Khalidi emphasizes that neither Britain nor the United States was willing to accept the refugees on the scale proposed for Palestine, making the debate about the destination of displaced people inseparable from the future constitutional status of the country. The committee heard Arab and Jewish representatives and produced recommendations that reflected the changing balance between Britain and the United States. Its work became an important stage in transferring decision-making toward international forums.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the committee’s sponsors, terms of reference, principal humanitarian issue, and the political consequences of its recommendations."
Key point:
- The committee linked the post-Holocaust refugee crisis to Palestine’s political future.
- Its recommendations exposed the growing influence of US policy in a matter Britain had previously dominated.
Source links:- PalQuest: Report of the Anglo-American Committee of Inquiry
12. Q: What was Albert Hourani’s contribution to the Anglo-American Committee of Inquiry?
A: Show answer
Albert Hourani presented the Palestinian Arab case through a detailed statement prepared with colleagues in the Arab Office. As Khalidi recounts it, Hourani warned that implementing a Jewish-state project in a country with an Arab majority would create grave injustice, repression, disorder, and wider regional instability. His testimony also raised the possibility that responsible Zionist figures contemplated transferring some or all of the Arab population elsewhere. Khalidi regards the presentation as prescient because later regional upheavals and the displacement of Palestinians demonstrated the seriousness of the issues Hourani identified. The chapter also notes that the committee largely ignored the Arab case and recommended the admission of 100,000 Jewish refugees, reflecting the new international balance.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish Hourani’s documented testimony from Khalidi’s retrospective judgment that it was prescient."
Key point:
- Hourani supplied a substantive Palestinian diplomatic case, not merely a protest.
- A warning can be analytically important even when decision-makers do not act on it.
Source links:- Journal of Palestine Studies: Albert Hourani’s 1946 Testimony
13. Q: Why does Khalidi describe the absence of Palestinian state institutions as a fatal weakness?
A: Show answer
By 1947, Palestinians lacked the central institutions that could coordinate diplomacy, finance, administration, and military action. Unlike the Jewish Agency, which had acquired significant governing functions under the Mandate, Palestinians had no foreign ministry, recognized diplomatic service, unified military command, or sufficiently developed treasury. Palestinian representatives were often treated by foreign officials as private visitors rather than as official envoys. Khalidi argues that this institutional gap weakened Palestinians at every level: they struggled to present a coordinated case internationally, raise resources, organize defense, and implement decisions across the country. The point is not that institutions alone guarantee success, but that a national movement confronting a highly organized opponent was disadvantaged without them.
Study instruction:
- "List the functions of a modern state and identify which Palestinian bodies attempted to perform each function before 1948."
Key point:
- Institutional capacity converts political aspirations into coordinated diplomatic, fiscal, and operational action.
- The absence of recognized institutions magnified other political and military weaknesses.
14. Q: What was the Arab National Fund, and how did its resources compare with the Jewish National Fund?
A: Show answer
The Arab National Fund was created by the Arab Higher Committee in 1944 as a proposed treasury to collect resources for Palestinian national purposes. Yusif Sayigh became its first director-general and tried to build a nationwide collection network under difficult security and administrative conditions. By mid-1947 it had raised 176,000 Palestine pounds, a substantial contribution relative to the population’s means. Khalidi contrasts this with the much larger and longer-established Jewish National Fund, which had a broad international fundraising network and received a reported million-pound gift shortly after Sayigh’s announcement. The comparison illustrates unequal fundraising infrastructure and international reach. It should not be reduced to a simple judgment about the generosity of ordinary people.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two funds by age, donor geography, collection systems, organizational capacity, and the scale of their resources."
Key point:
- Financial capacity depends on networks and institutions as well as local willingness to contribute.
- The chapter uses the funds to demonstrate a wider asymmetry in national organization.
15. Q: What organizational weaknesses did Yusif Sayigh identify in the Arab Higher Committee?
A: Show answer
Sayigh described the Arab Higher Committee as lacking a functioning secretariat, administrative routines, records, typing support, and the professional skills needed to prepare a major diplomatic case. The chapter recounts his observation that the committee’s office could be effectively closed when a leading member left because there was no independent bureaucratic structure. The committee’s leaders had also been weakened by British imprisonment and exile during the Great Revolt, and they returned to a political environment marked by factional rivalry. Khalidi argues that this organizational weakness limited the committee’s ability to coordinate research, fundraising, communication, and international advocacy at a critical moment.
Study instruction:
- "Translate Sayigh’s observations into an institutional diagnostic covering staffing, records, decision rights, continuity, and professional expertise."
Key point:
- Leadership without administrative infrastructure cannot reliably sustain complex national work.
- A functioning secretariat is essential for continuity, documentation, and coordinated action.
16. Q: What was the Arab Office, and what role was it intended to play?
A: Show answer
The Arab Office was established as an information and diplomatic body tasked with presenting the Palestinian case to the Anglo-American Committee and improving international understanding of Palestine. It was intended to become the nucleus of a Palestinian foreign service, supplying research, public information, and professional representation that the Arab Higher Committee lacked. It attracted capable figures including Musa al-‘Alami, Albert Hourani, Ahmad Shuqayri, Burhan Dajani, Wasfi al-Tal, and Walid Khalidi. Yet its dependence on support from the pro-British Iraqi government and its failure to coordinate effectively with other Palestinian leaders made it vulnerable to suspicion and regional competition. Its potential was undermined by the very divisions it was meant to overcome.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the Arab Office’s intended functions, its personnel, funding base, political dependencies, and reasons for losing legitimacy."
Key point:
- The Arab Office attempted to professionalize Palestinian external representation.
- A technically capable body can be weakened by disputed sponsorship and poor coordination.
Source links:- Journal of Palestine Studies: On Albert Hourani and the Arab Office
17. Q: How did the politics of notables shape Palestinian leadership before 1948?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a political culture dominated by prominent families and personal networks rather than mass-based parties with stable nationwide organization. The mufti and other leaders often relied on personal loyalty, close entourages, and hierarchical relationships. Such arrangements could mobilize influence but made it difficult to delegate authority, build a professional bureaucracy, or develop independent institutions that survived personal rivalries. The chapter contrasts this model with the more organized political structures that Palestinian leaders needed in the late Mandate period. The point is not that notable families lacked political commitment or expertise; it is that a leadership system centered on personal authority was poorly suited to the scale of coordination required in 1947–48.
Study instruction:
- "Compare personal-network leadership with party-based or bureaucratic organization in terms of delegation, succession, accountability, and continuity."
Key point:
- Personal authority can mobilize supporters but may constrain institution-building.
- The chapter treats political structure as a factor in national capacity, not merely a matter of individual character.
18. Q: What leadership limitations does the chapter attribute to Hajj Amin al-Husayni?
A: Show answer
Khalidi draws on Yusif Sayigh’s assessment that the mufti believed the justice of the Palestinian cause would itself attract support and that he did not build a modern fighting organization capable of independent operation. Sayigh suggested that the mufti preferred a tightly controlled entourage to a larger decentralized structure that might produce other influential leaders. Khalidi connects this style to the broader politics of notables and to the difficulty of creating professional institutions. This is an attributed assessment of leadership style, not a complete explanation of Palestinian defeat. The chapter also stresses external repression, regional rivalry, resource disparities, and the absence of state structures.
Study instruction:
- "Separate Sayigh’s assessment of the mufti’s leadership style from the broader structural causes identified elsewhere in the chapter."
Key point:
- A just cause does not automatically create the organization needed to defend it.
- Leadership style matters, but it must be evaluated alongside external constraints and institutional conditions.
19. Q: How did British influence intensify divisions within Palestinian political representation?
A: Show answer
Britain had outlawed the Arab Higher Committee during the Great Revolt, exiled or imprisoned key leaders, and continued to oppose an independent Palestinian political entity it feared would resist British interests. The chapter recounts British officials praising Musa al-‘Alami and pressing Dr. Husayn al-Khalidi to cooperate with the Arab Office. Dr. Husayn interpreted this intervention as an attempt to influence Palestinian representation and regarded al-‘Alami as too close to British interests. Khalidi argues that British influence did not operate only through formal Mandate institutions; it also worked through Arab governments, advisers, funding, and personal relationships. This made it harder for Palestinians to agree on who could legitimately speak for them.
Study instruction:
- "Map the channels of influence described in the chapter and distinguish direct colonial authority from indirect political leverage."
Key point:
- External power can shape internal representation through patronage and diplomatic pressure.
- Disputed legitimacy can paralyze institutions even when their personnel are capable.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Roosevelt to King Ibn Saud, 5 April 1945
20. Q: Why did the Arab Office’s Iraqi sponsorship create opposition among other Arab governments?
A: Show answer
The Arab Office received important support from Iraq’s government under Nuri al-Sa‘id, which was closely aligned with Britain. Egypt and Saudi Arabia, as well as Syria and Lebanon, suspected that the office advanced Iraqi regional ambitions and the proposed Fertile Crescent federation. They feared that an institution claiming to represent Palestine would become a vehicle for Baghdad’s influence. Their opposition weakened the Arab Office through the Arab League and deprived Palestinians of a potentially useful diplomatic platform. Khalidi presents this as an example of how inter-Arab rivalry repeatedly displaced the Palestinian national interest. Support from one government could therefore become a liability if neighboring states regarded it as an instrument of regional competition.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the sponsor, the suspected strategic objective, the rival governments, and the institutional consequence of the dispute."
Key point:
- Patronage can provide resources while undermining legitimacy among rival actors.
- Regional competition repeatedly fragmented the Palestinian diplomatic effort.
21. Q: What were King Abdullah’s ambitions in Palestine, and how did they conflict with Palestinian independence?
A: Show answer
King Abdullah sought to expand Transjordan westward and increase the territory and population under Hashemite rule. Khalidi describes secret contacts between Abdullah and Jewish Agency leaders as part of efforts to reach an accommodation over the territory allocated to an Arab state by partition. The king had little interest in an independent Palestinian leadership that could challenge his claim to represent or govern that territory. His ambitions conflicted with the Palestinian demand to control their own political future. The chapter emphasizes that Abdullah’s position was also shaped by British support, military dependence, and competition with other Arab rulers. His policy should therefore be understood as a regional strategy, not simply as an expression of Arab solidarity.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the relationship between Abdullah’s territorial ambition, his contacts with Zionist leaders, British support, and Palestinian objections."
Key point:
- Abdullah’s expansionist project competed directly with Palestinian self-government.
- Inter-Arab politics included both cooperation and negotiations that bypassed Palestinians.
22. Q: How did the United States and the Soviet Union change the international balance over Palestine?
A: Show answer
After the Second World War, the United States and Soviet Union became the decisive external powers in the Palestine question. The United States had developed a global military, economic, and diplomatic presence, while the Soviet Union emerged with major military strength and expanding influence in the Middle East. Khalidi argues that the Zionist movement anticipated this shift and cultivated relations with both powers, whereas Palestinian and Arab leaders were less prepared to operate in the new international environment. Both superpowers supported partition in 1947, although for different strategic reasons. Their agreement on the immediate question did not mean they had identical long-term interests or that their later policies toward Israel and Arab states would remain aligned.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two superpowers’ postwar capabilities, motives for supporting partition, and later changes in regional alignment."
Key point:
- The Palestine question became embedded in the emerging postwar international order.
- Shared support for one decision does not imply identical motives or permanent alliance.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
23. Q: Why did Britain refer the Palestine question to the United Nations in 1947?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Britain as financially weakened, increasingly unable to control armed opposition, and preoccupied with the unwinding of its wider empire. The Zionist campaign against British officials and installations, including the 1946 King David Hotel bombing, contributed to the Mandate’s growing ungovernability. The Attlee government therefore referred the problem to the United Nations. This transfer also reflected Britain’s declining ability to dictate the outcome and the rise of the United States and Soviet Union as dominant powers. Britain did not simply disappear from the process: its strategic interests and relationships with Arab governments continued to matter, but it no longer held the decisive position it had enjoyed earlier in the Mandate.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the interaction of British financial weakness, armed opposition, imperial priorities, and the emergence of the United Nations as the decision forum."
Key point:
- The 1947 referral reflected a loss of British capacity and authority.
- A change of forum did not eliminate British influence or responsibility.
Source links:- United Nations: Yearbook of the United Nations 1947–48
24. Q: What was the United Nations Special Committee on Palestine, and why did its recommendations matter?
A: Show answer
The United Nations Special Committee on Palestine, commonly called UNSCOP, was created to examine the future of the country after Britain referred the issue to the United Nations. Its majority recommended partition into Jewish and Arab states, with an international regime for Jerusalem, while a minority favored a federal arrangement. Khalidi argues that the majority proposal was highly favorable to the Jewish minority, allocating it more than 56 percent of the territory despite the Arab majority. The committee’s recommendations became the basis for the General Assembly’s partition resolution. Understanding the majority and minority proposals is important because partition was not the only constitutional option considered, and the final vote emerged from diplomatic negotiation and international pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the UNSCOP majority and minority plans by territorial design, constitutional structure, and proposed treatment of Jerusalem."
Key point:
- UNSCOP considered more than one constitutional solution.
- The majority recommendation shaped the proposal submitted to the General Assembly.
Source links:- United Nations: Special Committee on Palestine Report
- United Nations: Yearbook of the United Nations 1947–48
25. Q: What did General Assembly Resolution 181 provide for?
A: Show answer
Resolution 181, adopted on 29 November 1947, recommended partition of Mandatory Palestine into a Jewish state and an Arab state, with Jerusalem under a special international regime. It also envisaged an economic union and a transition toward the proposed political arrangements. In Khalidi’s account, the plan allocated more than 56 percent of the territory to the Jewish state and the remainder to the Arab state, even though the Jewish population was a minority in the country at the time. The resolution was a General Assembly recommendation, not by itself a mechanism that could enforce a peaceful transfer of authority. Its adoption intensified the struggle over the country’s future and became a major diplomatic and political turning point.
Study instruction:
- "Read the resolution’s operative provisions and distinguish the proposed states, Jerusalem regime, economic union, and implementation arrangements."
Key point:
- Resolution 181 recommended partition and an international status for Jerusalem.
- A General Assembly recommendation and an enforceable settlement are not the same thing.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
26. Q: Why does Khalidi describe the partition vote as a second declaration of war?
A: Show answer
Khalidi uses the phrase because he sees Resolution 181 as an international decision that legitimized the creation of a Jewish state over most of an Arab-majority country without meaningful Palestinian consent. He argues that the vote gave international standing to a project whose implementation would require a major territorial and demographic transformation. This is the author’s political and historical interpretation of the resolution, not the resolution’s formal title or wording. A careful reader should examine both the text of Resolution 181 and the chapter’s argument about self-determination, population, territory, and the power of the states that supported it.
Study instruction:
- "Write two separate summaries: one describing Resolution 181 in neutral legal terms and one explaining Khalidi’s interpretation of its political consequences."
Key point:
- The phrase is Khalidi’s interpretive characterization of the partition decision.
- Distinguish the resolution’s formal provisions from the author’s judgment about their implications.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
27. Q: How did the partition plan compare with the 1937 Peel Commission proposal?
A: Show answer
Khalidi contrasts the 1947 plan with the 1937 Peel Commission proposal to show how much the territorial allocation had shifted. He states that the Jewish state contemplated in the Peel plan would have covered about 17 percent of Palestine, whereas the 1947 plan allocated more than 56 percent to the proposed Jewish state. The comparison highlights the scale of the later proposal and the changing international balance. It should not be treated as a complete comparison of the two plans, which differed in political context, boundaries, and implementation proposals. Its purpose in the chapter is to show why Palestinians and Arab leaders viewed the 1947 partition recommendation as especially consequential.
Study instruction:
- "Locate both plans and compare their proposed borders, population distribution, political arrangements, and historical context."
Key point:
- The territorial proportions changed substantially between the 1937 and 1947 proposals.
- Percentages alone do not capture all differences between constitutional plans.
Source links:- United Nations: Special Committee on Palestine Report
- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
28. Q: How did Britain’s position on partition differ from that of the United States and the Soviet Union?
A: Show answer
By 1947 Britain had become hostile to the Zionist campaign that was driving it from Palestine and was concerned about preserving relationships with Arab states across its remaining Middle Eastern interests. It therefore abstained on Resolution 181 rather than voting for partition. The United States and Soviet Union both supported the resolution, despite their wider ideological and strategic rivalry. Khalidi argues that this alignment reflected a new global balance in which Britain no longer controlled the outcome. The contrast also shows that states can support the same international proposal for different reasons and that their preferences can change as regional and domestic circumstances evolve.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the three powers’ votes, strategic priorities, and relationships with the actors in Palestine in late 1947."
Key point:
- Britain abstained while the United States and Soviet Union supported partition.
- The postwar balance of power displaced Britain as the central external actor.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
29. Q: What was Plan Dalet, and where does it fit in the chronology of the Nakba?
A: Show answer
Plan Dalet was the name Khalidi gives to the broad Zionist military offensive in the spring of 1948. He describes it as involving the conquest and depopulation of major Arab urban centers and numerous towns and villages before Israel’s proclamation on 15 May 1948. The chapter identifies the fall of Tiberias, Haifa, Safad, Beisan, Jaffa, and Arab neighborhoods of West Jerusalem as part of the first phase of the Nakba. Historians debate the interpretation, scope, and implementation of Plan Dalet, so the term should not be used as a substitute for examining specific operations, orders, local circumstances, and civilian outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Build a dated map of the locations discussed and distinguish the plan as described by Khalidi from the events documented in individual local histories."
Key point:
- In the chapter’s chronology, large-scale displacement began before Israel declared independence.
- Analyze specific operations rather than treating a plan title as a complete explanation.
30. Q: Why were the falls of Jaffa and Haifa especially consequential?
A: Show answer
Jaffa and Haifa were among Palestine’s largest Arab urban centers and important economic, commercial, and cultural hubs. Khalidi describes Jaffa as besieged and bombarded before it was overrun in the first weeks of May 1948, after which most of its approximately 60,000 Arab residents were displaced. Haifa’s Arab population also fled or was expelled following attacks on poorly defended civilian neighborhoods. The loss of these cities destroyed more than residential communities: it removed commercial networks, workplaces, institutions, and leadership bases. Their fall therefore accelerated the transformation of Palestinian society from an established urban and rural population into a dispersed refugee population.
Study instruction:
- "For each city, identify its prewar social and economic role, the military sequence, the civilian consequences, and the resulting loss of institutions."
Key point:
- The loss of major cities had economic and social effects beyond immediate displacement.
- Urban destruction changed the capacity of Palestinian society to sustain itself.
Source links:- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
31. Q: What role did the Dayr Yasin massacre play in the first phase of displacement?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the killing of civilians at Dayr Yasin on 9 April 1948 as a major event that spread fear among Palestinian communities. The chapter reports that approximately one hundred villagers were killed, including women, children, and older people, during an assault involving Irgun and Haganah forces. News of the massacre, combined with the fall of nearby al-Qastal and the death of Palestinian commander Abd al-Qadir al-Husayni, contributed to panic and flight in surrounding areas. The massacre is therefore treated as part of the broader wartime pattern that affected civilian decisions to leave, not as the only cause of the mass displacement.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the immediate local events at Dayr Yasin from the wider military and psychological effects described in the chapter."
Key point:
- Violence against civilians can produce displacement far beyond the location of the attack.
- The chapter places Dayr Yasin within a wider sequence of military operations and social collapse.
32. Q: Why was the death of Abd al-Qadir al-Husayni at al-Qastal a major setback?
A: Show answer
Abd al-Qadir al-Husayni was described as one of the most capable and respected Palestinian military leaders remaining after the British repression of the Great Revolt. He died during the battle for al-Qastal on 8 April 1948, after returning from an unsuccessful attempt in Damascus to obtain arms from an Arab League committee. His death weakened Palestinian efforts to defend the approaches to Jerusalem at a critical moment. Khalidi uses the episode to connect battlefield outcomes with the broader failure of Arab governments to provide coordinated support. It also illustrates how the loss of experienced leaders could have disproportionate consequences when command structures were already fragile.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the sequence from the request for arms to the battle, the commander’s death, and the strategic importance of the Jerusalem approaches."
Key point:
- Leadership losses are especially damaging when organizations lack depth and succession capacity.
- The chapter links local military events to wider failures of regional coordination.
33. Q: What were the two broad phases of the Nakba described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The first phase ran from the UN partition vote on 29 November 1947 through the end of the British Mandate and the establishment of Israel on 15 May 1948. Khalidi describes Palestinian and Arab volunteer forces being defeated by organized Zionist forces, followed by a spring offensive and mass displacement of about 300,000 Palestinians before the end of the Mandate. The second phase followed the entry of Arab regular armies after 15 May. Khalidi estimates that another 400,000 Palestinians were expelled or fled during the subsequent fighting and that most were not allowed to return. The two phases show that displacement preceded and continued after the formal establishment of Israel.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-column timeline separating events before and after 15 May 1948, including military actors, affected locations, and displacement estimates."
Key point:
- The Nakba unfolded over months and across two major wartime phases.
- The proclamation of Israel did not mark the beginning or end of Palestinian displacement.
Source links:- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
34. Q: How should the chapter’s refugee estimates be interpreted?
A: Show answer
Khalidi gives an overall estimate of at least 720,000 refugees from a prewar Palestinian population of about 1.3 million in the relevant territory. He divides the displacement roughly between 300,000 people in the first phase before 15 May 1948 and another 400,000 in the later phase. These are historical estimates presented by the author and should be compared with demographic studies that use different definitions, dates, and geographic boundaries. A responsible account identifies whether a figure refers to people who crossed an international boundary, people internally displaced, or a broader population that lost homes and property. Figures should not be mixed without checking the population category and period.
Study instruction:
- "Record each figure alongside its source, date range, geographic scope, and definition of refugee or displaced person."
Key point:
- Historical estimates require clear definitions and denominators.
- The chapter’s totals describe a large-scale transformation but should be compared with other demographic research.
Source links:- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
35. Q: How did the absence of a Palestinian state-like structure affect the military campaign of 1947–48?
A: Show answer
The absence of a central government, unified command, and administrative network made it difficult to coordinate local defense, procure weapons, allocate funds, communicate strategy, and sustain forces across different areas. Palestinian resistance was real, but it was fragmented and often dependent on local commanders or volunteers. The Zionist movement, by contrast, had built military, political, financial, and administrative institutions over decades. Khalidi argues that this asymmetry contributed to the rapid loss of cities and territory. The comparison does not mean that institutions alone determine battlefield outcomes; it shows how military capability depends on logistics, intelligence, recruitment, command, and political coordination as well as courage and numbers.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two sides’ command structures, supply chains, intelligence, financial resources, and ability to coordinate local forces."
Key point:
- Military effectiveness depends on institutions and logistics, not only manpower.
- Fragmented command magnified the disadvantages faced by Palestinian defenders.
36. Q: Why does the chapter challenge the claim that seven Arab armies invaded Israel in 1948?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the familiar description exaggerates the number and role of regular Arab armies that entered the territory of Mandatory Palestine. He states that only five regular Arab forces were in the field, that Saudi Arabia and Yemen did not possess modern armies capable of participating in the way often implied, and that the Lebanese army did not cross the frontier. He also says that the Jordanian Arab Legion and Iraqi forces were restricted by their British relationship from entering the areas allocated to the Jewish state. His purpose is to challenge the simplified image of a united, overwhelming invasion and to show the actual limits and political constraints on Arab military action.
Study instruction:
- "Check which states had forces in the field, which crossed into Palestine, their operational areas, and the constraints described by the chapter."
Key point:
- The phrase “seven invading armies” can obscure differences in actual force deployment and mandate.
- Military claims should be tested against operational records, not repeated as slogans.
37. Q: What was the role of the Jordanian Arab Legion during the 1948 war?
A: Show answer
The Arab Legion was the most experienced and effectively organized Arab force in the chapter’s account. It had been trained, equipped, and commanded under strong British influence. Khalidi argues that it helped prevent Israeli forces from taking the West Bank and East Jerusalem, but that this outcome served Abdullah’s territorial ambitions and denied Palestinians an independent Arab state in those areas. The Legion therefore had a double significance: its military performance limited Israel’s territorial gains in one area, while its political role helped secure Hashemite control. A careful assessment must separate battlefield effectiveness from the political objectives of the government directing the force.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the Legion’s military performance, British command influence, territorial result, and implications for Palestinian sovereignty."
Key point:
- The Arab Legion’s military success did not produce Palestinian self-government.
- Military outcomes must be analyzed alongside the political arrangements they enabled.
38. Q: What was the Government of All Palestine, and why did it fail?
A: Show answer
After the Nakba, former members of the Arab Higher Committee attempted to establish a Government of All Palestine in Gaza as a government-in-exile for the Arab state envisaged by Resolution 181. Khalidi says the effort was led by Ahmad Hilmi Pasha and included Dr. Husayn al-Khalidi, but it failed to obtain support from key Arab states, particularly Jordan, which opposed independent Palestinian representation. It also received no meaningful international recognition. The failure illustrates how the postwar political vacuum was filled by Arab governments that often prioritized their own agendas. It also shows that declaring a government is not enough without territory, administrative capacity, diplomatic recognition, resources, and control over security.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the Government of All Palestine against the practical requirements of statehood: territory, population, institutions, recognition, revenue, and security."
Key point:
- The government-in-exile lacked the regional support and recognition needed to function.
- Political declarations cannot replace effective institutions and control.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
39. Q: How did the Soviet Union’s relationship with Israel change after 1948?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that Soviet support for the creation of Israel was initially based partly on an expectation that the new state might become a socialist ally and a counterweight to British influence in the Arab monarchies. That expectation did not endure. Israel moved closer to the United States and remained neutral during the Korean War, prompting Soviet leaders to reassess their assumptions. By 1955 the Soviet Union had developed closer relations with several Arab states, while Israel was secretly cooperating with Britain and France against Egypt. The episode shows that Soviet support for partition in 1947 did not amount to a permanent strategic alliance with Israel.
Study instruction:
- "Create a timeline of Soviet policy from support for partition to the cooling of relations by the mid-1950s."
Key point:
- Soviet support for Israel was conditional and changed as Cold War calculations evolved.
- A state’s vote on partition should not be mistaken for a permanent alliance.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
40. Q: How did the United States’ relationship with Israel develop after 1948?
A: Show answer
The United States recognized Israel and supported it politically, but Khalidi cautions against projecting the massive military and economic assistance of the 1970s backward onto the state’s first decades. He notes that early American support was more limited and that Washington sometimes voted for Security Council measures condemning Israeli actions. US policymakers increasingly saw Israel as a potential strategic asset during the Cold War, while oil interests and relations with Saudi Arabia remained important considerations. Khalidi argues that American leaders gave even less attention to Palestinian interests than they gave to Israel as a state. The development was gradual and contested rather than a fully formed alliance from the outset.
Study instruction:
- "Separate recognition, diplomatic support, military assistance, and strategic partnership, and note how each changed over time."
Key point:
- US support existed early, but its scale and strategic form changed substantially.
- The chapter distinguishes American support for Israel from the later institutionalized aid relationship.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Memorandum to President Truman, 2 October 1945
41. Q: What immediate social and economic effects did the Nakba have on Palestinian communities?
A: Show answer
The Nakba uprooted people from homes, land, workplaces, businesses, and established community networks. Rural families lost agricultural land and livelihoods, while urban residents lost property, commercial capital, and professional institutions. The old notable class also lost much of its economic base and political authority. Even families who avoided extreme poverty experienced a sudden loss of place, possessions, routines, and access to relatives. Khalidi’s family story illustrates how displacement separated generations and scattered relatives across several countries. The chapter therefore treats the Nakba as a social and economic rupture, not simply a change in sovereignty or a refugee count.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one rural and one urban example from the chapter and trace the loss of assets, income, community networks, and political influence."
Key point:
- Displacement destroyed livelihoods and social institutions as well as homes.
- The consequences affected both refugees and people who remained in Palestine.
Source links:- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
42. Q: How did the Nakba reshape Palestinian political and social leadership?
A: Show answer
The destruction of the pre-1948 social order weakened the established notable families and discredited many of the older leaders. Khalidi notes that younger Palestinians from more varied social backgrounds later became prominent, while the old leadership rarely regained its former authority. This change was connected to the loss of property, the dispersal of communities, the failure of earlier institutions, and the new political environments in host countries. The chapter compares this transformation with wider social changes in the Arab world, where military-backed revolutions also displaced older elites. The result was a political vacuum and a new generation of activism, although the transition was neither immediate nor uniform.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the pre-1948 notable class with the post-Nakba generation in terms of social base, political organization, and access to resources."
Key point:
- The Nakba changed who could exercise political influence within Palestinian society.
- Leadership change followed the destruction of the social and economic base of the old elite.
43. Q: What happened to Palestinians who remained inside Israel after 1948?
A: Show answer
Khalidi estimates that about 160,000 Palestinians remained inside the new state. They became citizens but were treated by the state with suspicion as a potential fifth column. The chapter states that most lived under military administration until 1966 and that substantial areas of their land were seized and transferred to Jewish settlements, the Israel Lands Authority, or the Jewish National Fund. Movement restrictions isolated communities from other Palestinians and the wider Arab world. Khalidi argues that the legal and institutional framework made it difficult for Arab owners to recover or lease property that had been taken. The account highlights the gap between formal citizenship and substantive equality in land, movement, and political status.
Study instruction:
- "Separate citizenship status, military administration, land policy, freedom of movement, and access to political participation when assessing the position of Palestinian citizens."
Key point:
- Remaining in the new state did not mean that Palestinians retained their land or enjoyed equal conditions.
- The chapter emphasizes the distinction between formal citizenship and practical rights.
44. Q: What role did the Jewish National Fund and land policy play in the post-1948 transformation?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes land seizure and the allocation of property to Jewish settlements, the Israel Lands Authority, and the Jewish National Fund as central to the new state’s demographic and political transformation. He notes that only a small share of Palestinian land had been Jewish-owned before 1948, and argues that the transfer of much of the remaining land helped consolidate Jewish control. The JNF’s charter restricted the use of its land to the benefit of the Jewish people, which the chapter presents as preventing dispossessed Arab owners and other non-Jews from buying or leasing former property. The analysis connects property law and administrative institutions to the material consequences of displacement.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how land title, absentee property rules, state administration, settlement, and restrictions on leasing affected former owners."
Key point:
- Land policy converted wartime dispossession into a durable postwar structure.
- Property institutions can preserve or deepen the consequences of displacement.
45. Q: How did UNRWA become central to the lives of Palestinian refugees?
A: Show answer
The United Nations Relief and Works Agency for Palestine Refugees in the Near East was established by the General Assembly in 1949 to provide assistance to Palestine refugees. The chapter describes UNRWA’s role in relief, education, vocational training, and services in refugee communities. These programs helped meet urgent needs and gave many Palestinians skills that later supported employment and migration. Khalidi emphasizes that refugee registration and camp residence are not identical: many registered refugees lived outside camps, while others moved out as their economic circumstances changed. UNRWA became a major institution in refugee life, but its services did not resolve the underlying political questions of return, property, or self-determination.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish UNRWA’s service mandate from the political and legal questions surrounding refugee status, return, compensation, and resettlement."
Key point:
- UNRWA provided essential services but did not itself settle the refugee question.
- Registered refugee status does not mean that a person lives in a camp.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 302 (IV), establishing UNRWA
- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
46. Q: What does the chapter say about the difference between registered refugees and camp residents?
A: Show answer
Khalidi reports that by the period covered in his account, about 5.5 million Palestinian refugees and descendants were registered with UNRWA, while roughly four million of those registered did not live in refugee camps. He gives examples across Jordan, the West Bank, Syria, Lebanon, and Gaza to show that most refugees and their descendants lived in towns, cities, or other housing rather than in camps. Some people never registered because they had resources, skills, relatives, or alternative accommodation. Others left camps as their circumstances improved. The distinction matters because camp populations are not a complete measure of the refugee population, and registration is an administrative category rather than a description of current housing.
Study instruction:
- "When using refugee data, record separately the number registered with UNRWA, the number living in camps, and the date of the estimate."
Key point:
- Refugee registration and camp residence are different categories.
- Statistics should not imply that all refugees live in camps.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 302 (IV), establishing UNRWA
47. Q: How did Jordan’s annexation of the West Bank affect Palestinians?
A: Show answer
In 1950 King Abdullah annexed the West Bank and extended Jordanian citizenship to Palestinians within the expanded kingdom. Khalidi notes that the annexation was recognized only by a small number of states, including the United Kingdom and Pakistan. Citizenship gave many Palestinians legal access to Jordanian institutions and social life, but it did not erase objections to the loss of an independent Palestinian political future. Some Palestinians adapted to the Hashemite state, while others remained hostile to Abdullah because of his opposition to Palestinian sovereignty and his reported contacts with Zionist leaders. The chapter therefore presents citizenship as a significant practical measure that coexisted with unresolved national and political claims.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the legal effects of Jordanian citizenship from the question of whether annexation fulfilled Palestinian self-determination."
Key point:
- Citizenship could improve legal status without resolving the political question of Palestine.
- Integration and national identity were not mutually exclusive.
48. Q: Why were Palestinians in Lebanon treated differently from Palestinians in Jordan?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that Lebanon’s political system depended on a delicate sectarian balance that had been shaped under French Mandate rule. Because most Palestinian refugees were Muslim, granting them Lebanese citizenship was viewed by many Lebanese political actors as potentially altering that balance. Palestinians faced restrictions on employment and property ownership and were monitored by the army’s intelligence service. Both Lebanese and Palestinian actors opposed tawtin, or permanent resettlement, although for different reasons. In Jordan, by contrast, Abdullah extended citizenship to Palestinians after annexing the West Bank. The comparison shows that host-state policy was shaped by domestic political structures, not by one uniform Arab approach to refugees.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Jordan and Lebanon across citizenship, employment, property rights, political representation, and the domestic reasons for each policy."
Key point:
- Host-country treatment varied according to national political arrangements.
- Opposition to permanent resettlement could coexist with very different legal regimes.
49. Q: What legal and social position did Palestinians have in Syria?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Palestinians in Syria as receiving many benefits associated with Syrian citizenship, including access to state education, government employment, and the right to own land, while remaining without Syrian nationality or the full political rights of citizens. They were generally issued refugee travel documents rather than national passports and could not vote. This produced a distinctive form of social and economic integration without full legal naturalization. Khalidi contrasts this with the more restrictive conditions in Lebanon and the citizenship granted by Jordan. The example demonstrates why refugee status should not be treated as a single uniform experience across host countries.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparison of Palestinian legal status in Syria, Jordan, and Lebanon, identifying which rights were granted and which remained restricted."
Key point:
- Social integration does not necessarily mean nationality or full political rights.
- Refugee experiences differed substantially across host states.
50. Q: How did education and vocational training affect Palestinian refugee mobility?
A: Show answer
Khalidi credits UNRWA’s education and vocational programs with helping Palestinians develop skills that were in demand across the Arab world. As oil-producing Gulf countries and other states expanded their economies, many Palestinians found work in education, government services, engineering, and other professions. This enabled some families to leave camps and establish lives in new cities. Yet migration did not guarantee citizenship or permanent residence. In Gulf states, the ability to remain often depended on continued employment, even for people who had lived there for many years. The chapter therefore presents education as a route to mobility and economic contribution, while emphasizing that professional success did not remove legal insecurity or the desire to return.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the link from UNRWA education to professional skills, employment migration, economic contribution, and continuing residency uncertainty."
Key point:
- Education expanded opportunities for many refugees and their descendants.
- Economic integration did not automatically provide citizenship or secure permanent residence.
Source links:- United Nations: UNRWA Special Report, 1956
51. Q: Why did the Nakba become a lasting focus of Palestinian collective identity?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Nakba became a shared historical reference because it disrupted families, communities, livelihoods, and the relationship between Palestinians and their homeland. People experienced it directly or through parents and grandparents, and the experience was transmitted across generations. Dispersal also divided Palestinians among different states and legal systems, creating varied identities and practical constraints on movement. The result was both fragmentation and a durable collective memory. The chapter’s family account illustrates how one household could be scattered across Palestine, neighboring Arab countries, Europe, and the United States while retaining a common attachment to the lost home.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the material, political, and intergenerational elements that connect personal displacement to collective identity."
Key point:
- Collective memory can persist across dispersed families and different legal settings.
- The Nakba produced both fragmentation and a shared national reference point.
52. Q: How did the 1948 defeat affect political change in other Arab countries?
A: Show answer
The chapter links the defeat in Palestine to wider instability in neighboring Arab states. The influx of refugees, military humiliation, and criticism of existing rulers intensified public dissatisfaction. Khalidi cites Gamal Abdel Nasser’s reflection that officers fighting in Palestine were also thinking about political change at home. He notes that military officers who had fought in Palestine later participated in coups in Syria, Egypt, and Iraq between 1949 and 1958. These developments had multiple causes, including poverty, colonial dependency, and domestic political conflict, so the Palestine war should not be treated as the sole cause. It nevertheless became a powerful symbol of governmental weakness and regional vulnerability.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a causal map linking the 1948 defeat to public pressure, military dissatisfaction, domestic political grievances, and later coups."
Key point:
- The Palestine war intensified existing political weaknesses in Arab states.
- Regional shocks interacted with domestic causes rather than replacing them.
53. Q: Why did Palestinians become politically invisible after 1948?
A: Show answer
After the Nakba, most Palestinians were dispersed and lacked an internationally recognized state or unified leadership. Arab governments often spoke about Palestine in intergovernmental forums, but they did not provide a consistent independent Palestinian voice. At the United Nations, the issue was commonly framed as an Arab-Israeli conflict, with Arab states taking the lead. Khalidi argues that Palestinians were invoked as a cause while having little control over the decisions made in their name. The failure of the Government of All Palestine and the weakness of the old leadership reinforced this marginalization. The chapter presents invisibility as a political condition created by displacement, institutional weakness, and the interests of surrounding states.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the forums in which Palestinians were represented by others and the reasons independent representation failed."
Key point:
- A people can remain central to a political dispute while being excluded from decision-making.
- Representation requires institutions and the ability to speak independently.
54. Q: How did Palestinian political activism revive after the Nakba?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a gradual revival of activism as Palestinians rejected the idea that Arab governments or international institutions would reverse the consequences of 1948 on their behalf. Small groups began organizing, and some undertook armed raids across borders. Initially these activities were scattered and lacked unified command, but they helped create the political environment in which organizations such as Fatah emerged in 1959. The chapter emphasizes that this activism developed despite repression by host governments and the danger of Israeli retaliation. It was a response to dispossession and political marginalization, but it also created new dilemmas about strategy, civilian risk, and the relationship between Palestinian organizations and the states in which refugees lived.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the development from local and clandestine activity to the emergence of Fatah, noting the constraints imposed by host governments."
Key point:
- Palestinian activism revived partly because existing state actors did not represent Palestinian priorities.
- New organizations emerged within a difficult regional security environment.
55. Q: Why did Egypt seek to control rather than simply encourage independent Palestinian armed activity?
A: Show answer
After the 1952 revolution, Nasser’s government prioritized domestic development and initially sought to avoid provoking Israel. Palestinian militant activity from Gaza threatened to draw Egypt into a conflict for which its army was not adequately prepared. Egyptian intelligence therefore arrested, harassed, and obstructed Palestinian organizers even while public sympathy for Palestine remained strong. Later, as Israeli attacks increased and Egyptian leaders faced domestic and regional pressure, Cairo changed its approach and assisted some militant activity. Khalidi presents the creation of the Palestine Liberation Organization by the Arab League in 1964 as a later and major attempt by Arab governments to channel and control independent Palestinian activism.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Egypt’s early post-revolution policy, its security concerns in Gaza, and its later use of regional institutions to influence Palestinian politics."
Key point:
- Governments could support the Palestine cause rhetorically while restricting independent Palestinian action.
- Host-state security concerns shaped the development of Palestinian organizations.
56. Q: What were the feda’iyin, and why did their activity matter in the early 1950s?
A: Show answer
Feda’iyin were Palestinian militants whose name is commonly translated as those who sacrifice themselves. The chapter describes early cross-border raids as sporadic and often lethal, emerging from small groups operating despite opposition from neighboring governments. Their actions reflected a refusal to accept the post-1948 status quo, but they also exposed Palestinians and nearby Arab communities to severe Israeli reprisals. Khalidi emphasizes that the raids were not simply the policy of Arab governments; in many cases they were undertaken against those governments’ wishes. Understanding the feda’iyin therefore requires examining both the political motive of resistance and the consequences of armed activity in a region where state forces were unequal.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the motivations of Palestinian militants, the policies of host governments, and the consequences of cross-border operations."
Key point:
- Early feda’iyin activity was often independent of Arab state policy.
- Resistance actions and retaliatory attacks formed a cycle with serious civilian consequences.
57. Q: How did Israeli retaliatory policy affect Jordan, Egypt, and the Palestinian population?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Israeli reprisals were often disproportionate and aimed not only at the individuals involved in raids but also at pressuring Arab governments to suppress Palestinian activity. The attacks affected border villages, refugee communities, and military positions in neighboring states. This created a dilemma for Arab governments: they faced public pressure to support Palestinians but feared further attacks from a militarily stronger Israel and disapproval from major powers. The chapter treats retaliation as a mechanism that shaped regional politics and contributed to escalation, rather than as a series of isolated border incidents. A careful account should examine the original raid, the target and scale of the response, civilian harm, and the relevant armistice obligations.
Study instruction:
- "For a selected incident, build a sequence showing the initiating event, military response, civilian impact, diplomatic reaction, and longer-term political effect."
Key point:
- Retaliation influenced both security policy and the political behavior of neighboring governments.
- The effects of a military response must be assessed against the original incident and applicable agreements.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Security Council Resolution 101, 24 November 1953
58. Q: What happened at Qibya in October 1953, and how did the United Nations respond?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an Israeli military raid on the West Bank village of Qibya on 14–15 October 1953 following the killing of three Israeli civilians. Israeli forces under Ariel Sharon destroyed homes and killed 69 Palestinian civilians according to the account cited by Khalidi. On 24 November 1953 the Security Council adopted Resolution 101, finding that the retaliatory action violated the cease-fire provisions of Resolution 54 and was inconsistent with the Israel-Jordan General Armistice Agreement and the UN Charter. The Council also called for measures to prevent recurrence and urged cooperation with the UN truce supervision arrangements. The resolution is a primary source for the international response.
Study instruction:
- "Read Resolution 101 and identify its findings, legal references, requests to the parties, and voting result."
Key point:
- The Security Council formally censured the Qibya raid as inconsistent with cease-fire and armistice obligations.
- The resolution addressed both Israeli retaliation and obligations to prevent cross-border violence.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Security Council Resolution 101, 24 November 1953
59. Q: What policy disagreement existed between David Ben-Gurion and Moshe Sharett over retaliation?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts Ben-Gurion’s belief that sustained military force would compel Arab states to accept peace on Israel’s terms with Prime Minister Moshe Sharett’s more cautious view that aggressive reprisals needlessly provoked neighboring states and closed opportunities for compromise. Both leaders opposed major territorial concessions and significant refugee return, but they differed over the use and scale of force. The debate matters because it shows that Israeli policy was not monolithic. It also helps explain how internal cabinet choices influenced the escalation that culminated in the 1956 Suez War. A careful historical analysis should distinguish the leaders’ stated views, the decisions actually adopted, and later interpretations of their motives.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two leaders’ positions on deterrence, retaliation, diplomacy, territorial concessions, and the risks of escalation."
Key point:
- There was an internal Israeli debate over the strategic value of disproportionate force.
- Policy differences should be traced to actual decisions, not reduced to labels.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
60. Q: Why did Egypt move from suppressing Palestinian militants to helping some of them in 1955?
A: Show answer
Egypt’s position changed as Israeli military operations against Egyptian and police posts in Gaza intensified. The chapter describes major attacks in Rafah and Khan Yunis during 1955, which exposed Egypt’s military weakness and embarrassed Nasser’s government before Egyptian and Arab public opinion. Egypt had first sought arms from Britain and the United States, but when those efforts failed it concluded a large arms agreement with Czechoslovakia, a Soviet client, in September 1955. At the same time, Egyptian intelligence began assisting Palestinian militants it had previously suppressed. Khalidi presents this shift as a response to pressure, strategic vulnerability, and the political cost of appearing unable to protect Gaza.
Study instruction:
- "Build a chronology linking Israeli attacks, Egypt’s arms requests, the Czechoslovak agreement, and the change in policy toward Palestinian militants."
Key point:
- Egypt’s policy shifted as military weakness and public pressure increased.
- The arms agreement and the change in support were connected to a wider Cold War setting.
61. Q: What were the principal causes of the Suez War of October 1956?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a convergence of Israeli, British, and French objectives. Israel sought to strike Egypt amid escalating border conflict and Palestinian militancy. Britain and France were angered by Egypt’s nationalization of the Suez Canal Company, opposed Nasser’s challenge to their regional influence, and objected to Egyptian support for Algerian independence. Britain also wanted to end Egypt’s challenge to its military presence and influence elsewhere in the Arab world. The three governments reached a secret agreement at Sèvres and launched a coordinated offensive under the public pretext of separating the combatants. The war was therefore not simply a spontaneous response to border raids; it was tied to longstanding imperial, strategic, and regional aims.
Study instruction:
- "Create a three-column comparison of Israeli, British, and French objectives and distinguish immediate triggers from longer-term strategic motives."
Key point:
- The Suez War was a coordinated intervention with distinct but overlapping state objectives.
- The nationalization of the canal and regional anti-colonial politics were central to British and French motives.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 997 (ES-I), 2 November 1956
- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
62. Q: What was the Protocol of Sèvres, and why was it significant?
A: Show answer
The Protocol of Sèvres was a secret agreement negotiated shortly before the 1956 war among Israel, Britain, and France. It provided the basis for their coordinated military action against Egypt. Khalidi regards it as a reversal of the estrangement between Britain and the Zionist movement that had developed after the 1939 White Paper. The agreement also demonstrates how public justifications for intervention can differ from the private planning and strategic objectives of the governments involved. Its significance lies in the evidence of prior coordination and the return of Britain and France to direct military action in the region, even as their wider imperial power was declining.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the parties to the agreement, the sequence of the planned operation, and the difference between the public pretext and private coordination."
Key point:
- Sèvres revealed a prearranged tripartite plan rather than an improvised response.
- Secret diplomatic agreements can clarify the motives behind public military explanations.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
63. Q: Why did the United States and Soviet Union oppose the 1956 tripartite attack on Egypt?
A: Show answer
The United States opposed the operation because Britain and France had acted without consulting Washington and because the intervention appeared neocolonial at a moment when the Soviet Union was suppressing the Hungarian uprising. The Soviet Union opposed the attack on its new Egyptian partner and saw an opportunity to challenge Western influence, while also benefiting from the diversion of attention from Hungary. Despite their Cold War rivalry, both superpowers used diplomatic and economic pressure to force withdrawal. The Soviet Union threatened force, the United States warned of economic consequences, and both supported action through the United Nations General Assembly after a Security Council resolution was blocked by the likely British and French veto.
Study instruction:
- "Compare US and Soviet motives for opposing the intervention and explain how their pressure differed from a shared long-term alliance."
Key point:
- The superpowers aligned against the Suez intervention for different strategic reasons.
- Their combined pressure demonstrated the reduced freedom of action of the former European imperial powers.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 997 (ES-I), 2 November 1956
- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
64. Q: What were the consequences of the Suez War for Britain, France, Israel, Egypt, and Nasser?
A: Show answer
Militarily, Egypt was rapidly defeated by the tripartite forces, but the political outcome favored Egypt. US and Soviet pressure forced Britain, France, and Israel to withdraw from Egyptian territory and the Gaza Strip, with Israel completing its withdrawal from Sinai and Gaza in early 1957. Nasser emerged as a pan-Arab hero because he had resisted the intervention of the old colonial powers. Britain and France suffered a major loss of prestige and demonstrated that they could no longer act freely without the approval of the superpowers. For Israel, the operation did not produce the durable strategic gains its leaders sought. Palestinian residents of Gaza, however, suffered severe violence during the occupation.
Study instruction:
- "Separate military results from diplomatic and political outcomes, and identify which actors gained or lost influence after the withdrawal."
Key point:
- Egypt’s battlefield defeat became a political victory for Nasser and a setback for the old imperial powers.
- The Gaza population bore severe costs that were obscured by the interstate focus of the war.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 997 (ES-I), 2 November 1956
- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
65. Q: What does the UNRWA Special Report say about the 1956 events in Khan Yunis and Rafah?
A: Show answer
The UNRWA Special Report A/3212/Add.1 covers the period from 1 November to mid-December 1956 and records the agency’s operations and the effects of military activity on Gaza refugees. Khalidi cites it in discussing the killings in Khan Yunis and Rafah during the Israeli occupation. The chapter reports 275 men killed in Khan Yunis and the nearby camp on 3 November, 111 in Rafah on 12 November, and another 66 between 1 and 21 November. Khalidi argues that the report contradicted the claim that these deaths occurred during continuing resistance. Readers should consult the report itself for its documented observations and distinguish those from the author’s broader interpretation of a pattern of violence.
Study instruction:
- "Read the UNRWA report’s sections on casualties, protests, and the situation in Gaza, then compare its wording with the chapter’s narrative."
Key point:
- A/3212/Add.1 is a contemporaneous UN agency report on the 1956 Gaza emergency.
- Separate what the report directly records from later historical interpretation.
Source links:- United Nations: UNRWA Special Report, 1956
66. Q: Why does Khalidi describe Gaza as a recurring center of Palestinian resistance?
A: Show answer
Gaza contained a large, concentrated refugee population after 1948 and experienced repeated military operations, occupation, and restrictions. Khalidi argues that the loss of homes and the concentration of displaced communities created a powerful environment for political mobilization. The chapter identifies Gaza as an important base for early Fatah leaders and later Palestinian organizations, while also describing the severe consequences of Israeli retaliation and the 1956 occupation. The area’s significance therefore came from both its social conditions and its political history. It became a focal point of resistance because it was central to the experience of dispossession, but that role also exposed its population to repeated violence.
Study instruction:
- "Connect Gaza’s refugee demographics, political organization, military geography, and exposure to retaliation in a short explanatory diagram."
Key point:
- Gaza’s political role was shaped by the concentration of refugees and the history of repeated conflict.
- The same conditions that enabled mobilization also increased civilian vulnerability.
Source links:- United Nations: UNRWA Special Report, 1956
67. Q: How did the Palestine issue become a political instrument for Arab governments?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Arab rulers often invoked Palestine because their populations cared deeply about the issue and because the 1948 defeat symbolized regional weakness. Yet those same governments feared Israel’s military power, dependence on Western states, and the risk of escalation. They therefore sometimes used strong public rhetoric while limiting independent Palestinian activity or avoiding direct confrontation. The issue became a means of competing for popular legitimacy and outbidding rival leaders, rather than a basis for a unified policy. This helps explain why Palestinian organizations faced repression in some host countries even while governments publicly declared support for the Palestinian cause.
Study instruction:
- "Compare public statements with concrete policies on refugee rights, political organization, border security, and diplomatic representation."
Key point:
- Rhetorical support and practical support are not equivalent.
- Domestic legitimacy and security calculations shaped Arab state policy toward Palestine.
68. Q: What did the creation of the Palestine Liberation Organization in 1964 represent in the chapter’s account?
A: Show answer
Although Chapter 2 ends with the 1956 Suez War, Khalidi refers to the PLO’s creation in 1964 as a later response by Arab governments to growing independent Palestinian activism. Established by the Arab League at Egypt’s initiative, the organization represented a major effort to channel, coordinate, and partly control Palestinian political activity. Its creation illustrates a recurring tension: Arab governments wanted to manage the Palestine issue and prevent actions that might provoke Israel, while Palestinians increasingly sought independent leadership. The reference helps connect the post-Nakba political vacuum of the 1950s to the institutional development of the Palestinian national movement in the following decade.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the PLO is mentioned as a later development and distinguish the 1964 organization from the earlier post-1948 Government of All Palestine."
Key point:
- The PLO emerged from a different political context than the 1948 government-in-exile.
- The chapter links independent Palestinian activism to later attempts by Arab states to manage it.
69. Q: How should a reader distinguish the chapter’s documented chronology from its causal interpretation?
A: Show answer
The chapter combines dates, quotations, family recollections, archival references, and the author’s argument about colonial power, institutional asymmetry, and regional rivalry. A careful reader should record what a primary document directly states, what a witness recalls, what a historian infers, and what Khalidi argues as an overall explanation. For example, Resolution 181’s text can be read directly, while the description of it as a second declaration of war is Khalidi’s interpretation. Similarly, a UNRWA report provides contemporaneous observations about Gaza, while conclusions about a wider pattern require additional historical analysis. Separating these levels strengthens rather than weakens critical understanding.
Study instruction:
- "For three major claims, label the evidence as primary document, eyewitness recollection, secondary scholarship, or authorial interpretation."
Key point:
- Evidence, recollection, and interpretation are different kinds of historical claims.
- Critical reading requires preserving the distinction between what a source records and what an author concludes.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
- United Nations: UNRWA Special Report, 1956
70. Q: What are the most important lessons of Chapter 2 for understanding the period 1947–1956?
A: Show answer
Chapter 2 argues that the Nakba emerged from a convergence of international realignment, Zionist institutional and military preparation, Palestinian political fragmentation, British imperial policy, and competing Arab state ambitions. Resolution 181 internationalized partition, but the subsequent war and displacement transformed the country before and after Israel’s establishment. The refugee crisis then reshaped Palestinian identity, host-country politics, and the regional balance. Palestinian activism revived as refugees concluded that Arab governments and international bodies would not independently restore their political agency. The 1956 Suez War demonstrated how local Palestinian resistance, Israeli retaliation, Arab state weakness, and great-power rivalry could combine into a wider regional crisis. The chapter’s central lesson is to analyze these developments as connected processes rather than isolated wars.
Study instruction:
- "Review the chapter’s timeline and prepare a one-page synthesis linking 1947 partition, 1948 displacement, postwar refugee regimes, renewed activism, and the 1956 Suez War."
Key point:
- The period is best understood as a connected sequence of political, social, military, and international transformations.
- Palestinian agency persisted despite displacement, institutional weakness, and regional constraints.
Source links:- United Nations: General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)
- United Nations: Resolution 194 and the Palestine Refugee Question
- United Nations: UNRWA Special Report, 1956
- U.S. Department of State: Suez Crisis and the Sèvres Discussions
