Table 3
The Third Declaration of War, 1967: Occupation, Palestinian Resurgence, and Regional Politics
1. Q: What is the chapter’s central argument?
A: Show answer
Khalidi treats the 1967 war as a turning point that brought the West Bank, East Jerusalem, Gaza, Sinai and the Golan Heights under Israeli control, altered US and regional diplomacy, and reshaped the Palestine question. He argues that Resolution 242 narrowed diplomacy toward state-to-state negotiations and sidelined Palestinians, while the defeat paradoxically accelerated Palestinian political and cultural revival. The chapter follows the rise of Fatah and the PFLP, the PLO’s diplomatic gains, its conflicts with host states and the Lebanese war through 1982.The chronology should be reconstructed before assigning responsibility or motive. Identify what each actor knew at the time, which alternatives were available and how earlier decisions constrained later choices. Compare contemporary records with later recollections, and distinguish a trigger from the structural conditions that made escalation possible. The specific issue is the chapter’s central argument. This comparison helps prevent a later outcome from being projected backward as if it had been inevitable.
Study instruction:
- "Map the argument across the war, Resolution 242, national revival, diplomacy and Lebanon."
Key point:
- Military defeat and diplomatic exclusion coexisted with Palestinian political resurgence.
- He argues that Resolution 242 narrowed diplomacy toward state-to-state negotiations and sidelined Palestinians, while the defeat paradoxically accelerated Palestinian political and…
Source links:- UNSC Resolution 242
2. Q: Which territories were occupied by Israel in 1967?
A: Show answer
Israeli forces took control of the Sinai Peninsula and Gaza Strip, the West Bank including East Jerusalem, and the Golan Heights. These areas had been administered by Egypt, Jordan and Syria respectively. Their occupation transformed the political and legal context of the conflict. Later annexation claims and different territorial arrangements must be considered separately; the territories should not be treated as having identical histories or legal status.The diplomatic significance should be judged by the text, the parties included, the obligations created and the mechanisms for implementation. A resolution or communiqué may establish a framework without resolving the underlying claims. Compare the formal wording with subsequent state practice and identify whose interests could be represented. The specific issue is territories were occupied by Israel in 1967. The distinction between formal language and actual implementation is essential to a sound conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Mark each territory on a map and identify its pre-war administering authority."
Key point:
- The war placed several distinct territories under Israeli military control.
- These areas had been administered by Egypt, Jordan and Syria respectively.
Source links:- UNSC Resolution 242
3. Q: How did Israel achieve rapid military success?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes the Israeli first strike against Arab air forces and the resulting air superiority. With most Egyptian, Syrian and Jordanian aircraft destroyed on the ground, Israeli ground forces gained a decisive operational advantage. Regional divisions, Egyptian forces committed to Yemen and differences in military preparedness also mattered. No single factor explains the outcome: military capability, intelligence, political choices and regional strategy interacted.The strategic analysis should distinguish declared aims from available capabilities and actual effects. Consider logistics, command, regional alliances, host-state constraints and the consequences for civilians. An action that creates political visibility may also impose military costs or narrow future options. The specific issue is Israel achieve rapid military success. Assess the immediate effect separately from longer-term political and humanitarian consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Compare air operations, ground advances, military readiness and regional commitments."
Key point:
- Air superiority was a decisive operational advantage.
- With most Egyptian, Syrian and Jordanian aircraft destroyed on the ground, Israeli ground forces gained a decisive operational advantage.
Source links:- US Foreign Relations: June 1, 1967 memorandum
4. Q: What role did Palestinian armed groups play before the war?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies Palestinian commando activity as one factor in the regional escalation. Fatah’s January 1965 operation against a water installation was intended partly as a symbolic demonstration of independent Palestinian action. Raids from Syrian bases and Israeli reprisals increased pressure on Arab governments, especially Egypt. These developments formed part of a wider crisis and should not be treated as the sole cause of war.The organizational dimension involves leadership legitimacy, internal decision-making, constituency, resources and continuity. A movement’s public claims may be stronger than its ability to coordinate or sustain action. Identify who had authority, who supplied resources and who bore the risks. The specific issue is Palestinian armed groups play before the war. Institutional capacity should be judged by what the organization could actually coordinate and sustain.
Study instruction:
- "Build a timeline from 1965 to May 1967 and note actions and reactions by each actor."
Key point:
- Palestinian activism affected regional calculations but did not alone cause the war.
- Fatah’s January 1965 operation against a water installation was intended partly as a symbolic demonstration of independent Palestinian action.
5. Q: What was the Arab Cold War?
A: Show answer
The term describes rivalry between competing Arab political blocs, especially Egypt’s Arab nationalist camp and the conservative monarchy-centered camp led by Saudi Arabia. The competition involved ideology, regional leadership and influence in Yemen and elsewhere. Khalidi argues that these rivalries weakened coordination and shaped Egyptian decisions during the 1967 crisis. 9. Q: How did US policy toward Israel change by 1967?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Cold War calculations, regional realignments and political relationships in Washington brought the Johnson administration into closer alignment with Israel. He contrasts this with earlier episodes when US administrations pressed Israel to withdraw from territory captured in 1956 or expressed reservations about specific policies. The shift was gradual but consolidated after 1967. 10. Q: What does the Amit–McNamara memorandum establish?
A: Show answer
The official US memorandum records a June 1, 1967 conversation between Israeli intelligence chief Meir Amit and Defense Secretary Robert McNamara. It documents Amit’s assessment and discussion of possible Israeli military action, and records McNamara’s questions and his indication that he would convey Amit’s views to President Johnson. Khalidi interprets the exchange as evidence that Israel sought reassurance about the US response. The document should be read carefully: its actual wording must be distinguished from later characterizations of the exchange.The diplomatic significance should be judged by the text, the parties included, the obligations created and the mechanisms for implementation. 11. Q: What was Security Council Resolution 242?
A: Show answer
Adopted unanimously on November 22, 1967, Resolution 242 set out principles for a peaceful settlement. It emphasized the inadmissibility of acquiring territory by war, called for withdrawal of Israeli armed forces from territories occupied in the recent conflict, addressed termination of belligerency and referred to a just settlement of the refugee problem. Khalidi argues that its state-to-state framing and omission of explicit reference to Palestinians narrowed the diplomatic agenda. Its text and drafting history should be studied directly.The strategic analysis should distinguish declared aims from available capabilities and actual effects. Consider logistics, command, regional alliances, host-state constraints and the consequences for civilians. An action that creates political visibility may also impose military costs or narrow future options. The specific issue is Security Council Resolution 242. Assess the immediate effect separately from longer-term political and humanitarian consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Annotate the resolution clause by clause and identify both its provisions and omissions."
Key point:
- Resolution 242 became a foundational framework but did not expressly name Palestinians.
- It emphasized the inadmissibility of acquiring territory by war, called for withdrawal of Israeli armed forces from territories occupied in the recent conflict, addressed…
Source links:- UNSC Resolution 242 (1967)
12. Q: Why does the chapter criticize Resolution 242?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Resolution 242 treated the conflict primarily as a dispute between Israel and neighboring Arab states and did not identify Palestinians as a principal political party. Its reference to a refugee problem did not explicitly address self-determination, national representation, Jerusalem or the full consequences of 1948 displacement. This is the author’s critical interpretation; readers should compare the resolution’s wording with later General Assembly decisions and subsequent diplomatic practice.The organizational dimension involves leadership legitimacy, internal decision-making, constituency, resources and continuity. A movement’s public claims may be stronger than its ability to coordinate or sustain action. Identify who had authority, who supplied resources and who bore the risks. The specific issue is the chapter criticize Resolution 242. Institutional capacity should be judged by what the organization could actually coordinate and sustain.

13. Q: Why did the wording of Resolution 242 become controversial?
A: Show answer
The English text calls for withdrawal from “territories occupied” in 1967, while the French text uses wording commonly translated as “the territories occupied.” Khalidi explains that the difference became part of later debate about the scope of withdrawal. 19. Q: What was the Movement of Arab Nationalists and how did it lead to the PFLP?
A: Show answer
The Movement of Arab Nationalists was a pan-Arab organization associated with George Habash and Wadi Haddad. It initially emphasized Arab unity as the means of reversing the consequences of the Nakba. After the 1967 defeat, confidence in Arab regimes declined and the movement shifted toward a more explicitly Palestinian focus. The PFLP emerged in 1967 with a Marxist-Leninist orientation. Its later tactics, including aircraft hijackings, generated serious political and ethical controversy.The strategic analysis should distinguish declared aims from available capabilities and actual effects. 20. Q: How did Fatah differ from the PFLP?
A: Show answer
The chapter characterizes Fatah as broadly nonideological and focused on direct Palestinian action, while the PFLP had a Marxist-Leninist identity and revolutionary program. Fatah’s broad-tent approach attracted people who rejected both Arab state tutelage and the idea that social transformation had to precede action on Palestine. The PFLP appealed particularly to leftist students, intellectuals and some refugee communities. Their differences shaped alliances and strategic choices.The organizational dimension involves leadership legitimacy, internal decision-making, constituency, resources and continuity. 26. Q: What rights did UNGA Resolution 3236 affirm?
A: Show answer
Resolution 3236 reaffirmed Palestinian rights to self-determination, national independence and sovereignty, and the right to return to homes and property from which people had been displaced. This language marked a significant development in international recognition.The diplomatic significance should be judged by the text, the parties included, the obligations created and the mechanisms for implementation. 33. Q: How did the two-state approach emerge within the PLO?

34. Q: What was the significance of the Khartoum “three nos”?
A: Show answer
The 1967 Khartoum summit is associated with no peace, no recognition and no negotiations with Israel. Khalidi cautions that this slogan did not capture all Arab state practice: Egypt and Jordan explored mediation and diplomatic channels, while Egypt’s acceptance of Resolution 242 implied willingness to negotiate secure and recognized boundaries. Table 4
The 1982 Lebanon War, the Siege of Beirut, and the Sabra and Shatila Massacre
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 4, “The Fourth Declaration of War, 1982”?
A: Show answer
Rashid Khalidi presents the 1982 Israeli invasion of Lebanon as a decisive turning point in the Palestinian struggle. The chapter argues that the invasion was not merely a limited border operation to stop attacks from Lebanon: its strategic purpose included destroying the Palestine Liberation Organization’s (PLO) political and military infrastructure in Beirut and weakening Palestinian nationalism in the occupied West Bank and Gaza. It also examines the interaction of Israeli military decisions, indispensable American diplomatic and material support, the passivity of Arab governments, the PLO’s own strategic and political weaknesses in Lebanon, and the failure of assurances intended to protect Palestinian civilians after the PLO’s evacuation. The author’s interpretation should be distinguished from the documentary record and from other historians’ assessments of motives and responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Trace the chapter’s argument from the June invasion through the siege, evacuation, September re-entry into West Beirut, and the massacre.
Key point:
- The chapter connects the Lebanon campaign to the wider struggle over Palestinian political agency.
- Treat the author’s thesis as an interpretation to test against documents and other accounts.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
2. Q: What events immediately preceded the Israeli invasion of Lebanon in June 1982?
A: Show answer
The chapter places the invasion in the context of the July 1981 cease-fire between Israel and the PLO, negotiated through U.S. envoy Philip Habib after exchanges of fire across the Israel-Lebanon border. The cease-fire largely held for about ten months, although the parties interpreted its scope and violations differently. On June 3, 1982, gunmen from the Abu Nidal organization, a group hostile to the PLO, attempted to assassinate Israeli ambassador Shlomo Argov in London. Israel used the attack as the immediate justification for launching its invasion on June 6, even though the perpetrators were not acting under PLO command. The sequence matters because it separates the stated trigger from the longer-running strategic plans and political objectives discussed in the chapter.
Study instruction:
- Build a dated timeline for the 1981 cease-fire, the Argov assassination attempt, and the June 6 invasion; identify the actors responsible for each event.
Key point:
- The Abu Nidal organization was distinct from the PLO.
- A proximate trigger does not by itself explain the broader aims of a military campaign.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
3. Q: Why does the chapter describe the invasion as more than a limited operation?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the scale, duration, geographic reach, and political objectives exceeded the public description of a narrow response to the Argov attack. Israeli forces advanced through southern Lebanon, the Shouf and Metn areas, and the Biqa‘ Valley, then encircled West Beirut and subjected it to a prolonged siege. The chapter describes the destruction of PLO offices and military positions, the confrontation with Syrian forces, and the attempt to reshape the political order in Lebanon. It further argues that the campaign was intended to reduce the PLO’s influence over Palestinians in the occupied territories. The label “limited operation” is therefore examined as a political description that should be compared with operational plans, force levels, territorial advances, and the campaign’s actual consequences.
Study instruction:
- Compare the stated rationale for the operation with the military objectives and geographic scope described in the chapter and contemporary records.
Key point:
- Military scale and strategic purpose are separate questions from the label used to justify an operation.
- Evaluate official descriptions against actions and outcomes.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
4. Q: What objectives does Khalidi attribute to Ariel Sharon and the Israeli government?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies overlapping objectives. The Israeli government sought to remove the PLO’s armed presence from Lebanon and push Syrian forces out of the country. Sharon also pursued a plan to install a Lebanese government aligned with Israel. Khalidi argues that the deeper objective was to weaken Palestinian nationalism by destroying the PLO’s institutional base in Lebanon, thereby reducing its capacity to influence Palestinians in the West Bank, Gaza Strip, and East Jerusalem. The chapter emphasizes that Begin and other cabinet members did not necessarily receive the same operational picture as Sharon, who is portrayed as controlling important details of the plan. These claims concern the author’s interpretation of intent and should be evaluated alongside cabinet records, diplomatic communications, and later declassified material.
Study instruction:
- Separate the immediate military objectives, Lebanon-related political objectives, and the claimed wider objective concerning Palestinian nationalism.
Key point:
- The chapter distinguishes the campaign’s declared purpose from its wider strategic ambitions.
- Claims about leaders’ intentions require documentary corroboration.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
5. Q: What role does the chapter attribute to the United States before the invasion began?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that U.S. diplomatic approval was an important enabling condition for the invasion. He discusses Sharon’s May 25, 1982 meeting with Secretary of State Alexander Haig, in which Sharon reportedly outlined a broad plan for action in Lebanon. The chapter interprets Haig’s response about the need for a “recognizable provocation” as a green light, while noting that the Argov assassination attempt was carried out by the Abu Nidal group rather than the PLO. The U.S. government’s internal debate was more complex than a single position: later official histories describe disagreement among senior officials over whether and how to pressure Israel. A careful assessment therefore distinguishes the author’s argument about prior assent from the documented evolution of U.S. policy during the war.
Study instruction:
- Compare Khalidi’s account of the Haig-Sharon meeting with the U.S. Department of State historical account and identify where the sources emphasize different aspects.
Key point:
- Diplomatic signaling can affect the perceived freedom of action of an ally.
- The U.S. administration contained competing views and should not be treated as a single undifferentiated actor.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
6. Q: How did the 1982 invasion unfold militarily?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a multi-front offensive beginning with air attacks on Beirut on June 4, followed by the ground invasion on June 6. Israeli forces advanced north along the coast, through the Shouf Mountains, and into the Biqa‘ Valley. Palestinian and Lebanese fighters resisted in the south, while Syrian forces fought Israeli units in the east and mountain areas. The Israeli military possessed major advantages in numbers, armor, air power, and naval support. A cease-fire with Syria on June 26 changed the military balance and left the PLO and its Lebanese allies facing the siege of West Beirut with much less external military support. The chronology is essential: the campaign was not a single battle but a sequence of offensives, encirclement, bombardment, negotiations, and withdrawal.
Study instruction:
- Draw a map showing the coastal advance, the Shouf, the Biqa‘ Valley, and West Beirut; annotate the key dates and cease-fire changes.
Key point:
- Geography and the sequence of cease-fires shaped the PLO’s increasingly isolated position.
- Military superiority did not eliminate the political and humanitarian consequences of urban warfare.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
7. Q: What made the siege of West Beirut particularly destructive for civilians?
A: Show answer
West Beirut was a densely populated urban area containing homes, businesses, hospitals, refugee communities, and PLO offices. The chapter describes repeated air, artillery, and naval bombardment, damage to apartment blocks, and shortages of water, electricity, food, and fuel. It recounts the effects of displacement, interrupted communications, damaged infrastructure, and the difficulty of obtaining safe passage or reliable information. Khalidi also distinguishes attacks that were described as carefully targeted from strikes that, in his account, destroyed buildings with no plausible military utility. The chapter’s estimates of deaths and injuries are attributed to the sources it cites and should be reported with their stated provenance rather than treated as uncontested totals.
Study instruction:
- Identify the direct effects of bombardment and the indirect effects caused by disruption of essential services and civilian movement.
Key point:
- Urban siege warfare affects civilians through both direct violence and the breakdown of essential systems.
- Casualty figures should be attributed to the specific source and counting method.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 512 (1982): Humanitarian assistance
8. Q: How did the PLO’s military and political position in Lebanon change during the siege?
A: Show answer
Before the invasion, the PLO had built a substantial political and military presence in Lebanon, including offices, communications facilities, armed units, and relationships with Lebanese political groups. The invasion and the Syrian cease-fire progressively narrowed its options. By the siege of West Beirut, the PLO faced a vastly superior Israeli force and increasing diplomatic pressure to leave. Its fighters could resist, but prolonged resistance risked further destruction of the city and harm to civilians. The evacuation therefore represented both a major military defeat and a negotiated effort to preserve personnel and protect those who would remain. The chapter also argues that the PLO’s earlier conduct had weakened its relationships with parts of Lebanese society, making the crisis more difficult to manage.
Study instruction:
- Assess the PLO’s position across three dimensions: military capacity, political legitimacy among Lebanese communities, and diplomatic support from regional governments.
Key point:
- The PLO’s military isolation was compounded by political and diplomatic isolation.
- A movement’s local relationships can determine its resilience during a military crisis.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon, 1981–1984
9. Q: What weaknesses in the PLO’s conduct in Lebanon does Khalidi acknowledge?
A: Show answer
The chapter does not present the PLO as free of responsibility for its own political failures. It describes indiscipline, abuses of authority, armed checkpoints, and incidents in which Lebanese civilians were harmed without adequate accountability. It also argues that the PLO became a dominant armed presence in parts of Lebanon, sometimes acting beyond the constraints contemplated by the 1969 Cairo Agreement. Such conduct generated resentment among Lebanese communities already suffering from civil war and Israeli attacks. Khalidi’s analysis is that the PLO failed to understand how its behavior was eroding support among potential allies. A balanced reading recognizes these criticisms while distinguishing them from any justification for attacks on civilians or violations by other armed forces.
Study instruction:
- Identify examples of organizational misconduct and explain how accountability, discipline, and respect for host communities affect political legitimacy.
Key point:
- A liberation movement’s stated cause does not excuse misconduct by its members.
- Local legitimacy is a strategic resource that can be lost through impunity.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 512 (1982): Civilian protection
10. Q: What was the significance of the 1969 Cairo Agreement for the PLO’s presence in Lebanon?
A: Show answer
The chapter refers to the Cairo Agreement as a framework that gave the PLO a defined role in Palestinian refugee camps and freedom of action in parts of southern Lebanon. Over time, however, the PLO’s armed and political influence expanded beyond what many Lebanese considered acceptable. The agreement therefore provides context for the tension between Palestinian organizational autonomy and Lebanese sovereignty. It is important not to treat the agreement as an unlimited license: its implementation, the Lebanese state’s authority, the civil-war environment, and the conduct of armed groups all affected how it operated in practice. The chapter uses the agreement to explain how a formal arrangement could become politically unsustainable when the balance of power and local consent changed.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter’s description of the Cairo Agreement and distinguish its formal framework from the later realities of PLO power in Lebanon.
Key point:
- Legal or political arrangements operate within changing political conditions.
- Host-community consent and accountability are essential to the durability of an armed presence.
11. Q: Why did support for the PLO erode among important Lebanese communities before and during the invasion?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several interacting causes. Some Lebanese civilians resented PLO checkpoints, armed dominance, and failures to discipline personnel. Residents of southern Lebanon, many of them Shi‘a, had repeatedly experienced displacement and Israeli attacks associated with the armed conflict. Sunni leaders and residents of West Beirut had historically regarded the PLO as an ally against Maronite political and militia dominance, but the prospect of the city being devastated by a prolonged siege changed their calculations. Druze leaders in the Shouf faced the threat of Israeli military advance and competing militia violence. The chapter therefore depicts support as conditional and diverse, not as a single unified Lebanese position. It also describes a later shift when Lebanese Muslim leaders backed civilian safeguards during the PLO withdrawal negotiations.
Study instruction:
- Compare the concerns of Shi‘a communities in the south and Biqa‘, Druze leaders in the Shouf, and Sunni communities in Beirut, Tripoli, and Sidon.
Key point:
- Lebanese political communities had distinct interests and experiences.
- Political alliances can weaken when the costs of an ally’s strategy fall heavily on local civilians.
12. Q: How did the positions of Amal, Walid Jumblatt, and Sunni political leaders affect the PLO’s situation?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses the Syrian-aligned Amal movement and its Shi‘a constituency, the Druze leadership associated with Walid Jumblatt in the Shouf, and Sunni political leaders and urban communities. Their support for the PLO was not uniform or constant. Some members of these communities continued to fight alongside Palestinian forces, while leaders faced pressure to protect their own populations from Israeli advances, militia violence, and further destruction. The chapter explains that these groups became more supportive of the PLO’s withdrawal demands when it became clear that the organization was prepared to leave Beirut in exchange for credible protection for civilians. Their changing positions demonstrate that alliances are shaped by immediate security concerns, political calculations, and confidence in the proposed outcome.
Study instruction:
- Construct a stakeholder table showing each group’s interests, sources of vulnerability, initial position, and later response to the withdrawal plan.
Key point:
- Coalition support can change as threats and proposed safeguards change.
- Avoid treating Lebanese factions as interchangeable actors.
13. Q: What was the PLO’s Eleven-Point Plan, and why was it important?
A: Show answer
According to the chapter, the PLO presented its Eleven-Point Plan on July 8 as a framework for withdrawing from Beirut while reducing the danger to the civilians who would remain. The proposals included a buffer between Israeli forces and West Beirut, Israeli military withdrawal or repositioning, an enduring international presence, and safeguards for Palestinian and Lebanese residents. The plan was important because it framed withdrawal as a negotiated security problem rather than a simple departure of combatants. It also helped persuade Lebanese Muslim leaders that the PLO was prepared to leave if civilian protection could be secured. The plan’s significance must be assessed against the final arrangements and the extent to which the proposed protections were actually implemented.
Study instruction:
- List the main protection mechanisms described in the chapter and compare them with the final evacuation arrangements.
Key point:
- Withdrawal arrangements must address the safety of people who remain, not only the movement of departing forces.
- A proposal is not effective unless its safeguards are implemented and monitored.
Source links:- U.S. Department of Defense: Plan for the Departure from Lebanon, August 1982
14. Q: What assurances were given to protect Palestinian civilians after the PLO evacuated Beirut?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes written assurances transmitted through Lebanese Prime Minister Shafiq al-Wazzan and U.S. envoy Philip Habib. They stated that law-abiding Palestinian non-combatants remaining in Beirut, including families of those who departed, would be allowed to live in peace and security. The United States said its guarantees rested on assurances received from Israel and certain Lebanese groups, while the Lebanese government was to obtain assurances from armed groups within its reach. Khalidi argues that the PLO relied on these commitments when agreeing to evacuate. The central issue is the gap between a diplomatic assurance and an enforceable protection mechanism: the notes lacked formal signatures and clear operational enforcement, and the chapter argues that no adequate long-term international force remained to protect civilians.
Study instruction:
- Identify who gave each assurance, who was expected to comply, which civilians were covered, and what monitoring or enforcement mechanism was available.
Key point:
- A security guarantee needs clear obligations, credible monitoring, and a response to violations.
- Written assurances do not protect civilians if the guarantors fail to act.
Source links:- Declassified U.S. departure plan and civilian safeguards
15. Q: How was the PLO evacuation from Beirut organized?
A: Show answer
The evacuation was negotiated through Lebanese intermediaries and Philip Habib, with the involvement of the United States, France, Italy, and regional governments. The agreed plan provided for the orderly departure of PLO leadership, offices, and combatants to destinations in several countries. A multinational force supervised aspects of the process, while the Lebanese government was expected to reassert authority in Beirut. The departures took place between August 21 and September 1, 1982. The chapter emphasizes the emotional response of many Beirut residents, who saw the departing fighters as having endured a prolonged siege, but also notes that the PLO’s dispersal left its personnel scattered across several Arab states and its former Beirut base dismantled.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the agreement’s parties, the multinational force’s role, the departure period, and the destinations or dispersal outcome described in the chapter.
Key point:
- The evacuation was a negotiated multinational process, not simply an unstructured retreat.
- Removing armed personnel did not itself resolve the protection needs of civilians left behind.
Source links:- White House report on the multinational force and PLO departure plan, August 24, 1982
16. Q: Why did the withdrawal of the multinational force matter to the safety of Palestinian civilians?
A: Show answer
The multinational force included American, French, and Italian personnel who helped supervise the evacuation. The chapter states that the force was withdrawn soon after the last PLO ship departed, rather than remaining for the longer period that many Palestinians believed was necessary to protect the refugee camps and neighborhoods. That withdrawal left civilians without the international presence that had been expected to deter armed groups and provide a measure of security. The distinction between supervising the departure and protecting the population afterward is crucial. A mission can complete its immediate task while leaving a foreseeable protection gap if the mandate, duration, and follow-on arrangements are not designed around the continuing risks.
Study instruction:
- Compare the multinational force’s evacuation mission with the separate requirement for sustained civilian protection after the departure was complete.
Key point:
- Mission completion and civilian safety are not equivalent outcomes.
- Protection plans must continue for as long as the threat remains credible.
Source links:- White House report on the PLO departure plan and multinational force
17. Q: What changed after Bashir Gemayel was assassinated on September 14, 1982?
A: Show answer
Following the assassination of President-elect Bashir Gemayel, Israeli forces entered and occupied West Beirut on September 15, despite prior assurances that they would not do so. The chapter describes how the Israeli army rapidly overcame limited resistance and positioned itself around the Sabra and Shatila refugee camps. Palestinian civilians and others in the area feared attacks because the PLO fighters had already departed and because of the history of violence by Lebanese militia forces. The sequence is important: the massacre occurred after the evacuation and after Israeli forces re-entered West Beirut. The assassination was the immediate trigger for the re-entry, but it does not remove the responsibility to protect civilians or to control forces operating in the occupied area.
Study instruction:
- Place the assassination, Israeli entry into West Beirut, the militia incursion into the camps, and the massacre on a single dated timeline.
Key point:
- The PLO’s departure preceded the Israeli re-entry into West Beirut.
- A change in military control creates immediate obligations toward the civilian population.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982): Sabra and Shatila
18. Q: What happened during the Sabra and Shatila massacre?
A: Show answer
Between September 16 and the morning of September 18, 1982, Lebanese Forces militiamen entered the Sabra and Shatila refugee camps and killed Palestinian and Lebanese civilians. The chapter describes Israeli forces surrounding the area and firing illumination flares during the night, enabling the militia operation to continue. Estimates of the number of victims vary among sources; Khalidi cites research that establishes a minimum of close to 1,400 killed while noting that the actual total may be unknowable because some people were abducted and never found. The massacre should be understood as an atrocity against civilians, not as combat between equivalent armed forces. When citing casualty estimates, state the source and its methodology rather than presenting one figure as beyond dispute.
Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter’s casualty discussion with the findings and evidentiary limits described by the Kahan Commission and later historical research.
Key point:
- The victims included civilians, including women, children, and older people.
- Responsible historical writing distinguishes confirmed minimums, estimates, and unknown totals.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982)
- Kahan Commission Report into the Events at the Refugee Camps in Beirut
19. Q: What was the significance of the Israeli army’s use of flares over Sabra and Shatila?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts that Israeli forces fired flares over the camps during the night of the massacre. The flares illuminated the area and, according to the chapter’s account, enabled the Lebanese Forces militiamen to continue operating after dark. The author uses this episode to explore responsibility beyond the individuals who physically carried out the killings. The use of illumination is a factual component of the sequence, while the legal and moral significance depends on what military commanders knew, intended, facilitated, or failed to prevent. The Kahan Commission examined the responsibilities of Israeli officials and commanders, providing an important documentary basis for assessing the chain of knowledge and command.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish direct perpetration, assistance, knowledge, command responsibility, and failure to prevent or stop an atrocity.
Key point:
- Responsibility can extend beyond the person who directly commits violence.
- Assess the evidence for each form of responsibility separately.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report
20. Q: What did the Kahan Commission investigate, and what were its principal conclusions?
A: Show answer
The Israeli government established the Kahan Commission of Inquiry after the massacre. Its mandate was to investigate the facts and factors connected with the atrocity committed by a Lebanese Forces unit against civilians in the Sabra and Shatila camps. The commission heard witnesses and examined the conduct and knowledge of Israeli political and military officials. Its findings assigned direct responsibility for the killings to the Lebanese Forces, while identifying personal responsibility and failures among senior Israeli officials, including Defense Minister Ariel Sharon, who was found to bear personal responsibility for disregarding the danger of a massacre and failing to take appropriate measures to prevent it. The report is a key primary source, but it should be read in full and compared with the additional archival material and criticisms discussed by historians.
Study instruction:
- Read the commission’s findings on Sharon and other officials and separate its conclusions from Khalidi’s assessment of the report’s limitations.
Key point:
- The commission distinguished the direct perpetrators from Israeli officials’ responsibility for the circumstances and foreseeable danger.
- A commission report is evidence to analyze, not a substitute for examining its mandate and omissions.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report into the Events at the Refugee Camps in Beirut
21. Q: Why does the chapter discuss declassified documents and secret appendices to the Kahan Commission report?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that later-released Israeli archival records and secret appendices provide additional evidence about what senior officials knew, the risks associated with sending Lebanese Forces militiamen into the camps, and the communications between Israeli and American officials. These records are used to question whether the public report fully disclosed the extent of prior knowledge and decision-making. The chapter’s interpretation is that the later documents make the responsibility of some Israeli officials more serious than the public report alone suggests. Readers should distinguish the existence and content of the documents from the author’s conclusions about their meaning, and should compare them with the commission’s published findings and other archival studies.
Study instruction:
- Identify which claims in the chapter rely on the public Kahan report, which rely on secret appendices, and which are the author’s synthesis of multiple records.
Key point:
- Newly available archives can change the evidentiary picture of a historical event.
- Documentary evidence still requires contextual interpretation and corroboration.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report
22. Q: What responsibilities does the chapter attribute to Israeli forces during the massacre?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that Israeli forces controlled the surrounding area, enabled the entry of Lebanese Forces militiamen into the camps, and provided illumination while the killings occurred. It also discusses the Israeli military and intelligence leadership’s prior knowledge of the militia’s record of atrocities and hostility toward Palestinians. These claims are part of Khalidi’s interpretation and should be tested against the Kahan Commission findings, the secret appendices cited in the chapter, and other archival research. The central analytical issue is not simply whether Israeli soldiers personally carried out the killings, but what they knew, what control they exercised, what actions they took or failed to take, and whether they had the ability and duty to prevent or stop the violence.
Study instruction:
- Build an evidence matrix with columns for knowledge, control, facilitation, preventive action, and response after reports of the killings emerged.
Key point:
- Responsibility in armed conflict can involve failures of prevention and protection as well as direct violence.
- Attribute each conclusion to the specific investigative or historical source that supports it.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report
23. Q: What role did the United States play in the PLO evacuation and the events that followed?
A: Show answer
The United States acted as a principal diplomatic intermediary through Philip Habib and participated in the multinational force that supervised the evacuation. It also provided Israel with substantial military assistance and exerted pressure on the PLO to accept withdrawal terms. The chapter criticizes the Reagan administration for refusing stronger international safeguards for civilians left behind and for allowing the multinational force to depart soon after the evacuation. It further argues that U.S. officials failed to ensure that the assurances they had given were honored after Israeli forces entered West Beirut. A complete assessment should consider both the administration’s stated objectives and the documentary record of its diplomatic commitments, military involvement, and subsequent responses.
Study instruction:
- Compare the evacuation plan, U.S. public statements, and the administration’s actions after the massacre became known.
Key point:
- The United States was both a diplomatic broker and a material supporter of Israel.
- Evaluate guarantees by examining implementation, monitoring, and follow-up, not wording alone.
Source links:- White House report on the PLO departure plan
- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon
24. Q: How did Arab governments respond to the invasion and siege of Beirut?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Arab governments offered little effective support to the PLO during the invasion and siege. Although leaders publicly expressed support for the Palestinian cause, they did not provide the military or diplomatic action that the PLO sought. The chapter describes how Arab governments were constrained by their relations with the United States, regional rivalries, domestic political concerns, and the danger of direct confrontation with Israel. It also notes the difference between official state policy and public opinion: televised images of the siege provoked anger among ordinary people, but repressive political systems limited the ability of that anger to force policy change. The analysis therefore distinguishes government decisions from popular sentiment.
Study instruction:
- Identify the difference between public statements, diplomatic initiatives, material assistance, and domestic public reaction in the Arab states discussed.
Key point:
- Expressions of solidarity do not necessarily translate into practical support.
- State policy and public opinion can diverge sharply.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon
25. Q: What was the Reagan Plan, and why did it fail to resolve the Palestinian question?
A: Show answer
President Ronald Reagan announced a Middle East peace initiative on September 1, 1982, shortly after the PLO evacuation. The plan proposed a form of Palestinian self-government associated with the West Bank and Gaza and opposed further Israeli settlement activity, but it ruled out an independent Palestinian state in those territories. Khalidi argues that the plan was not forcefully promoted by Washington and was readily rejected by the Begin government. It therefore did not create a durable negotiating framework or resolve the underlying dispute over sovereignty and Palestinian self-determination. The plan should be assessed by comparing its proposals with the positions of the parties and the political conditions of 1982, rather than treating the announcement itself as evidence of an implemented settlement.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the plan’s position on autonomy, settlements, and Palestinian statehood, then compare those proposals with the positions of the principal parties.
Key point:
- Autonomy and sovereign statehood are different political arrangements.
- An announced initiative has limited effect without sustained implementation and agreement by the parties.
Source links:- President Reagan: Address on U.S. Policy for Peace in the Middle East, September 1, 1982
26. Q: What did United Nations Security Council Resolution 512 (1982) require during the invasion?
A: Show answer
Resolution 512, adopted on June 19, 1982, expressed deep concern about the suffering of Lebanese and Palestinian civilians. It called on all parties to respect civilian rights, refrain from violence against civilians, and facilitate humanitarian assistance from United Nations agencies and nongovernmental organizations, including the International Committee of the Red Cross. It also emphasized the responsibilities of the United Nations and UNRWA toward affected civilian populations. The resolution provides an authoritative contemporaneous statement of the international community’s humanitarian concerns. It should be read as a Security Council resolution with specific requests and appeals, not as a complete account of the conflict or as a substitute for the applicable rules of international humanitarian law.
Study instruction:
- Read the operative paragraphs of Resolution 512 and identify the obligations or requests directed to parties, member states, and humanitarian agencies.
Key point:
- Civilian protection and humanitarian access were explicit international concerns during the conflict.
- Distinguish the resolution’s actual wording from broader legal conclusions.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 512 (1982)
27. Q: What did United Nations Security Council Resolution 521 (1982) say about Sabra and Shatila?
A: Show answer
Resolution 521, adopted unanimously on September 19, 1982, expressed the Security Council’s horror at the massacre of Palestinian civilians in Beirut and condemned the killings. It reaffirmed earlier resolutions calling for respect for civilian rights and repudiating violence against the civilian population. It also authorized an increase in United Nations observers in and around Beirut and called for their freedom of movement, while requesting urgent consultations about additional measures to protect civilians. The resolution is an important primary source for the international response immediately after the massacre. It establishes the Council’s condemnation and requested steps, but it does not by itself resolve all questions of individual criminal responsibility or the full chain of command.
Study instruction:
- Extract the resolution’s condemnation, observer provisions, and requests for further protection measures; distinguish them from later historical findings.
Key point:
- The Security Council expressly condemned the massacre of Palestinian civilians.
- International condemnation and monitoring requests are distinct from criminal adjudication.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982)
28. Q: How should the civilian-protection issues in the chapter be analyzed under international humanitarian law?
A: Show answer
The chapter raises questions about distinction between civilians and combatants, the prohibition of attacks directed at civilians, precautions in attack, the treatment of people in occupied areas, and the duty to protect persons under a force’s control. Resolution 512 refers to humanitarian principles in the 1949 Geneva Conventions and obligations associated with the Hague Convention. A careful legal analysis must identify the applicable treaty and customary rules, the status of the parties and territory, the conduct being assessed, and the evidence of knowledge and control. The chapter’s moral and historical judgments should not be confused with a judicial determination of liability. Where legal conclusions are drawn, they should be supported by the relevant legal texts and qualified legal analysis.
Study instruction:
- For each incident discussed, identify the protected persons, the actor exercising control, the alleged conduct, and the legal rule that may apply.
Key point:
- Civilian status and protection remain central even when armed groups operate in populated areas.
- Legal analysis requires precise facts and the applicable legal framework.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 512 (1982)
- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982)
29. Q: What was the significance of international media coverage during the siege and after the massacre?
A: Show answer
Beirut was an important center for international journalism, and the presence of experienced correspondents meant that the siege and its effects were widely reported. The chapter describes journalists documenting bombardment, civilian suffering, and the aftermath of the massacre. Media coverage helped make the events visible to audiences beyond Lebanon and contributed to public and diplomatic pressure. At the same time, the author discusses propaganda, censorship, and the difficulty of reporting amid danger and competing claims. Media accounts should therefore be evaluated by checking eyewitness access, contemporaneous records, corroboration, editorial decisions, and the distinction between firsthand observation and information supplied by interested parties.
Study instruction:
- Compare contemporaneous reporting with official statements and later documentary investigations; note where accounts corroborate or conflict.
Key point:
- Journalism can document events and shape public pressure, but it must still be critically evaluated.
- Eyewitness reporting, official claims, and retrospective analysis are different forms of evidence.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982)
30. Q: What were the immediate and longer-term consequences of the PLO’s evacuation from Beirut?
A: Show answer
The evacuation removed the PLO’s central political and military base from Beirut and dispersed its leadership and fighters among several Arab countries. It reduced the organization’s ability to operate from Lebanon and altered its relationship with regional governments and host communities. The chapter argues that the loss of the Beirut base did not end Palestinian nationalism, but it changed the geography and conditions of Palestinian political activity. The massacre also exposed the vulnerability of civilians left behind after armed forces withdrew and damaged confidence in international guarantees. Longer-term consequences should be traced through subsequent Palestinian political developments, the continuing Israeli occupation of southern Lebanon until 2000, and the changing regional environment rather than attributed to one event alone.
Study instruction:
- Separate the immediate organizational effects of evacuation from the longer-term political consequences for Palestinians and Lebanon.
Key point:
- The PLO’s loss of a base changed its strategy but did not extinguish the Palestinian national movement.
- Historical consequences develop through multiple interacting events.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon
31. Q: How should the chapter’s claim of joint Israeli-U.S. responsibility be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Khalidi characterizes the campaign as a joint Israeli-U.S. military endeavor because Israel initiated and conducted the invasion while relying on U.S. diplomatic support, military assistance, and influence over the evacuation negotiations. He also argues that U.S. assurances concerning civilians were not backed by effective protection after the PLO departed. Evaluating this thesis requires distinguishing different forms of involvement: political approval, arms transfers, operational participation, diplomatic pressure, guarantees, knowledge of risk, and post-event response. The U.S. Department of State history provides a useful comparison because it documents both the administration’s divisions and its later diplomatic and military actions. The phrase “joint endeavor” is an interpretive conclusion, not a neutral description that all sources necessarily adopt.
Study instruction:
- Create a responsibility matrix for Israeli decision-makers, U.S. officials, Lebanese militia leaders, and other actors, using only evidence that supports each attributed role.
Key point:
- Responsibility should be analyzed by type of conduct and supporting evidence.
- A historian’s thesis should be compared with primary documents and alternative interpretations.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon
32. Q: What is the difference between direct responsibility for a massacre and responsibility for failing to prevent it?
A: Show answer
Direct responsibility concerns those who physically carried out the killings or ordered them. Responsibility for failure to prevent or stop an atrocity concerns officials or commanders who had relevant knowledge, authority, and an opportunity to take reasonable action but did not do so. The Kahan Commission’s findings are important because they distinguish the Lebanese Forces militiamen who committed the killings from Israeli officials whose conduct and omissions were examined. The chapter goes further in its interpretation by discussing prior knowledge, facilitation, and the role of external actors. These categories should not be collapsed: the evidence required to establish direct perpetration differs from the evidence needed to assess command responsibility, complicity, or failure to protect.
Study instruction:
- For each actor, identify the alleged act or omission, authority, knowledge, ability to intervene, and source supporting the conclusion.
Key point:
- Different forms of responsibility require different evidence.
- Do not equate political criticism, moral responsibility, and legal liability without explaining the distinction.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report
33. Q: How should historians evaluate memoirs, eyewitness testimony, official records, and later archival evidence in this chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter combines the author’s personal recollections, interviews, contemporary journalism, PLO records, diplomatic documents, official inquiries, and later declassified material. Each source type has strengths and limitations. Eyewitness testimony can provide detail about lived experience but may be limited by location, memory, and what the witness could observe. Official records can reveal decisions and communications but may be incomplete, self-protective, or classified for decades. Memoirs and interviews can disclose perspectives unavailable elsewhere but require corroboration. A rigorous historian compares independent sources, checks chronology, distinguishes firsthand knowledge from hearsay, and makes clear when a claim is the author’s interpretation rather than an uncontested fact.
Study instruction:
- Select three major claims from the chapter and trace the supporting evidence back to the source type and any corroborating records.
Key point:
- No single source type is sufficient for every historical question.
- Transparent attribution and corroboration strengthen historical analysis.
Source links:- Kahan Commission Report
34. Q: What lessons does the chapter offer about military strategy, political legitimacy, and alliances?
A: Show answer
The chapter illustrates that military strength alone does not determine political outcomes. Israel’s overwhelming conventional superiority enabled it to advance and besiege Beirut, but the campaign generated severe civilian harm, international criticism, and political consequences. The PLO’s armed presence gave it influence but also created resentment when its conduct undermined local legitimacy. Lebanese communities made decisions based on their own security and political interests, while Arab governments and the United States pursued policies shaped by regional and domestic calculations. The chapter therefore invites analysis of the relationship between force, legitimacy, alliances, diplomacy, and the protection of civilians. These lessons should be applied cautiously: the historical circumstances were specific, and they do not provide a universal formula for later conflicts.
Study instruction:
- Explain how military capability, local legitimacy, alliance reliability, and civilian protection each affected the course of the 1982 crisis.
Key point:
- Tactical superiority does not guarantee durable political success.
- Alliances depend on trust, shared interests, and credible protection of affected communities.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: The Reagan Administration and Lebanon
35. Q: How can the chapter be summarized without confusing chronology, evidence, and interpretation?
A: Show answer
A disciplined summary should first establish the chronology: the 1981 cease-fire, the Argov assassination attempt, the June 1982 invasion, the advance and siege of Beirut, negotiations, the PLO evacuation, Gemayel’s assassination, Israeli entry into West Beirut, and the Sabra and Shatila massacre. It should then identify the main actors and their interests, including Israel, the PLO, Lebanese factions, Syria, the United States, Arab governments, and international observers. Finally, it should separate documented events and official findings from Khalidi’s arguments about strategic intent and shared responsibility. This method preserves the chapter’s central thesis while avoiding the mistake of presenting every interpretive claim as a universally accepted fact.
Study instruction:
- Write a one-page chronology, a separate actor-and-interest map, and a final paragraph that clearly labels the author’s principal interpretations.
Key point:
- Strong historical analysis keeps chronology, evidence, and interpretation distinct.
- A concise summary should preserve complexity rather than erase disagreement or uncertainty.
Source links:- UN Security Council Resolution 521 (1982)
Rows: 35
Table 5
The First Intifada, Palestinian Political Strategy, and the Oslo Process (1987–1995)
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 5, “The Fifth Declaration of War, 1987–1995”?
A: Show answer
Rashid Khalidi argues that the First Intifada transformed the Palestinian struggle by shifting its centre of gravity from armed organisations operating outside Palestine to a broad, locally rooted movement inside the occupied West Bank and Gaza. The uprising exposed the limits of military control and changed international perceptions, but the political opportunity it created was not converted into a settlement ending occupation. Khalidi traces how the PLO leadership in exile sought diplomatic recognition, how the Madrid and Washington negotiations restricted Palestinian representation and agenda, and how Oslo created limited self-government without transferring sovereignty. His conclusion is that Oslo modified the administration of occupation rather than resolving the struggle for self-determination. This is the chapter’s interpretive thesis and should be tested against the events and documents it discusses.
Study instruction:
- "Write a thesis summary that separates the uprising’s social and political effects from Khalidi’s assessment of the later negotiations."
Key point:
- The chapter links grassroots resistance, diplomatic strategy, and the Oslo framework.
- Khalidi’s central claim is that Oslo changed the form of control without delivering sovereignty.
2. Q: How did the 1982 invasion of Lebanon produce consequences that its planners had not intended?
A: Show answer
Khalidi presents the invasion as an attempt to destroy the PLO’s military and political infrastructure in Lebanon and weaken Palestinian nationalism in the occupied territories. Although the PLO was weakened and forced into exile, the campaign did not eliminate Palestinian resistance. Instead, the centre of mobilisation shifted toward people living under occupation. The First Intifada that began in December 1987 developed into a locally organised uprising that military authorities found difficult to suppress. The unintended consequence was a stronger internal Palestinian movement and a challenge to the assumption that removing external bases would extinguish opposition. The chapter uses this reversal to show how coercive action can generate political effects opposite to those intended.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the sequence from the 1982 invasion and PLO displacement to the local networks that emerged before December 1987."
Key point:
- Military defeat of an external organisation did not end popular opposition inside Palestine.
- The uprising demonstrated the limits of strategies based solely on coercion.
3. Q: What triggered the First Intifada in December 1987, and why is the trigger not the whole explanation?
A: Show answer
The immediate trigger was an Israeli army vehicle striking a truck in the Jabalya refugee camp in Gaza, killing four Palestinians. Protests spread rapidly across the occupied territories. Khalidi stresses that the incident was a spark rather than a sufficient explanation: the uprising arose from accumulated frustration with two decades of occupation, settlement expansion, restrictions on political expression, repression, and the absence of a credible path to self-determination. Distinguishing the trigger from the underlying causes prevents the uprising from being reduced to one accident. Its rapid spread reflected grievances and organisational capacities that had developed over many years.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the immediate Jabalya incident from the structural political and social conditions described in the chapter."
Key point:
- The Jabalya incident was the immediate catalyst, not the sole cause.
- Long-standing grievances explain why unrest spread so quickly.
4. Q: How could the First Intifada be spontaneous and organised at the same time?
A: Show answer
The uprising was not initially launched by the formal PLO leadership or by a centrally planned military campaign. It erupted from local communities and accumulated popular frustration. Yet spontaneity did not mean an absence of organisation. Activists built flexible networks in towns, villages, cities, and refugee camps, while a secret Unified National Leadership coordinated activity across the territories. This arrangement combined local initiative with shared political direction. Its decentralised structure made the movement adaptable and difficult for occupation authorities to dismantle. A movement can therefore arise without a pre-existing central command and then develop coordinating institutions as participants respond to changing circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how decentralised initiative and a coordinating leadership can coexist in a mass movement."
Key point:
- Spontaneous origins do not imply organisational disorder.
- Flexible local networks helped the uprising persist despite repression.
5. Q: What role did the Unified National Leadership play in the uprising?
A: Show answer
The Unified National Leadership provided a clandestine coordinating structure for a movement whose strength remained rooted in local communities. It helped align actions and messages across different places while allowing local groups to adapt tactics to their own circumstances. Khalidi describes it as secret and flexible rather than a conventional hierarchy with complete command over every participant. This arrangement reduced the vulnerability associated with a single visible headquarters and enabled the uprising to continue when activists were arrested or removed. It also created a political voice that could communicate the movement’s aims to Palestinians and international audiences. Its effectiveness depended on remaining connected to grassroots participants.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the advantages and limitations of clandestine coordination in a decentralised movement."
Key point:
- Coordination gave the uprising coherence without eliminating local initiative.
- Leadership is more effective when it remains connected to its base.
6. Q: Which social groups participated in the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes participation across Palestinian society: women and men, professionals, businesspeople, farmers, villagers, urban residents, students, shopkeepers, and people in refugee camps. This breadth challenged the idea that opposition was confined to a small group of militants. Different groups contributed through demonstrations, organising, boycotts, strikes, communication, community support, and other forms of civil disobedience. The range of participants meant that the movement could continue even when particular activists were detained or particular localities faced intense repression. Its social breadth was a source of resilience and made it harder to portray the uprising as a narrow armed campaign.
Study instruction:
- "Create a stakeholder map showing the different social groups involved and the forms of participation available to each."
Key point:
- The uprising drew support from multiple sectors of Palestinian society.
- Broad participation increased resilience and national reach.
7. Q: Why does the chapter emphasise women’s role in the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
Women played a central organising and leadership role, not merely a supporting role. Khalidi explains that women assumed more visible responsibilities as many men were imprisoned and helped mobilise people often excluded from conventional male-dominated politics. Their work strengthened local networks and broadened the movement’s social base. This contribution complicates accounts that treat political leadership as exclusively male or focus only on armed organisations. The chapter points to the documentary Naila and the Uprising as a fuller portrait of women’s participation. The wider lesson is that movement capacity depends on recognising the work of organising, sustaining communities, communicating, and coordinating collective action.
Study instruction:
- "Review the chapter’s discussion of women and compare visible public leadership with less visible organising work."
Key point:
- Women were central participants and leaders in the uprising.
- A complete account of collective action must examine who performs organising and sustaining roles.
Source links:- Just Vision: Naila and the Uprising
8. Q: What tactics were used during the First Intifada, and why is its largely nonviolent character significant?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists demonstrations, strikes, boycotts, tax withholding, and other forms of civil disobedience. Some confrontations became violent, and Khalidi notes that soldiers’ use of live ammunition and rubber bullets could intensify clashes. Nevertheless, he characterises the uprising as predominantly unarmed and nonviolent. This distinction matters because it broadened participation beyond those willing or able to engage in armed activity and shaped international perceptions. It also contrasted with the PLO’s earlier strategy of armed struggle from bases outside Palestine. The chapter does not claim that every incident was peaceful; it argues that the dominant form of mobilisation was popular civil resistance.
Study instruction:
- "Classify the tactics described and explain how nonviolent methods can broaden participation without assuming that every event was peaceful."
Key point:
- The uprising relied heavily on civil resistance and mass participation.
- Its largely unarmed character contributed to its political and international impact.
9. Q: Why does Khalidi identify Gaza as a central arena of the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
The uprising spread throughout the occupied territories, but Gaza was where it ignited and remained especially difficult for Israel to control. The chapter identifies Jabalya refugee camp as the setting of the triggering incident and describes local networks that sustained resistance. Gaza also illustrates the limitations of policing a population through heavily armed patrols in crowded urban settings. Khalidi recounts seeing soldiers move nervously through Gaza City, surrounded by a population that rejected the occupation. The point is not that Gaza alone constituted the movement, but that it was a key centre of its intensity and organisation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter’s descriptions of Gaza and the West Bank, noting shared grievances and differences in local conditions."
Key point:
- Gaza was the uprising’s initial flashpoint and a particularly difficult area to control.
- The First Intifada was nevertheless a territory-wide movement.
10. Q: What conditions under occupation contributed to the outbreak of the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes two decades of military occupation, settlement expansion, restrictions on political expression, and suppression of Palestinian national activity. Displaying the Palestinian flag, organising trade unions, supporting the PLO, or publicly protesting could lead to fines, beatings, imprisonment, or deportation. The chapter also discusses administrative detention, collective punishment, house demolitions, and removal of elected mayors. Although incomes rose for some Palestinians through employment in Israel, this did not remove political subordination or denial of national rights. A new generation had grown up knowing only occupation and became increasingly willing to challenge it. The uprising therefore reflected a cumulative crisis rather than a sudden reaction to one event.
Study instruction:
- "Build a cause-and-effect chart linking political restrictions, settlement activity, repression, and generational change to the uprising."
Key point:
- Economic change did not eliminate political grievances or denial of self-determination.
- The uprising followed years of accumulating restrictions and repression.
11. Q: What was the significance of the Israeli government’s “iron fist” response?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Defense Minister Yitzhak Rabin’s January 1988 policy of using force and beatings to suppress the uprising. Khalidi recounts severe beatings and the breaking of demonstrators’ limbs, and argues that the response was disproportionate and counterproductive. Images of armed soldiers confronting young demonstrators became widely televised and damaged Israel’s public image, particularly in the United States. A careful reader should distinguish the author’s account, contemporary reporting, and the dispute over the precise wording of Rabin’s orders: the cited biography by Itamar Rabinovich reportedly questions the accuracy of the “break bones” quotation while recognising Rabin’s use-of-force policy.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter’s description with contemporary reporting and the qualification in Rabinovich’s biography."
Key point:
- Repression did not end the uprising and generated international scrutiny.
- Specific quotations should be checked against competing historical accounts.
Source links:- New York Times: Talk with Rabin — Roots of the Conflict
12. Q: How should the casualty figures cited for the First Intifada period be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites B’Tselem figures stating that from the beginning of the First Intifada through the end of 1996, Israeli troops and armed settlers killed 1,422 Palestinians, including 294 minors aged sixteen and under, while Palestinians killed 175 Israelis, 86 of whom were security personnel. Khalidi describes the ratio as approximately eight to one. These figures extend beyond the uprising’s main 1987–1993 phase and include deaths in the occupied territories and inside Israel. They should not be presented as totals for 1987–1993 alone. Responsible use of statistics states the period, geographic scope, source, and categories counted.
Study instruction:
- "Record the time period, geographic coverage, and population categories before quoting any casualty statistic."
Key point:
- The cited figures run from 1987 through the end of 1996, not only through 1993.
- Statistics require clear definitions and scope to avoid misleading comparisons.
Source links:- B’Tselem: First Intifada casualty tables
13. Q: How did television coverage affect international perceptions of the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
Television repeatedly showed young Palestinian demonstrators confronting heavily armed Israeli troops and military vehicles. Khalidi argues that these images challenged the familiar portrayal of Israel as a perpetual victim and exposed the asymmetry between an occupying military power and a largely unarmed population. The visual contrast became a public-relations problem for Israel, whose international standing depended partly on favourable American public opinion. The chapter contrasts this sustained coverage with the shorter period of televised bombing during the 1982 Lebanon war. Media images did not determine every audience’s view, but they made the conditions of occupation more visible and helped activists communicate beyond the territories.
Study instruction:
- "Analyse how visual framing, duration of coverage, and the relative power of the parties influence public understanding of conflict."
Key point:
- Media visibility helped make the occupation a sustained international issue.
- Images should be assessed alongside documentary evidence and wider context.
Source links:- New York Times: Talk with Rabin — Roots of the Conflict
14. Q: How does Khalidi compare the First Intifada with the revolt of 1936–1939?
A: Show answer
Khalidi sees both as major popular uprisings with broad support, but argues that the First Intifada had a more unified leadership and a strategic approach that avoided worsening internal Palestinian divisions. He emphasises its largely unarmed and nonviolent character, in contrast with the earlier revolt’s armed dimension and eventual suppression by a large British force supported by Zionist auxiliaries. The comparison highlights how organisational structure, methods of resistance, and international context can shape a movement’s trajectory. It does not mean that the two episodes were identical or that the First Intifada had no internal disagreements or violent incidents.
Study instruction:
- "Compare leadership, social participation, tactics, external power, and political consequences in the two uprisings."
Key point:
- Both uprisings reflected broad opposition to imposed political arrangements.
- Khalidi identifies greater unity and predominantly nonviolent tactics as distinguishing features of the First Intifada.
15. Q: Why did the PLO leadership in Tunis seek to take control of the grassroots uprising?
A: Show answer
The PLO was widely regarded by Palestinians under occupation as their legitimate national representative, so its involvement initially had a natural basis. However, Khalidi argues that the leadership in Tunis was also concerned about losing influence to a locally rooted movement whose leaders were gaining credibility and international attention. The PLO issued directives and increasingly managed the uprising from a distance, sometimes disregarding the experience and preferences of people living under occupation. This created tension between an external political leadership and the local activists who had initiated and sustained resistance. The chapter treats this as a strategic weakness: legitimacy does not remove the need to understand local conditions or share decision-making with those directly affected.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the advantages and risks of an exiled leadership directing a movement whose principal participants live under occupation."
Key point:
- The PLO’s representative role coexisted with tensions over control and local knowledge.
- Remote management can weaken a movement when it disregards grassroots experience.
16. Q: Why was Abu Jihad’s assassination in April 1988 important to the PLO’s handling of the uprising?
A: Show answer
Abu Jihad was Arafat’s close lieutenant and had long overseen the PLO’s work concerning the occupied territories. Khalidi portrays him as unusually knowledgeable about conditions inside Palestine and Israeli society, and as a figure able to connect the external leadership with local developments. His assassination, about four months after the uprising began, removed an experienced intermediary when the PLO was struggling to understand and influence the movement. The chapter argues that his loss made it harder to replace informed engagement with remote directives. It places the killing within a broader pattern of targeting Palestinian organisers, while noting that the effectiveness and consequences of such operations were contested.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why accurate local intelligence and trusted intermediaries matter during a fast-moving popular uprising."
Key point:
- Abu Jihad’s role included knowledge of the occupied territories and links to local politics.
- Removing a key intermediary can deepen strategic and information gaps.
17. Q: How did the Fatah mutiny and Syrian interference affect Palestinian political unity?
A: Show answer
After the 1982 war, Fatah faced a serious mutiny among cadres in Lebanon and Syria led by senior commanders Abu Musa and Abu Khalid al-‘Amleh. Khalidi describes the revolt as Syrian-sponsored and as one of the most serious internal challenges to Fatah’s leadership. It intensified Arafat’s concern about rivals and Arab governments attempting to shape or divide the Palestinian movement. This background helps explain why the PLO leadership was sensitive to alternative centres of authority during the First Intifada. The chapter’s point is that external pressure and internal rivalry interacted: a leadership preoccupied with protecting its position could become less receptive to independent local initiative.
Study instruction:
- "Map the relationship among Fatah, the Syrian government, dissident commanders, and the PLO leadership after 1982."
Key point:
- Regional rivalries affected the internal cohesion of Palestinian organisations.
