- Fear of rival leadership can distort strategy and relations with grassroots movements.
18. Q: What was the significance of Hamas’s emergence during the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that Hamas was founded in 1987 and was becoming a formidable competitor to the PLO as the uprising developed. Khalidi argues that the PLO leadership’s concern about being superseded contributed to its jealousy of locally influential leaders and its desire to retain control. He also refers to claims that Israel initially supported the development of an Islamist rival as a way to weaken the PLO; this is a contested historical claim and should be attributed to the author and sources he cites rather than stated without qualification. The wider lesson is that political competition can encourage established organisations to prioritise control over cooperation.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish Hamas’s documented emergence from the chapter’s interpretation of early Israeli policy toward Islamist organisations."
Key point:
- The rise of a new political actor changed the competitive environment for the PLO.
- Claims about state support and intent require careful attribution and corroboration.
19. Q: What strategic weakness did Khalidi identify in the PLO’s understanding of the United States?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the PLO leadership placed too much confidence in winning recognition from the US government and too little effort into understanding American public opinion, political institutions, and networks shaping policy. Senior figures made limited use of opportunities around the United Nations and did not undertake the sustained public-diplomacy work that Israeli representatives pursued. The leadership assumed official recognition would lead Washington to act as a fair intermediary. In Khalidi’s assessment, this underestimated the depth of US commitments to Israel and the importance of domestic political influences. Diplomatic access to a powerful government is not the same as influence over its policy or an assurance of impartial mediation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare government-to-government lobbying with public diplomacy, coalition building, and engagement with civil society."
Key point:
- Recognition by a powerful state does not guarantee impartial mediation.
- A negotiating strategy needs a realistic understanding of the other side’s institutions and incentives.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Foreign Relations of the United States, 1969–1976, Volume XXVI
20. Q: What changed in the Palestinian Declaration of Independence adopted on 15 November 1988?
A: Show answer
The Palestine National Council adopted a Declaration of Independence in Algiers that, as described in the chapter, formally accepted partition, a two-state solution, and peaceful resolution of the conflict. Khalidi identifies the document as a major step in an evolution away from a claim to all of Palestine and toward acceptance of a Palestinian state alongside Israel. It was drafted largely by Mahmoud Darwish with assistance from Edward Said and Shafiq al-Hout. Its importance lay in clarifying the PLO’s diplomatic position and creating a basis for further engagement, although it did not itself produce negotiations or secure reciprocal recognition from Israel.
Study instruction:
- "Summarise the declaration’s political shift and distinguish formal policy change from the practical response of other governments."
Key point:
- The declaration formalised acceptance of partition and a two-state framework.
- A major diplomatic shift does not automatically produce reciprocal concessions.
21. Q: What did Arafat’s statement of 14 December 1988 commit the PLO to?
A: Show answer
In the statement described in the chapter, Arafat accepted US conditions for bilateral dialogue: acceptance of Security Council Resolutions 242 and 338, recognition of Israel’s right to exist in peace and security, and renunciation of terrorism. This secured the long-sought opening of a US-PLO dialogue. Khalidi argues, however, that the diplomatic breakthrough did not persuade Israel to negotiate directly with the PLO and did not immediately produce peace talks. The episode illustrates the difference between satisfying one government’s entry conditions and changing the policy of the other party. It also raises a strategic question about what leverage the PLO retained after making concessions.
Study instruction:
- "List each commitment in the statement and trace what diplomatic result followed and what did not follow."
Key point:
- The statement opened a US-PLO dialogue.
- US recognition did not itself compel Israel to accept the PLO as a negotiating partner.
Source links:- Israel Ministry of Foreign Affairs: Arafat statement, 14 December 1988
22. Q: How did Eqbal Ahmad’s critique inform the chapter’s discussion of armed struggle?
A: Show answer
Khalidi recounts advice from the Pakistani intellectual Eqbal Ahmad, who questioned whether the PLO’s particular form of armed struggle was strategically effective against Israel. Ahmad argued, as Khalidi remembers it, that attacks could reinforce Israeli perceptions of victimhood, strengthen social unity around militant politics, and attract external support rather than weaken the opponent as the Algerian struggle had weakened French colonial support. Ahmad favoured organised, creative, nonviolent political struggle in occupied Palestine. Khalidi notes that he has been unable to locate the original memorandum and relies partly on his recollection and Ahmad’s later writings. The account is therefore an attributed strategic critique, not a fully reproduced primary document.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between moral, legal, and strategic arguments about armed struggle, and note the evidentiary limitation identified by the author."
Key point:
- A tactic’s effectiveness depends on the political context and the adversary it confronts.
- The chapter explicitly qualifies the documentary basis for Ahmad’s advice.
23. Q: Why does Khalidi describe the First Intifada as a source of moral and political capital?
A: Show answer
The uprising made the conditions of occupation more visible, demonstrated the capacity of Palestinians under occupation to organise themselves, and improved international perceptions of the Palestinian cause. Khalidi argues that broad participation and predominantly unarmed tactics gave it a political impact that earlier PLO armed operations had not achieved. It created an opportunity to shape public debate and press for negotiations from a position of greater legitimacy. The author’s criticism is that the PLO did not adequately preserve or use this leverage. When the uprising waned and the leadership pursued negotiations without maintaining sufficient pressure, the movement’s political advantage diminished.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the sources of leverage created by a popular movement and explain how a negotiating party can preserve them during talks."
Key point:
- The uprising generated international attention and political legitimacy.
- Leverage can be lost if negotiations proceed without a strategy for sustaining it.
24. Q: What limitations does Khalidi identify in the PLO’s reliance on Security Council Resolution 242?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Resolution 242 did not explicitly address the Palestine question, identify Palestinians as a people with national rights, or provide for refugee return in the way Palestinian leaders sought. He also highlights the wording concerning withdrawal from territories occupied in 1967, which he interprets as leaving room for Israel to retain or adjust territory rather than requiring withdrawal from all the territories. In his view, accepting Resolution 242 as the basis for negotiations placed the PLO within a framework that did not directly recognise core Palestinian claims. This is the author’s legal and political interpretation; readers should distinguish it from the resolution’s text and from other legal analyses.
Study instruction:
- "Read Resolution 242 alongside the chapter’s critique and identify which issues are expressly addressed and which are omitted."
Key point:
- Khalidi considers Resolution 242 an inadequate framework for Palestinian national claims.
- Interpretations of resolution language should be checked against the primary text and legal analysis.
25. Q: How did the 1975 US commitment to Israel shape the negotiating environment described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Khalidi refers to a 1975 commitment made by President Gerald Ford to Israeli Prime Minister Yitzhak Rabin, and to related diplomatic records, as evidence that later US administrations were constrained in presenting proposals opposed by Israel. He argues that the PLO leadership did not fully understand the significance of these commitments when it pursued Washington’s recognition and later negotiations. The chapter presents this as a structural obstacle to the idea of the United States as an independent honest broker. The claim should be examined through the documentary record, especially the Foreign Relations of the United States volume cited in the notes, rather than inferred only from later outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Locate the cited Ford-Rabin correspondence in the official record and summarise the commitments without extending them beyond their wording."
Key point:
- The chapter treats prior US commitments as a constraint on later mediation.
- Primary diplomatic documents are essential when assessing claims about state commitments.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Foreign Relations of the United States, 1969–1976, Volume XXVI
26. Q: Why does the chapter treat the Camp David Accords and the 1979 Egyptian-Israeli treaty as important background to Madrid?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Camp David framework restricted the Palestinian issue to forms of autonomy rather than sovereignty and helped establish a negotiating architecture in which final questions could be deferred. The 1979 treaty removed Egypt from the immediate Arab-Israeli military confrontation while leaving the Palestinian question unresolved. In the chapter’s interpretation, these arrangements influenced the terms later applied at Madrid: limited self-government could be discussed, while statehood, refugees, Jerusalem, settlements, land, and water were postponed. The author sees this as a precedent for an interim process that could continue without resolving the underlying dispute.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how the treatment of Palestinian autonomy in Camp David reappears in the agenda and sequencing of Madrid and Oslo."
Key point:
- The chapter presents Camp David as a precedent for deferring Palestinian sovereignty.
- Negotiating structures shape outcomes by deciding which subjects are admissible and when.
27. Q: What was the PLO’s strategic miscalculation during the 1990–1991 Gulf crisis?
A: Show answer
After Iraq invaded Kuwait in August 1990, Arafat attempted to maintain a neutral or mediating position but was widely perceived as leaning toward Iraq. Khalidi attributes the decision partly to the PLO’s dependence on Iraqi political, military, and financial support and to Arafat’s hostility toward Syria. He argues that the leadership overestimated Iraq’s ability to withstand the US-led coalition and underestimated the consequences for Palestinians living in Kuwait and for the PLO’s relations with Gulf governments. The episode demonstrates how a liberation movement can become vulnerable when diplomatic choices are constrained by dependence on a regional patron and inaccurate geopolitical assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a decision analysis identifying the PLO’s dependencies, assumptions about Iraq, alternative options, and likely consequences."
Key point:
- The PLO’s position damaged relations with important Gulf states.
- Dependence on a patron can narrow strategic autonomy and increase exposure to that patron’s decisions.
28. Q: Why did Abu Iyad oppose Arafat’s position on the Gulf War?
A: Show answer
Abu Iyad, the PLO’s intelligence chief, understood that appearing to support Iraq would jeopardise the organisation’s standing and the Palestinian community in Kuwait. Khalidi describes that community as several hundred thousand people with important social and financial links to the PLO. Abu Iyad argued that failing to oppose Saddam Hussein’s invasion would endanger those Palestinians and weaken the organisation’s regional support. His position was grounded in geopolitical judgement and concern for a population whose safety and livelihoods could be affected by the PLO’s choices. The chapter reports that Abu Iyad was assassinated in Tunis in January 1991 by a gunman linked to the Abu Nidal network; responsibility and motive should be attributed to the account and sources cited.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the short-term advantages of Iraqi patronage with the longer-term risks to Palestinians in Kuwait and the PLO’s regional relationships."
Key point:
- Abu Iyad warned about the human and diplomatic consequences of the PLO’s Iraq policy.
- Strategic decisions should account for the communities that bear their costs.
29. Q: What were the consequences of the Gulf War for the PLO and Palestinians in Kuwait?
A: Show answer
After Kuwait was liberated, hundreds of thousands of Palestinians were uprooted, and Gulf states halted financial support to the PLO. The organisation became isolated in many Arab capitals and lost important sources of political and economic backing. Khalidi describes this as one of the PLO’s most severe periods of diplomatic weakness. The crisis coincided with the end of the Soviet Union and the emergence of the United States as the dominant international sponsor of a proposed peace process. The PLO’s reduced regional support and financial vulnerability made it more eager to obtain a place in negotiations, but also weakened its ability to resist unfavourable terms.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a regional conflict can affect a political movement through displacement, loss of funding, and reduced diplomatic support."
Key point:
- The Gulf crisis weakened the PLO’s regional position and financial base.
- The resulting vulnerability influenced the context in which the PLO entered Madrid.
30. Q: Why was the Madrid Peace Conference convened in October 1991?
A: Show answer
The Madrid conference was organised by the United States and the Soviet Union after the Gulf War and at the end of the Cold War. The Bush administration sought to use its increased influence to restart Arab-Israeli negotiations. James Baker played a central role in bringing the parties together and pressing for Palestinian participation despite opposition from Israeli Prime Minister Yitzhak Shamir. The conference opened bilateral talks involving Syria, Lebanon, and a joint Jordanian-Palestinian delegation. It created a negotiating forum, but the agenda and rules of participation already contained significant restrictions for the Palestinians.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a chronology linking the Gulf War, the Soviet Union’s collapse, Baker’s diplomacy, and the opening of Madrid."
Key point:
- Madrid created an opening for renewed Arab-Israeli negotiations.
- Rules of participation and agenda shaped the Palestinian position from the outset.
Source links:- Brookings: U.S.-Soviet invitation to Madrid conference, 18 October 1991
31. Q: Why did the Palestinian delegation attend Madrid through a joint Jordanian-Palestinian arrangement?
A: Show answer
Israel opposed direct participation by the PLO and sought to restrict who could represent the Palestinians. The resulting delegation was initially attached to Jordan, and Israel objected to representatives connected with the PLO, residents of Jerusalem, and members of the diaspora. Some excluded figures and advisers were eventually permitted to join after US intervention, but were barred from formal talks with Israel. Khalidi presents these arrangements as an example of how Israel influenced the identity and configuration of the Palestinian negotiating team. The restrictions were not merely technical: they affected political representation and the ability of Palestinians to speak independently about their future.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which categories of Palestinian representatives were initially excluded and explain the political consequences of limiting representation."
Key point:
- The delegation’s format reflected Israeli objections to independent PLO participation.
- Rules about who may negotiate can affect legitimacy and substance.
Source links:- Brookings: U.S.-Soviet invitation to Madrid conference, 18 October 1991
32. Q: Which Palestinian issues were excluded or postponed in the Madrid and Washington negotiations?
A: Show answer
According to Khalidi, the Palestinian agenda was restricted to limited self-rule or interim self-government. Core questions of self-determination, sovereignty, refugee return, ending occupation and settlement activity, Jerusalem, the future of settlements, and control of land and water were excluded from immediate negotiations and deferred to later final-status talks. Those talks were expected to conclude within a few years but were never completed on the original timetable. The chapter argues that postponement allowed the existing power holder to continue changing conditions on the ground while Palestinians were prevented from negotiating the issues most important to their future. Agenda design therefore became a substantive source of power.
Study instruction:
- "List the issues admitted to the interim agenda and those deferred to final-status negotiations."
Key point:
- The Palestinian track focused on limited self-government while core sovereignty questions were postponed.
- Deferral can become a lasting status quo when no effective deadline or enforcement exists.
Source links:- Brookings: Letter of assurances to the Palestinian delegation
33. Q: What assurances did the United States give the Palestinians before Madrid, and why did they matter?
A: Show answer
The US letter of assurances stated that parties should avoid unilateral actions that would worsen local tensions or pre-empt issues reserved for negotiation. The United States also described itself as an honest broker seeking a comprehensive resolution. Khalidi argues that these assurances were not enforced when Israel continued settlement expansion and imposed restrictions on Palestinian access to Jerusalem and movement. The distinction between a written assurance and an effective guarantee is central: a commitment has limited practical value if the sponsor does not monitor compliance or apply consequences when it is breached. The chapter uses this episode to question the credibility of the US role as mediator.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the wording of the letter with the unilateral actions described in the chapter and assess what enforcement mechanisms were available."
Key point:
- The US gave written assurances against unilateral steps that could pre-empt negotiations.
- Failure to enforce those assurances undermined Palestinian confidence.
Source links:- Brookings: Letter of assurances to the Palestinian delegation
34. Q: Why does Khalidi question the idea that the United States acted as an honest broker?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that US policy was constrained by long-standing commitments to Israel and by officials who treated Israel’s stated positions as the limit of what could be negotiated. He cites the 1975 Ford-Rabin commitment, the failure to prevent settlement expansion during talks, and the tendency of some US negotiators to coordinate closely with Israeli counterparts. He also recounts Aaron David Miller’s later description of his own stance and that of colleagues as acting as “Israel’s lawyer.” The chapter does not treat every American official as identical: Baker is described as more willing to press Israel than some subordinates. The issue is whether the mediator had the independence and willingness to enforce a balanced process.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an honest broker using independence, consistency, enforcement, and the ability to challenge both parties."
Key point:
- A mediator’s declared role must be assessed against actual conduct and constraints.
- Khalidi distinguishes Baker’s approach from that of other US officials.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Foreign Relations of the United States, 1969–1976, Volume XXVI
35. Q: How did James Baker differ from other US officials in the Madrid process?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Baker as politically skilled, persistent, and more willing than many subordinates to confront Israeli obstruction. Baker pressed for Palestinian participation despite Shamir’s opposition and recognised that progress required pressure on Israel. He also understood that the post-Cold War United States had an interest in a comprehensive settlement. However, Baker did not prevent settlement activity or restrictions on Palestinian access to Jerusalem while talks continued, and his departure from the State Department in 1992 reduced the influence of his approach. The chapter presents Baker as a comparatively forceful negotiator, not as someone who overcame the structural limitations of the process.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Baker’s reported interventions with the actions he did not prevent."
Key point:
- Baker was more prepared to challenge Israeli obstruction than some US officials.
- Individual negotiating skill does not necessarily overcome structural policy constraints.
Source links:- Newsweek: “When You’re Serious, Call Us”
- Washington Post: Baker bars Israeli loan aid unless settlements are halted
36. Q: What was the significance of the US decision to withhold loan guarantees sought by Israel in 1992?
A: Show answer
The Bush administration withheld ten billion dollars in loan guarantees that Israel sought in connection with the resettlement of Russian Jews, linking the issue to settlement activity. Khalidi describes this as evidence that Baker and Bush were willing to use leverage against the Shamir government. However, the pressure had little effect on Israeli policy and did not halt settlement activity. The episode illustrates both the possibility and the limits of external pressure: a mediator may possess financial or diplomatic tools, but their effectiveness depends on political will, the scale of leverage, and the target government’s willingness to change course.
Study instruction:
- "Assess the loan-guarantee episode as a case study in conditional assistance and the limits of diplomatic leverage."
Key point:
- The Bush administration used financial leverage in relation to settlements.
- The measure did not produce the policy change the Palestinians sought.
Source links:- Washington Post: Baker bars Israeli loan aid unless settlements are halted
37. Q: How did the change from the Bush to the Clinton administration affect the negotiating environment?
A: Show answer
After Baker left office, Khalidi argues that the officials managing the process lacked his political stature, negotiating approach, and willingness to confront Israel. The Clinton administration inherited many of the same personnel, including figures whom Khalidi describes as strongly aligned with Israeli positions. The chapter presents the professionalisation of the “peace process” as a shift in which officials’ careers became closely tied to continued negotiations, even when the process did not resolve central issues. This does not mean every official had identical motives. The author’s point is that personnel, assumptions, and institutional incentives can shape how a mediation process defines the limits of the possible.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Baker’s approach with the chapter’s account of officials who dominated the process after 1992."
Key point:
- Personnel and institutional incentives can change the substance of diplomacy.
- The chapter argues that the later US team was less willing to challenge Israeli positions.
38. Q: How did Rabin’s approach to negotiations with Syria differ from his approach to the Palestinians?
A: Show answer
Khalidi portrays Rabin as more flexible on the Syrian track, where he appointed Itamar Rabinovich and seriously explored a possible agreement involving the Golan Heights. On the Palestinian track, Rabin retained a narrow approach centred on limited self-rule rather than sovereignty or an independent state. Although there were adjustments on elections and territorial contiguity, the core position remained restrictive. The contrast suggests that Rabin assessed different tracks according to strategic priorities and perceived feasibility. Khalidi argues that an agreement with Syria could also have weakened the Palestinians’ bargaining position by leaving them more isolated.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the negotiating objectives, representatives, and substantive issues on the Syrian and Palestinian tracks."
Key point:
- Rabin showed greater flexibility on Syria than on Palestinian sovereignty.
- The choice of negotiating track affected the wider balance of power.
39. Q: What was the Palestinian Interim Self-Governing Authority proposal, known as PISGA?
A: Show answer
PISGA was a proposal developed by the Palestinian delegation in Washington in 1992 for an elected interim authority whose mandate would come from Palestinians in the West Bank, Gaza, Jerusalem, and specified displaced or deported groups. It envisaged transferring powers from the Israeli military government and Civil Administration to the elected authority and withdrawing those Israeli bodies. The proposal sought jurisdiction over people and territory, including land, air, and water, while not claiming full sovereignty or independent security control at the interim stage. It also proposed a settlement freeze and redeployment of Israeli forces. Khalidi presents it as an effort to make interim self-government a meaningful transition rather than a cosmetic administrative arrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Review the PISGA outline and identify its electorate, jurisdiction, transition measures, and limitations."
Key point:
- PISGA linked elected self-government to a transfer of meaningful jurisdiction.
- The proposal distinguished jurisdiction from full sovereignty and security control.
Source links:- Institute for Palestine Studies: PISGA outline, January 1992
40. Q: Why did the PISGA proposal fail to gain acceptance?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies several obstacles. The proposal challenged the Israeli position that Palestinians could receive limited autonomy without control over the land and resources that determine political sovereignty. The Israeli negotiating team rejected the breadth of jurisdiction contemplated by PISGA. The PLO leadership in Tunis also did not promote the proposal vigorously, perhaps because it expected rejection or preferred a quicker agreement. The chapter further describes tension between the external leadership and experienced Palestinian negotiators from the occupied territories. PISGA’s failure therefore reflected both the opposing party’s substantive limits and weaknesses in the Palestinian side’s strategy, coordination, and public advocacy.
Study instruction:
- "Separate external reasons for rejection from internal weaknesses in the proposal’s sponsorship and negotiating strategy."
Key point:
- PISGA challenged the limits of autonomy acceptable to the Israeli government.
- A technically developed proposal also needs sustained political ownership and advocacy.
Source links:- Institute for Palestine Studies: PISGA outline, January 1992
41. Q: How did tensions between the PLO in Tunis and the Palestinian delegation in Washington affect negotiations?
A: Show answer
The delegation included experienced leaders from the occupied territories who understood local conditions and had developed detailed legal and diplomatic positions. The PLO leadership in Tunis retained control over major decisions but, in Khalidi’s account, did not always appreciate the delegation’s expertise or support its proposals. This created disputes over strategy and authority. Israel was aware of these tensions and, according to the chapter, used changes in participation rules to encourage divisions among Palestinian leaders. The episode demonstrates the importance of a clear mandate, trust between negotiators and political leadership, and an agreed process for decisions. Internal fragmentation can weaken a delegation even when its members possess strong technical knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "Map the roles of the Tunis leadership, Washington delegation, local leaders, and advisers; identify where authority and expertise were misaligned."
Key point:
- A negotiating team needs technical expertise and political backing.
- Internal divisions can be exploited by the other party and reduce bargaining strength.
42. Q: What was the separate security channel between the PLO and Israel in 1993?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an undisclosed channel through which representatives of the PLO and Israel explored security arrangements, including possible entry of PLO cadres and officers of the Palestine Liberation Army into the occupied territories. Khalidi recounts that he and Hanan Ashrawi briefed US diplomats in June 1993 after being instructed by Arafat to describe the emerging understanding. He emphasises that the arrangement was an informal understanding rather than a fully documented formal agreement. This channel was separate from the Washington talks and the better-known Oslo negotiations. Its significance was that security cooperation was being addressed directly and secretly before the public signing of the Declaration of Principles.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the Washington talks, separate security channel, and Oslo track; note what the chapter says was informal and what was formally signed."
Key point:
- Multiple negotiating channels operated simultaneously in 1993.
- The chapter describes the security understanding as significant but not fully formalised.
43. Q: What did the 1993 mutual-recognition exchange between Israel and the PLO accomplish, and what did it not accomplish?
A: Show answer
The exchange led Israel to recognise the PLO as representative of the Palestinian people and the PLO to recognise the state of Israel. It enabled direct negotiations and the return of PLO leaders and cadres to the occupied territories. Khalidi stresses that the exchange was not symmetrical: Israel did not recognise a Palestinian state or commit itself to establishing one, while the PLO recognised an existing state. Recognition opened a diplomatic relationship and changed the formal status of the PLO, yet it did not resolve sovereignty, borders, refugees, Jerusalem, settlements, or the end of occupation. The chapter treats this imbalance as a central weakness of the Oslo framework.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the precise objects of recognition on each side and list the substantive issues that remained unresolved."
Key point:
- Israel recognised the PLO, while the PLO recognised Israel as a state.
- Mutual recognition did not amount to mutual recognition of statehood or equal sovereignty.
44. Q: Why does Khalidi criticise the Palestinian negotiating team’s lack of legal and technical expertise at Oslo?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Palestinian envoys sent to Oslo lacked the legal, linguistic, and negotiating expertise needed to understand the implications of the draft agreements. The Israeli team included experienced specialists and lawyers who could shape technical language in ways that preserved Israeli control. The Palestinian negotiators had not adequately studied the previous Washington rounds, where the delegation had already confronted the meaning of autonomy and questions of jurisdiction over land and people. The chapter’s criticism is not simply that the Palestinians made a compromise; it is that they failed to understand how detailed provisions would determine the distribution of powers in practice. Technical review is part of substantive strategy, not a minor administrative task.
Study instruction:
- "Identify agreement clauses allocating authority over land, resources, borders, security, and population; review their practical consequences."
Key point:
- Negotiating expertise matters because technical wording allocates real power.
- A delegation should use prior experience and specialist review before accepting an agreement.
45. Q: What is the difference between limited autonomy and sovereignty in the chapter’s discussion of Oslo?
A: Show answer
Limited autonomy allows an authority to administer specified matters within a framework in which another power retains ultimate control over territory, borders, resources, security, or movement. Sovereignty involves independent authority over territory and population, subject to applicable international law and obligations. Khalidi argues that Oslo granted Palestinian institutions administrative responsibilities without transferring the powers necessary for independent statehood. In his account, Israel retained decisive powers over land, water, borders, settlements, population registers, and external movement. The distinction matters because creating local institutions or elected bodies does not by itself demonstrate that a people has obtained sovereign control.
Study instruction:
- "Compare administrative responsibility, jurisdiction, security authority, territorial control, and sovereignty under the arrangements described."
Key point:
- Self-administration is not equivalent to sovereign control.
- The distribution of powers matters more than the name assigned to an institution.
46. Q: Why does Khalidi argue that Oslo resembled the earlier autonomy model proposed at Camp David?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Oslo reproduced the central limitation of the Begin-era autonomy concept: people could receive a form of self-administration while Israel retained decisive control over land and other attributes of sovereignty. The Palestinian delegation in Washington had rejected that model and developed PISGA as an alternative with broader jurisdiction. In the author’s view, the Oslo negotiators accepted a restricted arrangement without adequately recognising that it left the underlying structure of occupation in place. The comparison is about the substance of the powers transferred, not simply terminology. A framework labelled self-government may preserve external control if authority over territory and resources remains elsewhere.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the autonomy concept at Camp David, the PISGA proposal, and Oslo by listing who controls people, land, resources, and security."
Key point:
- Khalidi sees Oslo as accepting a limited autonomy model the Washington delegation had resisted.
- Assess agreements by their actual allocation of powers rather than their labels.
47. Q: How did Arafat’s return to Gaza in July 1994 shape the chapter’s assessment of Oslo?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Arafat’s return after decades in exile as emotionally and politically significant, but argues that the leadership’s euphoria obscured the restrictions that remained. He recounts raising concerns about Jerusalem’s isolation, land seizure, home demolitions, and restrictions on residency and movement, only to find Arafat and Abu Mazin optimistic that later negotiations would resolve them. The episode illustrates a gap between the symbolic achievement of returning to Palestine and the practical distribution of power under the interim arrangements. The author’s concern is that the leadership treated the return and creation of Palestinian institutions as signs of liberation before unresolved issues had been settled.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the symbolic importance of political return from the legal and practical powers transferred by an agreement."
Key point:
- The return from exile was significant but did not end occupation.
- Political optimism should be tested against conditions experienced by the wider population.
48. Q: What were the main features of the Oslo II division of the West Bank and Gaza into Areas A, B, and C?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Oslo II, agreed in 1995, as dividing the territories into three administrative and security categories. Area A comprised about 18 percent and was assigned Palestinian administrative and security control. Area B comprised about 22 percent and was assigned Palestinian administrative control while Israel retained security control. Area C comprised more than 60 percent and remained under complete direct Israeli control in the chapter’s account. Khalidi states that Areas A and B together contained about 87 percent of the Palestinian population, while Area C included all but one of the Jewish settlements. These percentages should not be confused with an assertion that the Palestinian Authority had sovereignty over Areas A or B.
Study instruction:
- "Memorise the chapter’s approximate percentages and distinguish administrative authority from security control in each area."
Key point:
- Area A: about 18 percent; Area B: about 22 percent; Area C: over 60 percent.
- Administrative and security powers differed across the three areas.
49. Q: How did Oslo II affect Palestinian movement and the relationship between Jerusalem, the West Bank, and Gaza?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes growing restrictions through checkpoints, walls, permits, and roads from which Palestinian travel was barred. Jerusalem became increasingly separated from the rest of the occupied territories, and residents of the West Bank and Gaza faced greater difficulty entering the city or moving between territories. Travel that had once been relatively straightforward became slow, uncertain, or impossible without Israeli permission. These restrictions affected access to work, education, healthcare, family networks, markets, and political institutions. The chapter argues that territorial fragmentation reinforced the unequal system of control that the Oslo arrangements had not resolved.
Study instruction:
- "Map the movement restrictions described and identify their potential effects on economic, social, and political life."
Key point:
- Checkpoints, barriers, and permits fragmented Palestinian movement.
- Control over mobility can determine whether formal administrative autonomy is meaningful.
50. Q: Why did Khalidi consider the relocation of most PLO personnel to the occupied territories a strategic risk?
A: Show answer
The return of PLO cadres from Tunis and other Arab capitals was understandable after years of exile, but Khalidi argues that the organisation moved much of its apparatus into a territory where Israeli military authority remained in place. In his metaphor, the leadership moved from one constrained environment into another, more vulnerable one. Israel could restrict travel, meetings, and political activity, and could raid Palestinian Authority offices even in areas nominally under Palestinian administration. The author suggests that retaining some organisational capacity outside the territories until the outcome of negotiations became clearer might have preserved greater independence. The strategic issue was loss of institutional freedom before sovereignty had been secured.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the advantages of returning political institutions home against the risks of operating under another authority’s continuing control."
Key point:
- The return of personnel did not remove the underlying military authority.
- Institutional location can affect a movement’s independence during an unresolved transition.
51. Q: How does the chapter distinguish the Palestinian Authority from a sovereign state?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Palestinian Authority had administrative and security responsibilities but lacked sovereignty, independent jurisdiction, and control over key state functions. In his account, Israel retained decisive powers over territory, movement, population registers, and important revenue flows, while the PA operated within permissions and limits imposed by Israel. The chapter criticises descriptions of the relationship as one between two near-equal state authorities because they obscure this unequal distribution of power. This is the author’s political and legal assessment of the Oslo system. A rigorous analysis should examine the specific powers transferred, retained, or restricted rather than infer sovereignty from the existence of institutions.
Study instruction:
- "List the attributes normally associated with sovereignty and compare them with the powers the chapter says the PA could exercise."
Key point:
- The existence of a governing authority does not by itself establish sovereign statehood.
- Assess power through control of territory, borders, resources, security, and public authority.
52. Q: What does Khalidi mean by describing the Palestinian Authority as a “subcontractor” for the occupation?
A: Show answer
Khalidi uses the term to argue that Oslo assigned Palestinian institutions a role in managing resistance and maintaining security conditions that benefited Israel while Israel retained ultimate control. In this interpretation, the PA assumed administrative and policing burdens without obtaining the sovereign powers or freedom of action normally associated with independent government. The chapter presents security coordination as especially consequential because it shifted responsibilities while leaving the occupation and settlement structure intact. The term is a critical political characterisation, not a neutral legal designation. Readers should test it by examining the actual allocation of security responsibilities and decision-making authority.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the author’s metaphor from formal security arrangements and identify who held ultimate authority in the system described."
Key point:
- The author argues that Oslo transferred administrative burdens without transferring sovereignty.
- Critical labels should be assessed against the powers and obligations they describe.
53. Q: How does the chapter explain the relationship between Oslo and the continuation of settlement activity?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the interim framework deferred decisions about settlements and final borders while Israel retained the ability to change conditions on the ground. The United States had given assurances against unilateral actions that could pre-empt negotiations, but the chapter describes ongoing settlement expansion and restrictions on Jerusalem as actions that were not effectively prevented. Because Palestinians were not permitted to negotiate final-status questions during the interim period, changes made during that time could affect the eventual bargaining position. The author’s criticism is that a process intended to lead to a final agreement lacked safeguards against one party altering facts while talks continued.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how settlement expansion during an interim negotiation can change the practical bargaining environment before final-status issues are resolved."
Key point:
- Deferring settlement questions did not stop activity on the ground.
- An interim agreement needs safeguards against unilateral changes that affect the final outcome.
Source links:- Brookings: Letter of assurances to the Palestinian delegation
54. Q: Why does Khalidi argue that the interim stage of the peace process became effectively permanent?
A: Show answer
The agreements anticipated later final-status negotiations on sovereignty, refugees, Jerusalem, settlements, and borders. Those negotiations were expected to be completed within a specified period, but the chapter notes that the timetable was extended and the final process was never concluded as originally envisaged. Meanwhile, interim institutions and restrictions continued to shape daily life. Khalidi argues that the promise of future resolution helped legitimise a temporary arrangement that became prolonged. Transitional provisions can become entrenched when deadlines are not enforceable, one party controls conditions on the ground, and the parties lack a workable mechanism to complete the promised transition.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the original timetable for final-status talks, later extensions, and the institutional consequences of the unresolved interim period."
Key point:
- A temporary framework can become durable when the final stage is repeatedly deferred.
- Deadlines require monitoring, enforcement, and a realistic path to implementation.
Source links:- London Review of Books: Edward Said, “The Morning After”
55. Q: What role did US support play in the Oslo framework, according to Khalidi?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Oslo framework could not have been imposed in its restricted form without American diplomatic, political, and material support. The United States helped define the negotiating route, backed the interim structure, and reinforced the view that no alternative process was available. The chapter also describes American aid, loans, and diplomatic protection as part of a broader relationship with Israel. This is the author’s interpretation of the partnership and should be assessed using diplomatic records and the conduct of successive administrations. A peace agreement is shaped not only by its formal parties but also by the sponsor that designs and supports the negotiating architecture.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the roles of the formal parties and external sponsor, then assess how the sponsor’s commitments affected the range of possible outcomes."
Key point:
- The chapter treats US support as central to the design and maintenance of the Oslo process.
- External sponsors shape negotiations through diplomacy, resources, and process control.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Foreign Relations of the United States, 1969–1976, Volume XXVI
56. Q: How should readers distinguish the chapter’s historical facts from its interpretation of the Oslo agreements?
A: Show answer
A careful reading separates verifiable dates, signed documents, named institutions, and quoted statements from Khalidi’s explanations of motives, power, and consequences. The dates of Madrid and the signing of Oslo can be checked against documentary records, while claims that a policy was designed to preserve colonial control are interpretations requiring analysis of evidence and comparison with other scholarship. The author’s participation in negotiations provides first-hand perspective but does not make every judgement uncontestable. Good historical method values participant testimony while testing it against primary documents, opposing accounts, and the limits of available evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Annotate a passage by marking dates and documents as factual claims, and motives or causal explanations as interpretations requiring evaluation."
Key point:
- Participant testimony is valuable but should be assessed critically.
- Separate the documentary record from the author’s argument about why events occurred.
57. Q: What practical lessons about negotiation strategy emerge from the PLO’s experience between 1988 and 1995?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that negotiators should understand the other party’s institutions and incentives, assess the mediator’s independence, maintain political leverage, ensure legal and technical expertise, align external leadership with local representatives, and avoid interim arrangements without safeguards for the final settlement. It highlights the importance of defining the issues to be negotiated, preventing unilateral changes during talks, and understanding how control over land, resources, movement, and security affects sovereignty. These lessons are derived from Khalidi’s critical account and should be applied analytically rather than treated as universal rules for every negotiation.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a negotiation checklist covering leverage, representation, expertise, agenda control, interim safeguards, and implementation."
Key point:
- Negotiation outcomes depend on process design as well as final wording.
- A party should understand which powers it retains or relinquishes before accepting an interim agreement.
58. Q: How can the First Intifada and the Oslo process be understood as connected but different phases of the Palestinian struggle?
A: Show answer
The First Intifada was a mass movement rooted in the occupied territories, driven by local organisation and broad social participation. Oslo was a diplomatic framework negotiated by political leaderships and external sponsors, with agreements that created limited Palestinian institutions. Khalidi’s central contrast is that the uprising generated political legitimacy and exposed the limits of military rule, while the subsequent negotiations channelled that leverage into an interim system that did not deliver sovereignty. The phases are connected because the uprising helped make negotiations possible, but they differed in leadership, methods, institutional setting, and distribution of power. Understanding the relationship requires examining popular mobilisation and elite diplomacy together.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a timeline from December 1987 to the 1995 Oslo II agreement and identify the transition from popular resistance to formal negotiation."
Key point:
- The uprising helped create the political conditions for negotiations.
- The diplomatic process did not automatically preserve grassroots leverage or objectives.
59. Q: What questions should a researcher ask when evaluating claims about the First Intifada and Oslo negotiations?
A: Show answer
A researcher should ask who produced a source, when it was created, what evidence it contains, what perspective it represents, and which claims it can support. Official statements establish declared positions but may not reveal private motives. Participant accounts illuminate meetings and internal dynamics but may reflect retrospective interpretation. Casualty statistics require clear dates and geographic definitions. Negotiating texts should be read directly to identify what powers were transferred or retained. Khalidi’s chapter is a detailed participant-informed argument, but a balanced study should compare it with official records, contemporary reporting, and scholarship offering different interpretations.
Study instruction:
- "Select three claims from the chapter and identify a primary source, an independent account, and an evidentiary limitation for each."
Key point:
- Source evaluation requires attention to authorship, date, scope, and purpose.
- No single account should substitute for comparison of documents and competing interpretations.
Source links:- U.S. Department of State: Foreign Relations of the United States, 1969–1976, Volume XXVI
60. Q: What are the chapter’s most important conclusions about the relationship between popular resistance and political leadership?
A: Show answer
Khalidi’s account suggests that a broad popular movement can create political opportunities that established leaders may struggle to use effectively. The First Intifada generated local legitimacy, social organisation, and international attention, but the PLO leadership in exile was concerned about control, lacked detailed understanding of conditions inside the territories, and later negotiated under severe regional and diplomatic pressure. The chapter also shows that grassroots leaders and technical advisers can possess expertise that is not fully reflected in central decisions. The conclusion is not that political leadership is unnecessary, but that it must remain accountable to its base, use local knowledge, and protect the movement’s strategic objectives during negotiations.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the sources of legitimacy and expertise held by grassroots organisers, exiled leaders, and professional negotiators."
Key point:
- Popular mobilisation can create leverage that formal leaders must preserve.
- Effective leadership requires local knowledge, internal coordination, and strategic accountability.
61. Q: Why does the chapter describe Oslo as a modification of occupation rather than its termination?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Oslo altered the way the occupied territories were administered but did not transfer the decisive powers required to end occupation. Palestinian institutions received limited responsibilities, while Israel retained control over major aspects of territory, movement, settlements, population registration, and security. The agreements postponed final-status questions and allowed the interim system to continue without the promised resolution. In the author’s view, the result was a reorganisation of the existing power relationship rather than a transition to Palestinian independence. This conclusion is central to the chapter’s title and should be evaluated by examining the actual distribution of powers under Oslo I and Oslo II.
Study instruction:
- "List the powers transferred to Palestinian institutions and those the chapter says remained under Israeli control; use the comparison to assess the author’s conclusion."
Key point:
- The author argues that Oslo changed administration without ending the underlying occupation.
- The practical distribution of authority is the key test of whether a transition to sovereignty occurred.
62. Q: How can the chapter’s arguments be applied to evaluating an interim political agreement in another context?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests a framework for assessing transitional agreements: identify who controls territory and borders; determine who controls security, resources, movement, and population records; check whether elected institutions have meaningful jurisdiction; examine whether final-status issues are deferred; assess safeguards against unilateral changes; and test whether deadlines and enforcement mechanisms are credible. It is also important to understand the mediator’s interests and the relative bargaining power of the parties. This does not mean every interim agreement is inherently defective. It means that labels such as autonomy, self-government, or transition should be tested against the rights, powers, obligations, and implementation mechanisms created by the text.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the framework to a hypothetical agreement and identify provisions needed to prevent indefinite interim rule."
Key point:
- An agreement should be judged by the powers and protections it creates in practice.
- A credible transition requires clear obligations, safeguards, and enforceable implementation.
Rows: 62
Table 6
The Second Intifada, Palestinian Political Division, and the Gaza Wars (2000–2014)
1. Q: What are the central developments examined in Chapter 6, “The Sixth Declaration of War, 2000–2014”?
A: Show answer
The chapter examines the deterioration of conditions after the Oslo Accords, the failure of the 2000 Camp David summit, the outbreak and course of the Second Intifada, the political consequences of suicide bombings, the death of Yasser Arafat, the rise of Mahmoud Abbas, Hamas’s electoral participation and victory in 2006, the Fatah-Hamas split, the blockade of Gaza, and the Israeli assaults on Gaza in 2008–2009, 2012, and 2014. Khalidi also analyses US policy and changes in American public opinion. His central argument is that the Oslo framework did not deliver sovereignty, while subsequent violence and political division further weakened Palestinian prospects. The chapter is a critical historical interpretation, so its factual chronology should be distinguished from its judgments about motives, responsibility, legality, and strategy.
Study instruction:
- "Create a timeline from the post-Oslo years to 2014 and classify each development as political, diplomatic, military, social, or economic."
Key point:
- The chapter links the failure of the Oslo process to later political fragmentation and repeated conflict.
- It distinguishes changes in institutions from changes in the underlying distribution of power.
2. Q: Why did many Palestinians become disappointed with the Oslo Accords after the 1993 signing ceremony?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes an initial wave of optimism that the accords would lead to an end to military occupation, settlement expansion, and ultimately Palestinian statehood. As the interim period continued, many Palestinians saw that these expectations were not being fulfilled. The chapter identifies restrictions on movement, permits, checkpoints, walls, separation of Gaza from the West Bank, settlement growth, and the continued absence of an independent Palestinian state as important sources of disappointment. The author argues that the gap between promises and lived experience became especially damaging because the accords were presented as a path toward peace and prosperity. This helps explain why public confidence in the PLO leadership and the Palestinian Authority declined over time.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the expectations associated with the 1993 agreement with the changes in movement, territory, and political status described in the chapter."
Key point:
- The chapter links popular disillusionment to the gap between Oslo’s promise and subsequent conditions.
- The continuation of interim arrangements weakened confidence in the peace process.
3. Q: How did movement restrictions affect Palestinian economic and social life after Oslo?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a system of permits, checkpoints, walls, fences, restricted roads, and controls on access to Israel, Jerusalem, Gaza, and the West Bank. Khalidi argues that these restrictions affected employment, trade, education, family contact, professional services, and access to markets. He notes that many Palestinians who had previously travelled or worked without the same permit constraints were increasingly confined to particular areas. The consequences were not limited to inconvenience: restricted movement fragmented economic networks and weakened the links between communities. The chapter also contrasts VIP access available to some PA and PLO figures with the restrictions faced by most of the population, using this disparity to illustrate how political and economic privilege could separate leadership experiences from ordinary life.
Study instruction:
- "Map the main movement restrictions described and identify their effects on employment, commerce, education, health care, and family life."
Key point:
- Movement controls affect the operation of an economy as well as individual mobility.
- The chapter emphasizes the unequal experience of ordinary residents and privileged officials.
Source links:- Al Jazeera: Gaza, Isolation and Control
4. Q: What does the chapter mean by Israel’s policy of separation between Gaza, the West Bank, and Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the progressive restriction of movement between Gaza, the West Bank, and East Jerusalem as a policy that divided a connected Palestinian social and economic space. Gaza became increasingly isolated from the West Bank and external markets, while East Jerusalem was separated from its West Bank hinterland by barriers and permit requirements. The chapter argues that this disrupted trade, employment, education, cultural institutions, health services, and political relationships. Separation should therefore be understood as more than a physical barrier: it changes the ability of people and institutions to function as a connected society. The author also notes that restrictions on Gaza began before Hamas took control in 2007, a point supported by the source cited in the chapter.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the development of restrictions on Gaza and Jerusalem and distinguish measures introduced before and after the 2007 Hamas takeover."
Key point:
- The chapter presents separation as a process that fragmented Palestinian economic and social connections.
- Gaza’s isolation did not begin only after Hamas took control.
Source links:- Al Jazeera: Gaza, Isolation and Control
5. Q: What economic consequences of the post-Oslo restrictions does the chapter identify?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes falling economic activity, reduced access to employment in Israel, interruptions to exports and imports, and increasing fragmentation of markets. Khalidi cites figures indicating that Palestinian GDP per capita declined between 2000 and 2004 after remaining broadly stable in the preceding period. He also describes the particular damage to Gaza, where many households depended on work in Israel or on exporting goods. The economic analysis connects political and security arrangements to household income, business viability, and public welfare. The figures should be interpreted using the periods and definitions in the underlying UNCTAD report rather than treated as a general measure of every Palestinian household’s experience.
Study instruction:
- "Review the cited UNCTAD figures and record the period, measure, and limitations before using them in an economic comparison."
Key point:
- Movement and trade restrictions can reduce income and productive activity.
- Economic indicators should be interpreted with attention to their dates and definitions.
6. Q: How did the worsening conditions after Oslo affect Hamas’s political position?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Hamas benefited from growing disappointment with the PLO’s diplomatic strategy and the failure of the Oslo process to deliver the promised statehood. Hamas had opposed the turn toward negotiations and presented itself as a more militant Islamist alternative. As restrictions continued and final-status negotiations failed to materialize, the organization’s criticism of the PLO and the newly formed Palestinian Authority gained greater resonance. The chapter does not attribute Hamas’s growth to one cause alone: it also discusses regional changes, ideological appeal, organizational activity, and factional competition. The central point is that political movements can gain support when established leaders are judged by the outcomes of their strategies rather than by the promises they made.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the political, economic, ideological, and organizational factors that Khalidi associates with Hamas’s growth."
Key point:
- Disappointment with Oslo weakened the standing of the PLO and created opportunities for rivals.
- Hamas’s rise had several interacting causes rather than one explanation.
Source links:- The Intercept: Blowback and the History of Hamas
7. Q: Why was the 2000 Camp David summit poorly positioned to resolve the Israeli-Palestinian conflict?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the summit was convened late in President Bill Clinton’s second term, after the Israeli government had lost its parliamentary majority and Arafat’s popularity had declined. The chapter emphasizes that there had been no prior understandings on the main issues and that Arafat attended under pressure, fearing that he would be blamed for failure if he refused. The author also notes that the summit process was unusually structured, with Barak avoiding substantive direct meetings and communicating a proposal through the Americans. The broader lesson is that a summit cannot substitute for preparation: complex negotiations require agreed procedures, technical work, prior exploration of possible compromises, and a clear understanding of what each party is authorized to accept.
Study instruction:
- "List the political timing, preparation gaps, and procedural features that Khalidi identifies as weaknesses of the summit."
Key point:
- The chapter portrays Camp David as poorly prepared and politically constrained.
- A high-level meeting cannot compensate for unresolved technical and substantive disagreements.
8. Q: Which issues made the Israeli proposal at Camp David unacceptable to the Palestinian side in Khalidi’s account?
A: Show answer
The chapter says the proposal would have left Israel with permanent control of the Jordan Valley, Palestine’s airspace, and West Bank water resources, while annexing areas that would have divided the West Bank into separate blocs. Khalidi also identifies Jerusalem as the deepest point of disagreement, particularly Israel’s demand for exclusive sovereignty over the Haram al-Sharif and most of the Old City. The author argues that these provisions would have left the projected Palestinian entity without meaningful sovereignty or reliable access to the outside world. Because the proposal was not published and has been reconstructed by participants, readers should distinguish the chapter’s description from a fully authenticated public text of the proposal.
Study instruction:
- "Group the disputed issues into territorial control, security, resources, contiguity, access, and Jerusalem, and explain the effect of each."
Key point:
- Khalidi argues that the proposed entity would have lacked core attributes of sovereignty.
- The chapter acknowledges that the proposal was not published as a complete public document.
9. Q: Why did the failure of the Camp David summit strengthen Ariel Sharon and deepen Palestinian political polarization?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Barak’s public presentation of Arafat as an obstructionist helped Sharon, who had consistently opposed the Oslo process and claimed that no agreement was possible. On the Palestinian side, the failed summit reinforced the view that negotiations were not producing a settlement that met even minimum demands for sovereignty. This increased recriminations between political factions and strengthened Hamas’s challenge to Fatah and the PLO’s leadership. The chapter treats the summit as a turning point because it damaged confidence in the diplomatic strategy at the same time that the Palestinian political system was already divided. The resulting polarization made a unified response to the next crisis more difficult.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the political consequences of the summit separately for Israeli party politics and Palestinian factional politics."
Key point:
- The summit’s failure affected domestic political competition on both sides.
- Loss of confidence in negotiations contributed to greater Palestinian polarization in the chapter’s account.
10. Q: What was the significance of Ariel Sharon’s visit to the Haram al-Sharif in September 2000?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Sharon’s visit, accompanied by a large security detail, as the immediate provocation that helped ignite the Second Intifada. The site is known to Muslims as the Haram al-Sharif and to Jews as the Temple Mount, and the chapter explains that it was already a focus of religious and nationalist tensions. The visit followed the failed Camp David summit, continued restrictions on Palestinian access to Jerusalem, and longstanding fears about changes to the status of the area. Khalidi interprets Sharon’s action as politically opportunistic and notes his public declaration that the site would remain in Israeli hands. The visit should be understood as a trigger in an already volatile environment, not as the sole cause of the uprising.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the immediate provocation from the preceding political conditions, including Camp David, Jerusalem restrictions, and earlier disputes over the site."
Key point:
- Sharon’s visit acted as a trigger amid accumulated tension and disappointment.
- The chapter situates the event within a longer history of contestation over Jerusalem.
Source links:- The Guardian: Rioting as Sharon Visits Islamic Holy Site
11. Q: What long-term conditions contributed to the outbreak of the Second Intifada?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several accumulated conditions: disappointment with Oslo, continued settlement growth, restrictions on movement, the failure to complete final-status negotiations, the absence of a credible path to sovereignty, and growing rivalry between Fatah and Hamas. These factors created widespread frustration before Sharon’s visit to the Haram al-Sharif. Khalidi describes the uprising as an eruption of a population that felt betrayed by the peace process and remained under occupation. The distinction between immediate trigger and underlying conditions is essential to historical explanation. The visit provided the spark, but the chapter’s analysis suggests that the political environment had become combustible through years of unresolved issues and deteriorating daily conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Create a causal map showing how the Oslo interim period, movement restrictions, political rivalry, and the Haram visit interacted."
Key point:
- The Second Intifada arose from accumulated political and social conditions as well as an immediate provocation.
- A single event cannot explain the full scale of the uprising.
12. Q: How does the chapter compare the scale of deaths during the First and Second Intifadas?
A: Show answer
Khalidi reports approximately 1,600 deaths during the eight-plus years of the First Intifada, about 90 deaths in the following four years, and approximately 6,600 deaths during the eight years of the Second Intifada. He breaks the latter total into Israeli and Palestinian deaths and notes that many Israeli civilian deaths resulted from suicide bombings, while Palestinian deaths were largely caused by Israeli security forces and settlers. These figures are attributed to B’Tselem and should be checked against the underlying tables, date ranges, and classifications. The comparison is used by the author to illustrate the sharp escalation in violence after 2000, not to suggest that every year or phase had the same pattern of casualties.
Study instruction:
- "Reproduce the figures from the cited tables and note the date ranges, categories, and attribution of responsibility."
Key point:
- The chapter reports a major increase in deaths during the Second Intifada.
- Casualty comparisons require consistent time periods and classifications.
Source links:- B’Tselem: Statistics on Fatalities
13. Q: What role did the Israeli use of live ammunition play in the early phase of the Second Intifada according to the chapter?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the use of live ammunition against demonstrators from the outset was a major factor in the rapid escalation. He cites reporting that Israeli forces fired approximately 1.3 million bullets in the first few days, a figure attributed in the notes to a Haaretz analysis. The chapter says that the scale of casualties helped provoke some Palestinians, including members of the Palestinian Authority security forces, to take up arms. The author also raises the possibility that Israeli forces were prepared for escalation, although that is an interpretation that should be examined against the cited reporting and other evidence. The important analytical point is the interaction between force, protest, retaliation, and the rapid transformation of the uprising.
Study instruction:
- "Read the cited reporting and distinguish the documented ammunition figure from the author’s interpretation of military planning and intent."
Key point:
- The chapter treats early lethal force as a major escalation factor.
- Claims about intent require evidence beyond the sequence of events alone.
Source links:- Haaretz: One Million Bullets
14. Q: How did suicide bombings change the character and geographic reach of the Second Intifada?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes suicide bombings by Hamas, Islamic Jihad, and later Fatah as attacks that brought violence from the occupied territories into civilian spaces inside Israel. Targets included buses, cafés, and shopping centres. Khalidi argues that these attacks increased the death toll and changed how the conflict was perceived internationally. The tactic also produced severe moral and legal concerns because it deliberately or indiscriminately targeted civilians. The chapter discusses the attacks as part of an escalation cycle in which Israeli military force, Palestinian armed responses, and factional competition reinforced one another. A careful account should condemn attacks on civilians without collapsing all Palestinian political activity into the actions of armed groups.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the groups, target types, timing, and political consequences of the attacks described in the chapter."
Key point:
- Suicide bombings extended the violence to civilian targets inside Israel.
- The tactic had serious humanitarian, legal, and strategic consequences.
Source links:- B’Tselem: Statistics on Fatalities
15. Q: How did competition between Hamas and Fatah contribute to the escalation of suicide bombings?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that factional rivalry became one factor in the spread of suicide attacks. Hamas and Islamic Jihad pioneered the tactic, while Fatah later faced pressure to demonstrate that it too was prepared to use violence. The chapter cites a study attributing different proportions of attacks to Hamas, Islamic Jihad, Fatah, and other PLO-linked groups during the first five years of the Second Intifada. This competition did not operate in isolation: it developed alongside the use of force by Israeli troops, the collapse of confidence in negotiations, and internal Palestinian political fragmentation. The author’s criticism is that factional competition encouraged tactics that inflicted grave harm on civilians and weakened the Palestinian national movement strategically.
Study instruction:
- "Review the cited study and distinguish its statistical findings from Khalidi’s explanation of the political incentives behind factional competition."
Key point:
- Rivalry between factions contributed to the spread of suicide attacks in the chapter’s analysis.
- Competitive escalation can encourage tactics with severe civilian and political costs.
16. Q: Why did Fatah become involved in suicide bombings despite the PLO’s earlier renunciation of violence?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that the PLO had renounced terrorism in 1988, but the Second Intifada created intense pressure on Fatah as Israeli forces shot demonstrators and Hamas and Islamic Jihad carried out suicide attacks. Fatah faced the risk of losing popular support to more militant rivals. The chapter describes its later involvement as part of a competitive escalation rather than as a carefully planned strategic reversal that achieved a defined political objective. This account illustrates how organizations can abandon prior commitments when they face internal competition and pressure from a rapidly escalating conflict. It also raises the question of whether tactical imitation of a rival can strengthen an organization or undermine its stated political aims.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the change in Fatah’s conduct and identify the pressures that Khalidi says contributed to it."
Key point:
- The chapter links Fatah’s participation to factional and popular pressure during escalation.
- Tactical imitation does not necessarily produce strategic advantage.
17. Q: What strategic criticism does Khalidi make of suicide bombings against Israeli civilians?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that attacks on civilians were not only morally and legally indefensible but also strategically counterproductive. Drawing on the earlier critique attributed to Eqbal Ahmad, he contends that such attacks strengthened Israeli public unity behind hard-line leaders, undermined international sympathy for Palestinians, and divided Palestinian society. The chapter challenges the idea that civilian attacks would dissolve Israeli society or force a political concession. It argues that the attacks instead helped the Israeli government present itself as the victim of irrational violence. This is the author’s strategic assessment; students should distinguish it from claims about the intentions of individual attackers and from the separate legal question of responsibility for specific incidents.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the intended political effect of attacks on civilians against the consequences Khalidi identifies for public opinion and Palestinian cohesion."
Key point:
- Khalidi considers suicide bombings strategically damaging to the Palestinian cause.
- The chapter links attacks on civilians to increased Israeli unity and reduced international sympathy.
Source links:- Jerusalem Media and Communications Centre: Poll No. 52
18. Q: How did the Second Intifada affect the international image of Palestinians?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the First Intifada and the subsequent peace negotiations had improved the international image of Palestinians, but the suicide bombings of the Second Intifada reversed much of that change. Repeated images of attacks on civilians allowed Israeli leaders to present the conflict as a struggle against irrational or fanatical violence, while the greater scale of Palestinian casualties and destruction received less attention in many media accounts. The chapter argues that the tactic undermined the moral and political gains made through earlier mass mobilization. This does not remove responsibility for the use of force by Israeli authorities; rather, it examines how different forms of violence affect public perception and the political space available to a national movement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the media narratives associated with the First and Second Intifadas and identify the events that shaped each image."
Key point:
- The author argues that suicide bombings damaged the international standing Palestinians had gained earlier.
- Media visibility and strategic communication can influence the political effects of a conflict.
19. Q: What does the chapter say about the political consequences of Arafat’s death in November 2004?
A: Show answer
Khalidi presents Arafat’s death as the end of an era in Palestinian politics. Mahmoud Abbas succeeded him as head of the PLO and Fatah and was elected president of the Palestinian Authority in January 2005. The author argues that Palestinian political fortunes were already weak at that point and deteriorated further in the following years, with intensified factional conflict, expanding settlements, and repeated wars on Gaza. The chapter also reflects critically on the loss of earlier Fatah leaders, many of whom had been killed by various intelligence services or armed groups. Arafat’s death therefore marked a leadership transition, but it did not resolve the institutional and strategic problems facing the Palestinian movement.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the leadership roles transferred after Arafat’s death and distinguish the PLO, Fatah, and PA positions."
Key point:
- Arafat’s death changed the leadership but did not resolve the movement’s strategic crisis.
- The chapter connects the transition to subsequent institutional weakness and factional division.
20. Q: What concern does the chapter raise about Mahmoud Abbas’s electoral mandate?
A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that Abbas was elected president of the Palestinian Authority in January 2005 for a four-year term and that no presidential election had been held since then at the time the chapter was written. The author therefore argues that Abbas had governed without a renewed democratic mandate from 2009 onward. This is a claim about the electoral timeline and the political legitimacy of the presidency, not a claim that all PA institutions ceased to exist. The distinction between formal office-holding and current electoral authorization is important when assessing political legitimacy, accountability, and the relationship between a governing authority and the population it represents.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the election date and term length, then explain the difference between constitutional office, electoral mandate, and political legitimacy."
Key point:
