- The chapter identifies the absence of a subsequent presidential election as a legitimacy problem.
- Formal continuation in office is not the same as renewed electoral authorization.
21. Q: How does the chapter describe the effect of targeted killings on Palestinian political leadership?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that repeated assassinations removed experienced political organizers, spokespeople, and factional leaders, weakening the capacity of Palestinian organizations. He lists senior figures from Fatah and other movements who were killed by Israeli intelligence services or by groups backed by regional governments. The chapter also notes that targeted killings continued during the Second Intifada and the Abbas years. It cites the killing of Ismail Abu Shanab, described as an opponent within Hamas of suicide bombings, to question whether all assassinations were driven by immediate military or security considerations. These are serious historical claims that require careful examination of the cited evidence and alternative accounts; the chapter’s analytical point is that leadership losses can affect organizational quality and strategic choice.
Study instruction:
- "Create a timeline of the leaders named and distinguish the chapter’s claims about responsibility, motive, and organizational consequences."
Key point:
- The author argues that targeted killings weakened Palestinian leadership capacity over time.
- Claims about motive should be assessed separately from the fact of a killing.
Source links:- New York Review of Books: Which Way for Hamas?
22. Q: What role does the chapter attribute to the Palestinian Authority’s security services during the Second Intifada?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the PA security apparatus as trying to suppress attacks by Hamas and Islamic Jihad in order to keep the Oslo process alive. He says that the security services included many former Fatah militants and that torture was used against Hamas suspects. The author argues that this produced deep hostility between the factions and contributed to the later rupture. These claims should be assessed against the relevant human-rights documentation and other historical accounts. The chapter’s central point is that the PA’s security responsibilities were politically contested: actions taken in the name of preserving negotiations could be experienced by rival Palestinian groups as repression and collaboration.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the PA’s stated security objective, the practices Khalidi describes, and the factional consequences he associates with them."
Key point:
- Security policy became a source of internal Palestinian conflict.
- The chapter links repression of Hamas suspects to deeper Fatah-Hamas hostility.
23. Q: Why did Hamas decide to participate in the Palestinian Legislative Council elections in January 2006?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Hamas’s decision to contest the 2006 parliamentary elections as a major change from its earlier rejection of the PA and the Oslo process. In the campaign, Hamas emphasized reform and change and downplayed some of its usual Islamist social agenda and armed-resistance messaging. Khalidi argues that entering the legislative process implied acceptance of the institutions created by Oslo and, indirectly, the political framework that had produced them. The organization did not fully resolve the tension between electoral participation and its continued commitment to armed resistance. The episode shows how a movement may enter institutions it previously rejected when it sees an opportunity to gain popular support and political influence.
Study instruction:
- "Compare Hamas’s earlier position on the PA and Oslo with its campaign platform and decision to field parliamentary candidates."
Key point:
- Electoral participation represented a significant strategic shift for Hamas.
- The shift created tension between governing through institutions and maintaining an armed-resistance identity.
24. Q: What were the results of the 2006 Palestinian Legislative Council elections?
A: Show answer
Khalidi reports that Hamas won 74 seats in the 132-member assembly compared with 45 seats for Fatah. He also notes that Hamas received about 44 percent of the vote and Fatah about 41 percent, illustrating how the electoral system translated vote shares into a larger seat advantage. The chapter emphasizes that the result was unexpected, including for Hamas itself. The figures should be interpreted with attention to the electoral system and the difference between popular vote share and seat distribution. A parliamentary majority can arise from constituency results and seat allocation even when the winning party does not receive a majority of all votes cast.
Study instruction:
- "Verify the seat and vote totals in the cited post-election sources and explain how electoral rules can produce different vote and seat shares."
Key point:
- Hamas won a parliamentary majority in seats, not an absolute majority of the popular vote.
- Electoral systems affect how votes translate into legislative representation.
Source links:- Palestinian Center for Policy and Survey Research: Post-Election Analysis
- Near East Consulting: 2006 Legislative Election Analysis
25. Q: Why does the chapter argue that Hamas’s 2006 victory reflected a desire for political change as well as support for the movement itself?
A: Show answer
Khalidi cites post-election polling suggesting that many voters were motivated by dissatisfaction with Fatah incumbents, whom they viewed as corrupt or unresponsive, rather than by a simple demand for Islamist governance or intensified armed resistance. The chapter notes that Hamas performed strongly in some areas with substantial Christian populations, which the author interprets as evidence that the election also functioned as a vote for change. This distinction matters because election results can combine ideological support, protest voting, candidate preferences, and dissatisfaction with the governing party. A careful interpretation should therefore avoid assuming that every vote for a party endorses every element of its platform.
Study instruction:
- "Review the post-election polling cited in the chapter and distinguish voter motivations from the formal programme of the winning party."
Key point:
- The chapter attributes part of Hamas’s victory to dissatisfaction with Fatah governance.
- Election results do not necessarily indicate uniform endorsement of every party policy.
Source links:- Palestinian Center for Policy and Survey Research: Post-Election Analysis
26. Q: What was the purpose of the Palestinian Prisoners’ Document issued in 2006?
A: Show answer
The Prisoners’ Document was an initiative by prominent Palestinian leaders held in Israeli prisons, representing several major factions, including Fatah, Hamas, the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine, and Islamic Jihad. Khalidi describes it as a call to end the internal rupture and develop a common political programme based on a two-state solution. The document mattered because it showed that influential figures from different factions could articulate a shared framework despite deep disagreements. The chapter treats it as an expression of pressure from below for national reconciliation. It also highlights the symbolic authority of prisoners in Palestinian society and the significance of a political proposal emerging from leaders who were themselves detained.
Study instruction:
- "Read the final revised document and identify its proposals on reconciliation, political representation, and the two-state framework."
Key point:
- The Prisoners’ Document sought a common programme and an end to factional division.
- It demonstrated the possibility of cross-faction agreement despite leadership conflict.
Source links:- Palestinian Prisoners’ Document, Revised 28 June 2006
27. Q: Why did attempts to form a Fatah-Hamas coalition government face strong external opposition?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Israel and the United States rejected Hamas’s participation in a PA coalition unless it met conditions including explicit recognition of Israel and other requirements. He interprets these demands as another round of external conditions imposed on a Palestinian faction seeking political legitimacy. The chapter says that this pressure made compromise difficult even when Palestinian political figures and public opinion favoured reconciliation. The issue illustrates the interaction between internal democratic outcomes and external diplomatic and financial leverage. A government may win an election but still face constraints on its ability to govern if major donors and diplomatic partners refuse to recognize or fund it.
Study instruction:
- "List the conditions described in the chapter and assess how external recognition and funding can affect a coalition government’s ability to operate."
Key point:
- External conditions complicated attempts at Palestinian national reconciliation.
- Electoral victory does not guarantee international acceptance or access to funding.
28. Q: How did the Fatah-Hamas split develop into separate Palestinian authorities in Gaza and the West Bank?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes escalating conflict after the 2006 election, repeated failed coalition efforts, and a 2007 armed confrontation in Gaza. Khalidi recounts that US-trained, Fatah-controlled security forces under Muhammad Dahlan attempted to remove Hamas by force, after which Hamas defeated those forces and established its own administration in Gaza. The Ramallah-based PA continued to operate in parts of the West Bank under the constraints imposed by Israeli control. The result was two competing Palestinian authorities, each with limited jurisdiction and distinct political and security structures. The split weakened the ability of Palestinians to present a unified negotiating position and created a long-term institutional division.
Study instruction:
- "Build a timeline of the election, coalition negotiations, failed reconciliation efforts, 2007 fighting, and emergence of separate administrations."
Key point:
- The split produced separate political administrations in Gaza and the West Bank.
- Institutional division weakened national political coordination.
29. Q: What were the consequences of the blockade imposed on Gaza after Hamas took control in 2007?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes severe restrictions on goods entering Gaza, the stopping of regular exports, cuts to fuel supplies, and very limited permission for people to enter or leave. He characterizes the result as an open-air prison and cites poverty and unemployment figures to illustrate the humanitarian and economic impact. The chapter argues that the blockade intensified Gaza’s isolation and contributed to the conditions under which later military assaults took place. The figures it cites are dated to 2018 and should not be presented as current statistics without checking the underlying sources. The broader point is that restrictions on movement, trade, energy, and access can affect an entire economy and population, not only the governing faction.
Study instruction:
- "Review the cited OCHA and Gisha figures and record their dates, definitions, and population coverage."
Key point:
- The chapter links the blockade to severe economic and social conditions in Gaza.
- Poverty and unemployment figures must be labelled with their reference dates.
Source links:- OCHA: Palestinians in Gaza Living in Poverty
- Gisha: Gaza Employment Conditions
30. Q: Which major Israeli military assaults on Gaza does the chapter examine?
A: Show answer
The chapter focuses on major Israeli offensives in 2008–2009, 2012, and 2014. Khalidi describes these campaigns as involving air and ground operations, widespread destruction, and serious civilian harm. He situates them within the broader context of Gaza’s isolation, restrictions on movement and trade, and the political division between Hamas-led Gaza and the Fatah-led PA in Ramallah. The chapter also considers the use of rockets by Hamas and other armed groups, the response of Israeli forces, and the international media narrative surrounding each conflict. The purpose is not simply to list operations, but to analyse the relationship between military power, civilian vulnerability, political division, and international response.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table for the 2008–2009, 2012, and 2014 offensives covering dates, reported casualties, damage, and cited sources."
Key point:
- The chapter treats the three assaults as connected episodes in the deterioration of Gaza’s conditions.
- Each campaign should be studied with its own chronology and evidence.
Source links:- B’Tselem: Fatalities During the 2008–2009 Gaza Operation
- B’Tselem: Fatalities After the 2008–2009 Operation
31. Q: What casualty figures does Khalidi report for the three major Gaza assaults between 2008 and 2014?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that 3,804 Palestinians were killed in the three major attacks, including almost one thousand minors, while 87 Israelis were killed. Khalidi notes that most of the Israelis killed were military personnel involved in the operations, while most Palestinian casualties were civilians. These figures are attributed to B’Tselem and should be checked against the underlying tables, the precise definition of each operation, and the classification of civilian and combatant status. The author uses the figures to illustrate the disparity in casualties, but a responsible analysis should preserve the source’s definitions and avoid treating a single aggregate number as a substitute for incident-level investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Retrieve the cited B’Tselem tables and record the operation dates, categories, and methods used to classify deaths."
Key point:
- The chapter reports a large disparity in deaths across the three Gaza campaigns.
- Aggregate figures should be checked against the underlying source and definitions.
Source links:- B’Tselem: Fatalities During the 2008–2009 Gaza Operation
- B’Tselem: Fatalities After the 2008–2009 Operation
32. Q: What features of the 2014 Gaza assault does the chapter use to illustrate the scale of Israeli firepower?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes extensive air attacks and artillery and tank shelling during the 2014 operation. He cites figures for thousands of air attacks and tens of thousands of shells, as well as the use of fighter aircraft, attack helicopters, drones, and heavy bombs. The chapter argues that the weapons and volume of firepower were especially destructive in a densely populated territory where civilians had limited ability to leave. These figures come from sources identified in the chapter’s notes, including Israeli reporting and military commentary. They should be treated as reported estimates and checked against the cited sources, rather than repeated as independently verified measurements.
Study instruction:
- "Identify each reported category of weapon and ammunition and note the source and date for each estimate."
Key point:
- The chapter emphasizes the scale and type of firepower used in a densely populated area.
- Weapons and ammunition figures should retain their source attribution and uncertainty.
Source links:- Defense News: Gaza War and F-16 Close-Air Support
- Ynet: Protective Edge in Numbers
33. Q: Why does the chapter focus on the Shuja’iyya assault of July 2014?
A: Show answer
Khalidi uses the assault on Shuja’iyya to illustrate the intensity of artillery, tank, and air attacks in a densely populated urban neighbourhood. He cites accounts from American military sources describing a very high volume of shells fired in a short period and quotations from former US officers who considered the scale of firepower extraordinary. The author argues that the weapons used were designed for open battlefield conditions and were inherently dangerous in a dense civilian environment. A careful study should distinguish the number of shells reported, the identity and access of the sources, the military circumstances, and the author’s legal and strategic conclusions about proportionality.
Study instruction:
- "Review the cited account and separate the reported operational figures from expert quotations and Khalidi’s interpretation."
Key point:
- Shuja’iyya is presented as a case study of intense firepower in an urban setting.
- Military assessments and legal conclusions require distinct evidence and analysis.
Source links:- Al Jazeera America: US Officers Stunned by Gaza Bombardment
- Idan Barir: Civilian Casualties and Artillery Use in Gaza
34. Q: What is the Dahiya doctrine as described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the Dahiya doctrine as an Israeli military approach associated with the destruction of the southern Beirut suburb of al-Dahiya in 2006. He cites a 2008 statement by Major General Gadi Eizenkot describing the use of disproportionate force and extensive destruction against places from which Israel is attacked. The chapter argues that this approach helps explain the scale of destruction in Gaza in 2014. The doctrine should be studied as a concept discussed by the author and attributed to specific public statements, not as a substitute for examining the orders, operational decisions, and legal rules applicable to individual attacks.
Study instruction:
- "Read the cited statements and distinguish the doctrine’s stated logic from the evidence needed to assess particular military operations."
Key point:
- The chapter links the Dahiya doctrine to the use of extensive destructive force in populated areas.
- A general military concept does not determine the legal status of every individual strike.
Source links:- Ynet: Israel Warns of Destruction in a Future War
- Ynet: The Dahiya Strategy
35. Q: How does the chapter approach the legal question of proportionality in the 2014 Gaza assault?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the scale of firepower, the type of weapons used, the density of the population, and the resulting civilian harm raise serious questions under the laws of war. He cites the Dahiya doctrine and reports of heavy artillery and large bombs used in urban areas as evidence for his argument about disproportionate force. However, legal proportionality is a specific legal test requiring an assessment of the expected civilian harm in relation to the anticipated concrete and direct military advantage for each attack. A historian’s argument or a casualty ratio alone does not automatically establish a legal violation. The chapter is therefore a starting point for legal inquiry, not a substitute for incident-level investigation and authoritative legal findings.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the legal test of proportionality from general comparisons of casualties or firepower, and identify what evidence an incident-level assessment would require."
Key point:
- The author argues that the scale and context of the assault raise grave legal questions.
- Proportionality must be assessed under the applicable legal test, not inferred from casualty totals alone.
Source links:- US Code: 22 USC 2754, Purposes for Which Military Sales Are Authorized
36. Q: How does the chapter assess Hamas and Islamic Jihad rocket attacks on Israeli civilian areas?
A: Show answer
Khalidi acknowledges that the use of imprecise rockets against civilian areas violates the rules governing attacks and can raise serious legal concerns. He notes that these weapons caused fear among Israeli residents and that some civilians were killed. The chapter compares their limited guidance and explosive power with the far greater volume and destructive capacity of Israeli artillery and aerial weapons. This comparison is used to challenge the way media coverage sometimes focused almost exclusively on rocket fire. It does not excuse indiscriminate attacks on civilians: all parties remain bound by the laws of war. A sound analysis should assess the legality of each type of attack independently and should not treat one party’s violations as a justification for another’s.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the distinction between indiscriminate attacks, the weapons’ technical characteristics, civilian harm, and the legal obligations of all parties."
Key point:
- The chapter criticizes rocket attacks on civilians while comparing their effects with the much greater firepower used by Israel.
- Violations by one party do not excuse violations by another.
Source links:- B’Tselem: Fatalities in the 2014 Gaza Conflict
37. Q: What does the chapter argue about the relationship between media coverage and perceptions of the Gaza wars?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that mainstream US media coverage often focused heavily on rocket fire from Gaza while giving less attention to the scale of Israeli firepower and the civilian destruction in the densely populated territory. He contends that this imbalance obscured the disparity in casualties and military capabilities. The chapter also notes that images of violence can influence international perceptions in different directions: the suicide bombings of the Second Intifada damaged the Palestinian image, while images from the Gaza wars contributed to increased criticism of Israeli policy among some American audiences. These are the author’s interpretations of media and opinion trends and should be assessed through the underlying coverage and polling data.
Study instruction:
- "Compare coverage from multiple outlets and identify differences in framing, casualty emphasis, source selection, and historical context."
Key point:
- Media framing can shape which aspects of a conflict become most visible.
- The chapter links images of civilian harm to changes in some segments of US public opinion.
Source links:- Brookings: American Attitudes on the Israeli-Palestinian Conflict
38. Q: What changes in American public opinion about Israel and Palestine does the chapter identify?
A: Show answer
Khalidi cites polling from 2016 to 2019 suggesting that sympathy for Palestinians increased among younger people, Democrats, and some liberal or minority groups, while other segments remained strongly supportive of Israeli government policy. The chapter interprets these figures as evidence of a growing generational and partisan divide. It also stresses that public opinion did not translate automatically into changes in US policy, legislation, or military assistance. Polls measure responses to particular questions at particular times, so the wording, sample, and date matter. The chapter’s central point is that changes in public attitudes may occur more quickly than changes in political institutions and foreign policy.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the 2016, 2018, and 2019 polls and record question wording, sample, dates, and differences between partisan groups."
Key point:
- The chapter identifies a growing partisan and generational divide in US public opinion.
- Opinion changes do not automatically produce policy changes.
Source links:- Pew Research Center: Views of Israel and Palestinians, 2016
- Pew Research Center: Partisan Views, 2018
- Pew Research Center: American Views, 2019
39. Q: Why does the chapter argue that changes in US public opinion did not produce equivalent changes in US policy?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that public attitudes were only one part of the policy-making environment. He identifies party leadership, major donors, congressional incentives, advocacy organizations, executive-branch personnel, and long-standing assumptions about Israel as factors that could limit policy change. The chapter notes that a growing share of Democratic voters expressed more critical views of Israeli policy, while party leaders and many major donors remained strongly supportive of Israel. The author therefore distinguishes the preferences of voters from the incentives facing elected officials and the institutional networks involved in foreign policy. This is a political interpretation that should be examined alongside polling, campaign finance, congressional votes, and executive decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Map the channels through which public opinion can influence foreign policy and identify where institutional or financial incentives may intervene."
Key point:
- Public opinion is only one input into foreign policy decisions.
- Party leadership, donors, Congress, and executive institutions can mediate or constrain its effect.
Source links:- Brookings: American Attitudes on the Israeli-Palestinian Conflict
40. Q: How does the chapter describe the Republican Party’s position on Israel during the period it covers?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Republican Party’s leadership and electoral base were strongly supportive of hawkish Israeli policies, particularly among Evangelical Christians and conservative donors. He identifies ideological affinity, party politics, and financial contributions as influences on Republican positions. The chapter also discusses the close relationship between the Netanyahu government and Republican political figures. These claims are part of the author’s analysis of US domestic politics and should be assessed against public statements, legislative records, and campaign-finance evidence. The key distinction is between the views of party supporters, elected officials, and the policy choices made by an administration; they may overlap but are not identical.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter’s account with public statements, congressional votes, and documented campaign-finance information for the period."
Key point:
- The author links Republican support for Israeli policy to ideology, electoral constituencies, and donors.
- Party-base preferences and administration policy should be analysed separately.
Source links:- Pew Research Center: American Views, 2019
41. Q: Why does the chapter describe the Democratic Party as divided over Israel and Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a generational and ideological split between younger, more liberal, and minority voters who were increasingly critical of Israeli policy and older party leaders and donors who remained strongly supportive of Israel. He discusses the 2016 Democratic primary campaign, debates over the party platform, and the controversy surrounding Keith Ellison as examples of the tension. The chapter argues that fundraising needs and the influence of major donors helped preserve the party leadership’s established position even as parts of its voter base changed. This analysis should be evaluated using polling, party-platform documents, campaign statements, and voting records rather than assuming that all Democrats share one position.
Study instruction:
- "Compare attitudes among Democratic voters, elected leaders, and major donors using the polls and political episodes cited in the chapter."
Key point:
- The chapter portrays the Democratic Party as divided across generational, ideological, and donor lines.
- A party’s voter base may shift before its leadership and policy positions do.
Source links:- Pew Research Center: Partisan Views, 2018
42. Q: What distinction does Khalidi make between Congress and the US executive branch in foreign policy-making?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that presidents often have greater room to manoeuvre in foreign policy than members of Congress, who face frequent elections, fundraising pressures, and organized constituent interests. Khalidi cites historical examples in which US presidents or secretaries of state acted against Israeli preferences when they believed broader US strategic interests required it. He then argues that successive administrations did not treat Palestinian-Israeli peacemaking as sufficiently important to justify using comparable political capital. This is the author’s explanation of why policy remained largely unchanged despite some shifts in public opinion. The distinction is useful, but the actual authority of each branch depends on the issue, statutory framework, appropriations, and political circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Compare examples of executive action and congressional constraints cited in the chapter, noting the legal and political tools available to each branch."
Key point:
- The chapter argues that presidents may have more foreign-policy flexibility than Congress.
- Political willingness to use that authority is a separate question from legal capacity.
43. Q: What role did George Mitchell play in the Obama administration’s approach to Israeli-Palestinian negotiations?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Mitchell as a special envoy appointed in 2009 to restart direct negotiations and as an experienced mediator with a record in the Northern Ireland peace process. The chapter portrays him as more independent-minded than officials who treated Israeli positions as the limits of US policy. Mitchell sought to address difficult issues including settlement freezes, Jerusalem, and refugee return, and he considered Hamas’s inclusion important for a comprehensive process. Khalidi argues that Mitchell was undermined by internal opposition and by officials who operated separate channels with Israel. The episode illustrates how the effectiveness of a special envoy depends not only on personal skill but also on authority, access, coordination, and support from the administration.
Study instruction:
- "Identify Mitchell’s mandate, proposed negotiating priorities, and the institutional obstacles described by Khalidi."
Key point:
- Mitchell’s effectiveness depended on consistent backing from the administration.
- Parallel channels can weaken a mediator’s authority and coherence.
44. Q: Why does the chapter criticize Dennis Ross’s role in the Obama administration?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Ross undermined Mitchell by maintaining close contacts with Israeli officials and conducting separate discussions that bypassed the special envoy. The chapter says Ross was viewed by Israeli officials as a preferred interlocutor and that his actions allowed Israel to play different US representatives against one another. This is the author’s critical interpretation of the internal dynamics of the administration. To evaluate it, readers should distinguish reported events, anonymous interviews cited in the chapter, and Khalidi’s conclusions about their effect. The broader institutional lesson is that a negotiating team needs clear authority and coordination; otherwise, the counterpart may exploit divisions within the sponsoring government.
Study instruction:
- "Map the roles of Mitchell, Ross, the State Department, and the National Security Council as described, and identify where coordination broke down."
Key point:
- The author argues that competing US channels weakened the negotiating effort.
- Internal coherence is important when a government acts as a mediator.
45. Q: How does the chapter assess President Obama’s response to the Gaza wars?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Obama did not challenge the dominant US narrative that Israeli operations in Gaza were responses to rocket fire and did not interrupt the supply of US weapons used in the campaigns. The author criticizes the administration for not using its leverage to address the blockade or the civilian consequences of the assaults. These are the author’s political judgments and should be distinguished from the administration’s public statements, military-aid records, and other documentary evidence. The chapter’s central criticism is that rhetorical concern and diplomatic initiatives did not translate into significant changes in policy or conditions on the ground.
Study instruction:
- "Compare public statements by the administration with its actual policy decisions on military assistance, the blockade, and diplomatic pressure."
Key point:
- The author judges Obama’s response by the practical policy consequences, not only by public rhetoric.
- Diplomatic engagement does not necessarily produce leverage or policy change.
46. Q: What was the significance of UN Security Council Resolution 2334 in the chapter’s account?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies Resolution 2334, adopted in December 2016 with a US abstention, as a statement that Israeli settlement activity in the West Bank and East Jerusalem violated international law and had no legal validity. He argues that the resolution did not include sanctions or coercive measures and therefore had little practical effect on the situation on the ground. The episode illustrates the distinction between a declaratory diplomatic position and an enforceable policy. A resolution can express an international legal or political judgment, but its practical consequences depend on implementation, monitoring, and follow-up by states and international institutions.
Study instruction:
- "Read the resolution itself and identify its legal language, requested actions, and the enforcement mechanisms it does or does not establish."
Key point:
- The chapter treats Resolution 2334 as significant in wording but limited in practical enforcement.
- Diplomatic condemnation does not automatically change conduct on the ground.
47. Q: How does the chapter interpret the relationship between US policy and the views of Arab publics?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that US policy toward Israel was insulated from some of the negative consequences it might otherwise face in the Arab world because many regional governments were authoritarian and maintained close relations with Washington. The chapter contrasts pro-Palestinian public sentiment in Arab countries with the limited pressure those governments exerted on the United States. It identifies Saudi Arabia as an especially important example of a state that publicly supported the Palestinian cause while maintaining a cautious relationship with Washington. This is a political interpretation that should be assessed alongside polling, diplomatic records, and the policies of individual governments. The central analytical distinction is between public opinion in a country and the foreign-policy choices of its government.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the cited Arab public-opinion survey with the foreign-policy actions of selected governments during the same period."
Key point:
- Public opinion and government policy may diverge substantially.
- The chapter argues that regional political structures reduced pressure on Washington.
Source links:- Arab Center for Research and Policy Studies: Arab Index 2017–2018
48. Q: What was the significance of the 2004 Bush letter to Ariel Sharon discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Khalidi refers to a letter from President George W. Bush to Israeli Prime Minister Ariel Sharon that endorsed the idea that major Israeli population centres or settlement blocs would be taken into account in a final peace settlement. The chapter interprets this as a diplomatic concession that helped legitimize Israeli expectations about retaining certain settlement areas. The significance lies in how statements by a US president can influence negotiating assumptions and the perceived parameters of a future agreement. Readers should examine the letter’s exact wording and distinguish what it explicitly says from the author’s interpretation of its legal and political consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Read the letter and identify its commitments, qualifications, and implications for negotiations over territory and settlements."
Key point:
- The chapter treats the Bush letter as a significant US political assurance concerning settlement blocs.
- Exact wording matters when evaluating diplomatic commitments.
Source links:- Israel Ministry of Foreign Affairs: Exchange of Letters, Sharon–Bush, 14 April 2004
49. Q: How should readers evaluate the chapter’s claims about the legality and proportionality of military operations?
A: Show answer
Readers should distinguish the chapter’s historical narrative, reported casualty and weapons figures, quoted military statements, and the author’s legal conclusions. Claims about indiscriminate attacks, civilian harm, proportionality, and possible war crimes require analysis under the applicable rules of international humanitarian law and evidence about specific attacks. The chapter argues that the scale of destruction in Gaza raises grave legal questions, while also acknowledging that rockets fired at civilian areas by Palestinian armed groups may be unlawful. A balanced legal analysis should apply the same legal principles to all parties while considering the different factual circumstances of each attack. Neither political affiliation nor a casualty comparison replaces the need for evidence and legal reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "For one incident, identify the target, weapon, expected military advantage, foreseeable civilian harm, precautions, and available investigation findings."
Key point:
- The chapter raises serious legal questions but does not replace incident-specific legal assessment.
- International humanitarian law applies to all parties to an armed conflict.
Source links:- US Code: 22 USC 2754
50. Q: What is the overall argument of Chapter 6 about the period from 2000 to 2014?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the failure of the Oslo process, the collapse of the 2000 Camp David summit, the Second Intifada, the use of suicide bombings, the Fatah-Hamas split, and repeated wars on Gaza deepened Palestinian political and social weakness. He presents the period as one in which neither the diplomatic strategy associated with the PLO nor the armed tactics used by Hamas and other groups achieved the central goal of ending occupation and securing sovereignty. The chapter also argues that US policy remained substantially supportive of Israel despite some changes in American public opinion. Its overall thesis is that political fragmentation and ineffective strategies, combined with continuing external control and repeated military conflict, left Palestinians in a more difficult position by 2014. This is the author’s critical synthesis and should be assessed against the primary documents and sources cited throughout the chapter.
Study instruction:
- "Write a synthesis connecting the Camp David summit, Second Intifada, Arafat’s death, 2006 election, 2007 split, Gaza wars, and US policy."
Key point:
- The chapter argues that both the Oslo process and the armed escalation failed to produce Palestinian sovereignty.
- Political division and external constraints compounded the consequences of the conflict.
Rows: 50
Table 7
A Century of War on the Palestinians: Historical Argument, Equality, and Political Futures
1. Q: What is the central argument of the conclusion, “A Century of War on the Palestinians”?
A: Show answer
Rashid Khalidi argues that the conflict cannot be understood as a temporary dispute between two equivalent national movements. He presents it as a century-long struggle shaped by settler colonialism, external great-power sponsorship, unequal power, and the denial of Palestinian political agency and rights. The conclusion links the Balfour Declaration era to later international decisions, including the Trump administration’s treatment of Jerusalem, to illustrate a recurring pattern: powerful states making decisions about Palestine without meaningful Palestinian participation. Khalidi’s proposed route beyond this history is not the elimination of either people, but a political future founded on equality, mutual recognition, and the ending of domination. The chapter is an argument and interpretation; its historical claims and prescriptions should be evaluated against the evidence and context it presents.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the author’s diagnosis, the historical mechanisms sustaining it, and the principles he considers necessary for a durable resolution.
Key point:
- The conclusion places colonial power, external sponsorship, and inequality at the centre of the author’s explanation.
- Its proposed alternative is equal rights and mutual recognition rather than the removal of either people.
2. Q: Why does the author place the 1917 Balfour position alongside President Trump’s 2017 recognition of Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
The comparison illustrates, in Khalidi’s interpretation, a recurring method of deciding Palestine’s future over the heads of its inhabitants. He contrasts Balfour’s reported refusal to consult the country’s Arab population with Trump’s declaration that Jerusalem had been “taken off the table” before a negotiated settlement. The historical settings and officials differ, but the author argues that both episodes treat Palestinian wishes as dispensable when powerful external actors define the political agenda. Jerusalem is especially significant because the author identifies it as central to Palestinian history, identity, culture, and worship. The comparison is therefore about political agency and representation, not a claim that the two decisions were identical in legal form or historical circumstance.
Study instruction:
- Compare the two episodes by identifying the decision-maker, the people consulted or excluded, the issue affected, and the consequences for negotiation.
Key point:
- The parallel highlights a recurring exclusion of Palestinians from decisions about their political future.
- Similarity of political logic does not mean the historical events were identical.
3. Q: What does the conclusion mean when it says that great powers repeatedly acted “in spite of” the Palestinians?
A: Show answer
Khalidi uses this idea to describe policies in which outside powers ignored Palestinian preferences, represented Palestinians without their consent, or treated them as if they were not a relevant political community. He cites decisions and diplomatic arrangements across the mandate period and later decades, including the partition process, the sidelining of the PLO, and policies that he argues enabled territorial seizure and settlement. The phrase does not mean that every external decision had the same motive or effect. It identifies a pattern in which the people most affected were denied an effective role in determining the terms of their future. The author treats this exclusion as a central source of the conflict’s persistence.
Study instruction:
- Build a timeline of the external decisions mentioned in the conclusion and record how Palestinian participation was limited in each case.
Key point:
- The author treats political exclusion as a recurring feature of international management of the Palestine question.
- Analyse each historical episode in its own context rather than assuming all great powers acted identically.
4. Q: How does the conclusion explain Palestinian persistence despite the imbalance of power?
A: Show answer
The author emphasizes the continuing existence of Palestinian national identity, political claims, civil society activity, and resistance to displacement. He argues that the project of replacing or politically erasing the indigenous population has not achieved its intended result, even though Palestinians have faced powerful military, diplomatic, and economic opponents. Persistence is not presented as proof that every Palestinian strategy has succeeded. Indeed, the conclusion is sharply critical of the established political leadership. Rather, persistence explains why the Palestine question remains unresolved and why an imposed settlement cannot simply make the underlying people or their claims disappear. The author treats steadfastness as a political resource that must be connected to a coherent long-term strategy.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the continued existence of a people and their claims from the effectiveness of any particular political organization or tactic.
Key point:
- Palestinian persistence has kept the national question alive despite severe power asymmetry.
- Persistence alone is insufficient without effective organization and strategy.
5. Q: How does the author characterize the United States’ changing public position and continuing strategic alignment with Israel?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes American policy as alternating between limited acknowledgment of Palestinian statehood or a homeland and actions that, in his account, marginalized Palestinian claims. He cites the unimplemented Arab-state provision in the 1947 partition resolution, later rhetorical support for Palestinian statehood, and contrasting episodes such as the treatment of the PLO, Camp David, the 1982 Lebanon war, and continued settlement expansion. His larger claim is that shifts in diplomatic language did not displace the underlying strategic support extended to Israel. This distinction matters: public statements, formal recognition, mediation practices, military assistance, and the practical effects of policy may point in different directions and should be assessed separately.
Study instruction:
- Create a two-column chronology separating American statements about Palestinian rights from the policies and actions the author identifies as limiting them.
Key point:
- The conclusion distinguishes rhetorical acknowledgment from material and diplomatic policy.
- Evaluate policy by examining actions and consequences as well as official language.
6. Q: What is the contradiction in imposing a colonial arrangement during a postcolonial era?
A: Show answer
The conclusion argues that the major powers supported a settler-colonial project at a time when decolonization was becoming a defining international principle. Khalidi uses Eqbal Ahmad’s observation about the end of British rule in India and the simultaneous colonization of Palestine to underline this historical contradiction. The point is not simply that colonial practices continued after formal empires weakened. It is that a political project based on exclusive control over land and national rights was being advanced in an era increasingly shaped by anti-colonial claims to self-determination. This tension, in the author’s analysis, helps explain why the project encountered sustained resistance and why its legitimacy remains contested internationally.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between formal decolonization and the continuation of colonial relationships through settlement, unequal rights, or external sponsorship.
Key point:
- The author sees Palestine as a settler-colonial project advanced during the wider age of decolonization.
- Historical timing is central to his explanation of the conflict’s political and international character.
7. Q: What three broad outcomes does the author identify in settler-colonial confrontations?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes three broad historical outcomes: elimination or full subjugation of the indigenous population, as he characterizes the experience in North America; defeat and expulsion of the colonizer, which he describes as rare and associates with Algeria; or the abandonment of colonial supremacy through compromise and reconciliation, with South Africa, Zimbabwe, and Ireland offered as examples. This typology is intended to show that domination is not the only possible endpoint. It also frames the author’s argument that a negotiated future based on equality is preferable to extermination, expulsion, or permanent subjugation. The examples are broad historical comparisons, not identical case studies; each has its own chronology, institutions, actors, and unresolved legacies.
Study instruction:
- Reproduce the three-outcome typology and identify the limits of applying any one historical case directly to Palestine.
Key point:
- The three outcomes are elimination or subjugation, expulsion of the colonizer, and negotiated abandonment of supremacy.
- Khalidi favours the third path as the only sustainable alternative to renewed violence.
8. Q: Why does the conclusion warn about the possibility of renewed mass expulsion of Palestinians?
A: Show answer
The author argues that prior expulsions in 1948 and 1967, together with later practices of displacement and demographic engineering, make the risk a serious subject for political analysis. He points to annexation proposals, home demolitions, evictions, rezoning, and property-related schemes as measures that can pressure people to leave or lose their homes. He also notes that some political figures had publicly advocated removing Palestinians. The conclusion does not present a new mass expulsion as inevitable; it assesses both the factors that could make it more possible and the resistance, international attention, and diplomatic costs that could constrain it. The warning is a risk assessment grounded in the author’s historical interpretation, not a prediction with certainty.
Study instruction:
- Separate documented past displacement, ongoing administrative or coercive measures described by the author, and the future risk he evaluates.
Key point:
- The chapter treats renewed expulsion as a risk, not as an inevitable outcome.
- Analyse both the mechanisms that could enable displacement and the factors that might deter it.
9. Q: What factors does the author believe could constrain a renewed campaign of mass expulsion?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies several constraints: the likelihood of determined Palestinian resistance, the intense international attention focused on Palestine, the growing circulation of Palestinian accounts of the conflict, and the potential damage to Western support if a clearly identifiable campaign of ethnic cleansing occurred. He contrasts this with situations in which population displacement unfolds amid complex civil wars and extensive foreign intervention, arguing that a new expulsion in Palestine would be more visible as a deliberate colonial act. These are the author’s assessments of political and reputational constraints, not guarantees that abuses cannot occur. The distinction between deterrence and prevention is important: factors that raise the cost of an action may reduce its likelihood without eliminating the danger.
Study instruction:
- Classify the constraints as domestic resistance, international scrutiny, reputational consequences, and dependence on external support.
Key point:
- Resistance and international visibility may increase the political cost of mass expulsion.
- Deterrents reduce risk but do not remove the need for monitoring and protection.
10. Q: Why does the author regard the international representation of the conflict as politically important?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that political outcomes are shaped not only by material power but also by the stories through which a conflict is understood. He draws on Edward Said’s observation that Zionism won an important political contest in the international arena of ideas, representation, rhetoric, and images. In the conclusion, he contends that presenting Israel as an ordinary nation-state facing irrational hostility can obscure the colonial relationship and the unequal distribution of rights and power. If that framing dominates, Palestinian claims may appear peripheral or illegitimate. Changing public understanding therefore becomes part of political strategy: it can influence diplomatic support, civil society engagement, and the range of solutions that international audiences consider possible.
Study instruction:
- Identify the narrative used to describe a conflict and compare it with the underlying distribution of land, power, rights, and decision-making authority.
Key point:
- Representation can shape which political claims are considered legitimate.
- The author treats public understanding as a field of political contest, not merely a communications issue.
11. Q: What three approaches does the conclusion recommend for changing how the Palestine conflict is understood?
A: Show answer
The author identifies three mutually reinforcing approaches. First, compare Palestine with other settler-colonial experiences, including those of Native Americans, South Africans, and the Irish, while recognizing differences between cases. Second, make the imbalance of power visible rather than presenting the conflict as a contest between equally equipped parties. Third, foreground inequality, which Khalidi regards as the most promising and fundamental issue because it concerns the distribution of rights within the same territory. Together, these approaches challenge a narrative of symmetrical conflict and direct attention to historical structures and current arrangements. They are strategic methods of explanation; each still requires careful evidence and should not be used as a substitute for examining specific events and institutions.
Study instruction:
- Apply all three approaches to one example and note what each reveals that a simple two-sided-conflict narrative might miss.
Key point:
- The three approaches are comparative analysis, power asymmetry, and inequality.
- Inequality is presented as the most fundamental because it concerns the rights of people living in the same land.
12. Q: Why does the author say that the word “colonial” has a different resonance in the United States?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the term carries different historical and emotional associations depending on the audience. In former European imperial centres, “colonial” may evoke overseas empire and domination. In the United States, he contends, national memory often gives positive connotations to settlement and pioneering, while evangelical Protestant traditions can make biblical narratives especially influential in interpretations of the land. This makes it harder, in his view, to persuade American audiences to recognize the conflict as a settler-colonial encounter. The practical implication is that advocacy cannot rely on a label alone. It must explain the historical processes, the people affected, and the present-day consequences in terms that audiences can assess critically.
Study instruction:
- Compare how the same historical term may be understood differently in societies with different national narratives and imperial histories.
Key point:
- Political vocabulary is interpreted through national memory and cultural assumptions.
- Effective explanation requires evidence and context, not labels alone.
13. Q: How does the conclusion use comparisons with Native American, South African, and Irish experiences?
A: Show answer
These comparisons are used to place Palestine within a wider history of settler expansion, indigenous dispossession, resistance, and possible political transformation. Khalidi argues that the comparison can make visible patterns that may be obscured when Palestine is treated as an exceptional or isolated dispute. He also notes that American narratives about westward expansion often portray indigenous resistance as an obstacle to progress, a framing he sees as comparable to depictions of Palestinian resistance. However, comparisons must be used carefully. Different societies experienced distinct forms of settlement, state formation, violence, legal change, and reconciliation. The value of comparison lies in identifying relevant patterns while preserving the differences that make each case historically specific.
Study instruction:
- For each comparison, list one relevant structural similarity and one important historical difference; avoid treating the cases as interchangeable.
Key point:
- Comparative history can expose recurring colonial patterns.
- A valid comparison must preserve differences in context, actors, law, and outcome.
14. Q: What does the author mean by the imbalance of power between Israel and the Palestinians?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the conflict is often presented as if two equivalent sides confront one another, even though the resources and external backing available to them are profoundly unequal. He traces the stronger side’s support through different periods, including British military power before 1939, international backing around 1947–48, European involvement in later decades, and extensive United States support from the 1970s onward. He contrasts Israel’s state institutions and military capacity with the much more limited capabilities of the Palestinians. The analytical point is that formal descriptions of two parties can conceal major differences in coercive power, diplomatic influence, territorial control, and access to resources. A fair assessment must examine those asymmetries directly.
Study instruction:
- Map the parties’ military, diplomatic, territorial, economic, and institutional resources for a defined historical period.
Key point:
- The author rejects an assumption of equal power between the parties.
- Power asymmetry affects negotiation conditions and the practical meaning of formal agreements.
15. Q: Why does the conclusion describe inequality as the central issue in the conflict?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that inequality is not a secondary consequence but a core feature of the political arrangement he criticizes. In his account, unequal national, civil, political, and land rights helped establish and sustain a state structure intended to privilege one national group in a territory with a large Arab population. Inequality is also strategically important because it can be discussed in terms of principles that democratic societies publicly endorse, such as equal citizenship and equal rights. Most importantly, the author argues that no durable settlement can succeed if it preserves unequal rights as its foundation. The chapter therefore shifts attention from the mere existence of two national movements to the rights and status of the people who live under the resulting political order.
Study instruction:
- Identify the forms of inequality discussed in the chapter and explain how each affects citizenship, land, political participation, or national self-determination.
Key point:
- Inequality is treated as both a historical mechanism and a present political problem.
- Equality of rights is the author’s proposed foundation for a sustainable settlement.
16. Q: How does the conclusion distinguish the position of Palestinian citizens of Israel from Palestinians in the Occupied Territories?
A: Show answer
Khalidi distinguishes between Palestinians who hold Israeli citizenship and Palestinians living under Israeli military rule in the Occupied Territories. He argues that important rights are unequal even within Israel’s citizenry, while Palestinians in the occupied areas face a different and more restrictive legal and political regime. He contrasts those conditions with the rights and protections available to Israeli settlers living in the same territories. The distinction matters because “Palestinians” are not all situated under one legal status, and a rights analysis must specify the population, territory, governing authority, and legal framework involved. The population figures and descriptions in the conclusion reflect the book’s publication-era account and should not be repeated as current statistics without separate verification.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison table that separates citizenship status, governing regime, political rights, and territorial location for each population discussed.
Key point:
- Legal status and rights differ across Israel and the Occupied Territories.
- The chapter’s figures are historically situated and are not current demographic estimates.
17. Q: Which legal and administrative mechanisms does the author identify as producing unequal rights?
A: Show answer
The conclusion refers to laws and policies affecting access to land, residence in certain communities, property ownership, refugee return, and access to benefits. Khalidi argues that these measures transformed a proclaimed principle of equality into a system in which rights and opportunities were distributed unequally. He specifically discusses restrictions on Arab access to land and residency, appropriation of private and collective property, and the prevention of most Palestinian refugees from returning while Jewish immigrants received citizenship rights. These examples show why inequality must be analysed through actual legal rules and administrative practices, not only through constitutional language or political slogans. A rigorous assessment should examine the wording, implementation, affected groups, remedies, and relevant judicial decisions for each measure.
Study instruction:
- Select one mechanism mentioned in the conclusion and trace how a legal rule or administrative practice can affect land, residence, property, or political rights.
Key point:
- The author links inequality to concrete laws and administrative arrangements.
- A rights analysis should examine implementation and effects, not declarations alone.
18. Q: Why does the author regard demographic equality across the Jordan River and the Mediterranean Sea as politically significant?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the combined Arab and Jewish populations in the territory make the question of equal rights impossible to avoid. In his account, a political system that seeks to preserve exclusive national privilege while governing populations of comparable size faces a fundamental legitimacy problem. The significance is not simply numerical. It concerns whether people living under the same overarching power can enjoy equal civil, political, personal, and national rights. The conclusion’s population comparison is tied to its publication-era context and should be treated as a claim made by the author at that time, not as a current census. The enduring analytical question is how a political settlement can protect equal rights for all affected communities.
Study instruction:
- Explain why population size alone does not determine rights, then connect the author’s demographic argument to citizenship, sovereignty, and equality.
Key point:
- The author uses demographic realities to challenge the sustainability of exclusive national privilege.
- Equal rights are a normative principle, not a reward dependent on population size.
19. Q: How does the conclusion examine the tension between the claim that Israel is both “Jewish and democratic” and the 2018 Nation-State Basic Law?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the tension became more explicit when the Knesset adopted the Basic Law on the Jewish Nation-State in July 2018. He describes the law as reserving national self-determination for the Jewish people, changing the status of Arabic, and identifying Jewish settlement as a national value. He contrasts these provisions with the equality language in the 1948 Declaration of Independence and with the idea of equal national rights for all citizens. The conclusion presents the law as evidence that, when the two principles are treated as conflicting, some political leaders openly prioritize the Jewish character of the state over equality. This is the author’s constitutional and political interpretation; careful study should distinguish the text of the law from commentary about its implications.
Study instruction:
- Compare the equality language cited from the 1948 declaration with the provisions of the 2018 Basic Law described in the conclusion.
Key point:
- The author sees the 2018 law as formalizing a hierarchy between national identity and equal national rights.
- Distinguish legal text, political statements, and the author’s interpretation of their effects.
20. Q: Why does the author argue that a durable settlement must guarantee equality of rights?
A: Show answer
Khalidi contends that a settlement built on unequal rights would preserve the central cause of the conflict rather than resolve it. He specifies equality across human, personal, civil, political, and national rights. This means that an agreement should not merely allocate territory or establish administrative institutions; it must also determine whether individuals and communities enjoy equal legal status, political voice, security, and national recognition. The conclusion presents equality as both a moral requirement and a practical condition for durability. If one people remains structurally subordinate, the author argues, resentment and resistance will continue and the arrangement will remain unstable. The proposal is a normative principle, not a complete institutional blueprint for how every right should be implemented.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed settlement, test whether it protects personal, civil, political, and national rights for all affected populations.
Key point:
- The author treats equality as a precondition for legitimacy and durability.
- Territorial arrangements alone cannot resolve a conflict rooted in unequal rights.
21. Q: How does the conclusion address the Israeli security argument used to justify unequal arrangements?
A: Show answer
Khalidi acknowledges that Jewish Israeli insecurity has roots in a real history of persecution and trauma. He argues, however, that a response built around permanent domination and aggressive nationalism turns historical fear into a rationale for unequal treatment of Palestinians. The author suggests that this attachment to inequality will be difficult to change because it has been reinforced across generations and presented as a security necessity. He therefore places responsibility on Israelis and their supporters who recognize the harms of the current course to challenge the historical and ideological assumptions that sustain it. A careful reading should distinguish genuine security concerns from policies that restrict another population’s rights, and should ask whether a proposed measure is necessary, proportionate, and compatible with equal rights.
Study instruction:
- Separate a legitimate security objective from the specific policy used to pursue it; evaluate necessity, proportionality, discrimination, and long-term effects.
Key point:
- The chapter recognizes historical insecurity but rejects it as a blanket justification for inequality.
- Security and equal rights must be assessed together rather than treated as mutually exclusive.
22. Q: Why does the author challenge the idea that Israeli Jews are not a “real” people with national rights?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Palestinians must recognize that Israeli Jews today possess a developed sense of national belonging, even though the author traces modern Zionism to a transformation of Jewish religious and historical identity into a modern nationalism. Denying the national existence of Israeli Jews, he contends, mirrors the denial of Palestinian peoplehood that has characterized the conflict. The chapter does not claim that the two national movements have identical historical origins or claims. It stresses that both communities now exist as national groups in the same land and that denying either group’s existence makes a just settlement impossible. Recognition of national existence is therefore distinguished from endorsing every territorial claim or political practice associated with a national movement.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish recognition of a community’s present national identity from agreement with its historical narratives, territorial claims, or government policies.
Key point:
- The author argues that mutual recognition must include the national existence of both peoples.
- Recognition does not require accepting every claim made in the name of a nation.
23. Q: What does the conclusion say about the constructed nature of modern national identities?
A: Show answer
Drawing on Ernest Gellner and other scholarship on nationalism, Khalidi argues that nations are not simply timeless, natural, or divinely fixed political units. National movements may build on existing cultures, transform older identities, or create new forms of collective belonging. He applies this insight to both Palestinian and Israeli Jewish national identities. The author also insists that the constructed nature of national identity does not make a people unreal or remove its rights. Instead, it challenges claims that one nation’s identity is uniquely authentic while another’s is inherently illegitimate. The concept helps separate historical explanation from political recognition: identities have histories and can be studied critically, while the people who hold them still require equal rights and protection.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a national movement can draw on older cultural material while producing a modern political identity; avoid equating “constructed” with “false.”
Key point:
- National identities develop historically rather than existing as fixed natural categories.
- The constructed character of national identity does not invalidate the rights of people who share it.
24. Q: How does the author distinguish Palestinian indigeneity from the more recent settlement of many Israeli Jews?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that most Palestinians descend from people who have lived in the country for many centuries, while many Israeli Jews arrived from Europe and Arab countries in more recent waves associated with the Zionist project. He uses this distinction to explain why the conflict has a colonial character and why the historical relationship to land matters. At the same time, he acknowledges that many Israeli Jews have lived there for generations and possess a deeply felt religious and national connection to the country. The conclusion therefore distinguishes historical indigeneity from present-day national existence. It does not treat the latter as a reason to expel or deny rights to people now living there; its proposed resolution is equality for both peoples.
Study instruction:
- Separate historical questions of indigeneity and settlement from present-day questions of citizenship, national identity, and equal rights.
Key point:
- The author regards the historical distinction between indigenous Palestinians and settlers as politically significant.
- Historical origins do not remove the need to protect the rights of both present-day communities.
25. Q: Why does the conclusion argue that mutual recognition is necessary for resolving the conflict?
A: Show answer
The author contends that the conflict cannot be resolved while either side denies the national existence of the other. Recognition is not presented as a substitute for addressing dispossession, unequal rights, occupation, or historical responsibility. Rather, it is a necessary starting point for a political arrangement in which neither people seeks to erase the other. Khalidi argues that mutual recognition must be grounded in complete equality, including national rights, while still acknowledging the unequal historical relationship between indigenous Palestinians and the settler movement. This is a demanding combination: it requires recognition of present national communities without pretending their histories or power positions are identical. The author regards any alternative based on extermination, expulsion, or permanent subordination as unsustainable and unacceptable.
Study instruction:
- Formulate a definition of mutual recognition that includes national existence, historical accountability, and equal rights without collapsing these into one issue.
Key point:
- Denial of either people’s national existence blocks a sustainable settlement.
- Recognition must be accompanied by equality and attention to historical asymmetry.
26. Q: What role does external support play in maintaining the unequal status quo, according to the author?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the existing arrangement has been sustained not only by internal political forces but also by military, diplomatic, economic, and political backing from powerful states. He suggests that reducing this sustained external support for discriminatory arrangements would make a transition toward equality easier. This does not mean that external actors alone determine the conflict or that changing their policy would automatically produce peace. It means that international assistance can alter the incentives and capabilities of the parties, and can either reinforce or challenge the existing distribution of power. The conclusion therefore treats foreign policy, arms and diplomatic backing, public opinion, and international legal pressure as relevant parts of any strategy for change.
Study instruction:
- Identify forms of external support and assess whether each reinforces the status quo, encourages negotiation, or supports equal rights.
Key point:
- External sponsorship can help preserve unequal political arrangements.
- Changing international incentives is one component of a broader political strategy.
27. Q: How does the conclusion characterize the Trump administration’s “deal of the century” approach?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the initiative as a plan developed in close alignment with the Israeli government and its allies, rather than as a neutral mediation process. He highlights the administration’s appointments, statements about occupation and settlements, recognition of Jerusalem as Israel’s capital, and support for an “outside-in” regional approach involving Gulf monarchies. In the author’s account, these measures were designed to pressure Palestinians to accept an arrangement that left Israel with decisive control. The conclusion is critical of both the substance of the proposal and the process by which it was advanced. Because the book was written around 2019, its account should be understood as an analysis of that administration’s policy at that time, not a description of later policy developments.
Study instruction:
- Identify the proposal’s main political assumptions, the actors involved, and the decisions the author regards as prejudging the outcome.
Key point:
- The author interprets the initiative as aligned with Israeli government preferences rather than neutral brokerage.
- Keep the analysis anchored to the historical period described in the book.
28. Q: What is the difference between “economic peace” and a political settlement based on sovereignty and rights?
A: Show answer
In the conclusion, economic development offers are criticized when they are presented as compensation for the absence of political freedom, territorial control, or equal rights. Khalidi argues that investment and improved living standards cannot substitute for sovereignty or resolve the restrictions imposed by occupation. He describes the 2019 Bahrain economic initiative as an attempt to offer material benefits while preserving the underlying political conditions of Israeli control. The analytical distinction is between improving welfare within an existing power structure and changing the structure itself. Economic development can be valuable, but it cannot by itself settle questions of self-determination, refugees, Jerusalem, borders, security, or equal citizenship. A proposal should therefore be assessed for both economic outcomes and political rights.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a peace proposal by separating economic benefits from questions of sovereignty, territorial control, legal status, and political participation.
Key point:
- Economic improvement is not equivalent to self-determination or equal rights.
- Development proposals should be assessed alongside the political conditions they leave unchanged.
29. Q: Why does the conclusion treat the recognition of Jerusalem and the Golan Heights as consequential policy shifts?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the Trump administration’s recognition of Jerusalem as Israel’s capital and its recognition of Israeli sovereignty over the Golan Heights departed from earlier United States positions and affected the diplomatic framework. He emphasizes that Jerusalem’s status had been treated internationally as an issue to be resolved through negotiations, and that the unilateral recognition effectively removed a central Palestinian claim from the negotiating agenda. The author also argues that recognition of annexation could encourage further territorial annexation. The significance is therefore procedural as well as substantive: when a powerful actor decides a disputed issue in advance, the parties do not negotiate from an open position. The conclusion’s assessment should be read in its 2017–2019 policy context.
Study instruction:
- Explain how unilateral recognition of a disputed territorial claim can alter bargaining power and the scope of later negotiations.
Key point:
- The author sees the decisions as prejudging issues that had been reserved for negotiation.
- Diplomatic process and the substance of territorial claims are closely connected.
30. Q: How does the conclusion connect the refugee issue and UNRWA to the broader political dispute?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that attempts to deny refugee status to descendants of Palestinians displaced in 1948, together with efforts to weaken or eliminate UNRWA, would remove important elements of the Palestinian claim from the political agenda. In the conclusion, these measures are presented as part of a wider effort to narrow the issues that could be negotiated and to reduce international recognition of Palestinian displacement. The refugee question includes return, compensation, legal status, and the historical consequences of dispossession. It cannot be resolved merely by relabelling the population or ending the work of an agency. A rigorous assessment should distinguish the legal and humanitarian functions of refugee institutions from the political arguments surrounding a final settlement.
Study instruction:
- List the distinct dimensions of the refugee issue—status, assistance, return, compensation, and political recognition—and explain why they should not be conflated.
Key point:
- The conclusion treats refugee status and UNRWA as integral to the unresolved political questions.
- Humanitarian assistance and a final political resolution are related but not interchangeable.
31. Q: Why does the author reject the idea that the United States can act as a neutral mediator?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that United States policy has been too closely aligned with Israeli government positions for Washington to be treated as an impartial broker. He criticizes the Palestinian leadership’s reliance on United States-mediated negotiations, contending that the mediator’s commitments and policy choices shape the bargaining framework. The conclusion does not say that the United States could have no role in talks; it argues that its role should be understood realistically rather than assumed to be neutral. The author proposes broader international sponsorship and a negotiation structure that recognizes the United States’ interests and alignment. This is a strategic recommendation from the book, and it should be distinguished from the separate question of whether other mediators would be more effective or impartial.
Study instruction:
- Assess a mediator by examining its declared commitments, material relationships, previous decisions, and ability to apply pressure to all parties.
Key point:
- The author challenges the assumption of United States neutrality.
- Mediation design should account for the interests and influence of every participating power.
32. Q: How does the conclusion evaluate the strategies pursued by Fatah and Hamas?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that neither reliance on negotiations under the ‘Abbas-era diplomatic approach nor a nominal strategy of armed resistance has advanced Palestinian national objectives over the preceding decades. He points to the continued expansion of Israeli control as evidence that the existing strategies have not achieved their stated aims. The conclusion also criticizes the political division between Fatah and Hamas and the absence of a coherent national direction. This assessment is not a claim that all actions by either movement were identical or that the movements have no social base. It is a strategic judgment about outcomes and the need to reassess methods. The author calls for a new consensus on goals before attempting to rebuild unity.
Study instruction:
